Almost sure and moment convergence for triangular Pólya urns Thanks: Supported by the Knut and Alice Wallenberg Foundation and the Swedish Research Council (VR)
Svante Janson

Abstract
. We consider triangular Pólya urns and show under very weak conditions a general strong limit theorem of the form X n ​ i / a n ​ i ​ ⟶ a . s . ​ 𝒳 i X_{ni}/a_{ni}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{X}_{i} , where X n ​ i X_{ni} is the number of balls of colour i i after n n draws; the constants a n ​ i a_{ni} are explicit and of the form n α ​ log γ ​ n n^{\alpha}\log^{\gamma}n ; the limit is a.s. positive, and may be either deterministic or random, but is in general unknown. The result extends to urns with subtractions under weak conditions, but a counterexample shows that some conditions are needed. For balanced urns we also prove moment convergence in the main results if the replacements have the corresponding moments. The proofs are based on studying the corresponding continuous-time urn using martingale methods, and showing corresponding results there. In the main part of the paper, we assume for convenience that all replacements have finite second moments; in an appendix this is relaxed to L p L^{p} for some p > 1 p>1 .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60F15, 60F25; 60C05, 60G44, 60J85

1.
Introduction

A (generalized) Pólya urn contains balls of different colours.
A ball is drawn at random from the urn, and is replaced by a set of balls
that depends on the colour of the drawn balls; more generally, the replacement
set may be random, with a distribution depending on the drawn colour.
We assume that the set
\mathsf{Q}
of colours is finite, and let
q:=|\mathsf{Q}|
be the
number of colours.
(See e.g.
[
31
]
,
[
38
]
,

[
27
]
for the history and further references.)
It is often convenient to
assume that
\mathsf{Q}=\{1,\dots,q\}
,
but for us it will be convenient not to assume this.

The Pólya urn process can be defined formally as follows.
The composition of the urn at time
n
is given by the vector

\mathbf{X}_{n}=(X_{ni})_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}\in[0,\infty)^{q}
,
where
X_{ni}
is the number of balls of colour
i
.
The urn starts with a given vector
\mathbf{X}_{0}
,
and evolves according to a discrete-time Markov process.
Each colour
i
has an
activity

a_{i}\geqslant 0
,
and a (generally random)

replacement vector

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}=(\xi_{ij})_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}
.
At each time
n+1\geqslant 1
,
the urn is updated by drawing
one ball at random from the urn, with the probability of any ball
proportional to its activity.
(In many cases
a_{i}=1
for all
i
,
so all balls are drawn with equal probability;
the reader may concentrate on this case
until Section
5
.)
Thus, the drawn ball has colour
i

with probability

\frac{a_{i}X_{ni}}{\sum_{j}a_{j}X_{nj}}.

(1.1)

If the drawn ball has colour
i
, it is replaced
together with
\xi^{(n)}_{ij}
balls of colour
j
,
j\in\mathsf{Q}
,
where the random vector

\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}_{i}=(\xi^{(n)}_{ij})_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}

is a copy of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
that is
independent of everything else that has happened so far.
Thus, the urn is updated to

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{n+1}=\mathbf{X}_{n}+\boldsymbol{\xi}^{(n)}_{i}.

(1.2)

Remark 1.1
.

Note that, as in many other papers on Pólya urns, we do not assume that

X_{ni}
are integers; any real numbers
X_{ni}\geqslant 0
are allowed.
In general, it is thus a misnomer to call
X_{ni}
the “number” of balls
of colour

i
; it is more precise to regard
X_{ni}
as the amount of colour
i
in
the urn. Nevertheless, we will continue to use the traditional terminology,
which thus has to be interpreted liberally by the reader.

\triangle

Remark 1.2
.

Since the drawn ball is replaced, it is also really a misnomer to call

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
the “replacement” vector; it is really an
addition
vector.
(The replacements are really
\xi_{ij}+\delta_{ij}
.)
Nevertheless, we use the terminology above, which is used in many papers.

\triangle

Remark 1.3
.

We allow the replacement vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
to be random.
(Some papers consider only the special case of deterministic
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
,
which is an important special case that appears in many applications.)
We may say that the urn has

deterministic (or non-random) replacements
if all
\xi_{ij}

are deterministic, and otherwise

random replacements
.
These terms should thus be interpreted as conditioned on (the colour of) the
drawn ball.

\triangle

Remark 1.4
.

It is often convenient to describe the replacements by the

replacement matrix

(\xi_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
.
Note, however, that unless the replacements are deterministic, this may be
somewhat misleading, since the rows should be regarded as separate random
vectors, not necessarily defined on the same probability space; there is
no need for a joint distribution of different rows.

\triangle

Remark 1.5
.

In the first part of the paper we assume that the replacements
\xi_{ij}\geqslant 0

(Condition
(A5)
below), meaning that we only add balls to the urn and
never remove any. In Section
8
, and also usually in later sections, we more
generally allow also that balls may be removed from the urn
(assuming some hypotheses).

\triangle

Remark 1.6
.

We allow some activities
a_{i}
to be 0; this means that balls of colour

i
never are drawn.
See Section
5
for an important example of this.
(If all
a_{i}>0
, we may reduce to the standard case

a_{i}=1
by considering the urn
(a_{i}X_{ni})_{i}
, with corresponding replacement
vectors
(a_{j}\xi_{ij})_{j}
, but we will not use this.)

\triangle

Remark 1.7
.

We assumed tacitly above that the denominator

\sum_{j}a_{j}X_{nj}

in (
1.1
)
is
>0
for every
n\geqslant 0
, so that the definition makes sense.
This holds, for example, under assumptions
(A1)
and
(A5)
below.
(Urns that do not satisfy this, and therefore may stop at some finite time,
have also been studied, but they will not be considered here.)

\triangle

We are interested in asymptotic properties of
\mathbf{X}_{n}
as
{n\to\infty}
.

In the present paper, we study
triangular urns
, i.e., Pólya urns
such that, for a suitable labelling of the colours by
1,\dots,q
, we have

\xi_{ij}=0
when
i>j
. (See also Section
2.3
.)
This includes the original Pólya urns studied by
Markov 1917
,

Eggenberger and Pólya 1923
, and
Pólya 1930
(all for
q=2
), where the replacement
matrix is

diagonal
:

\xi_{ij}=0
when
i\neq j
, but also many other interesting cases.
See Section
14
for some examples.

There are many previous papers on triangular urns; we mention here
only a few that are particularly relevant to the present paper;
see also the references in the examples in Section
14
.

Athreya 1969
studied diagonal urns with random replacements
and showed a.s. convergence of the proportions of different colours,
using the embedding mthod of
Athreya and Karlin 1968
that is also the basis of
the present paper.

Gouet 1989
;
Gouet 1993
proved (in particular)
an a.s. convergence result
for triangular urns with 2 colours and deterministic replacements,
assuming also that the urn is
balanced
, meaning
\sum_{j}\xi_{ij}=b
for some constant
b

(and all
a_{i}=1
, see further Section
10
).

Janson 2005
studied triangular urns with 2 colours and deterministic

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
, and proved convergence in distribution (but not a.s.)
of the components
X_{ni}

after suitable normalizations; there are several cases, and the limits are
sometimes normal and sometimes not.
This was partially extended
by
Aguech 2009
, who also studied triangular urns with 2 colours,
but allowed random replacements
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}

(under some hypotheses, see Example
14.4
); moreover, he proved
convergence a.s., and not just in distribution.

Bose, Dasgupta, and Maulik 2009

(and
[
11
]
for
q=2
)
studied triangular urns with an arbitrary (finite) number of
colours;
they assumed that the replacements are deterministic, and that
the urn is balanced, and then, under some further assumptions,
showed convergence a.s. of the components
X_{ni}
,
suitably normalized, see Example
14.12
.

The main purpose of the present paper is to extend these results by

Gouet 1989
;
Gouet 1993
,

Aguech 2009
, and

Bose, Dasgupta, and Maulik 2009
, and show a.s. convergence for triangular urns with any
(finite) number of colours, allowing replacements
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
that are both
random and unbalanced.
Our main result is the following, using the technical assumptions
(A1)
–
(A5)

in Section
2.1
and the notation defined in (
2.7
)–(
2.13
)
in Section
2.4
below. See also the extensions Theorems
8.4

and
B.1
where the technical assumptions are weakened
(allowing urns with subtractions and reducing our moment assumption,
respectively); since the proof of the theorem is rather long and
we want to focus on the main ideas, we use first
the conditions
(A1)
–
(A5)
(which suffice for many applications), and
add later the extra arguments needed for the extensions.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
(X_{ni})_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a triangular Pólya urn satisfying the
conditions
(A1)
–
(A5)
below.
Then, for every colour
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, there exists a random variable
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}

with
0<\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}<\infty
a.s. such that as
{n\to\infty}
:

(i)

If
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}\log^{\gamma_{i}}n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}.

(1.3)

(ii)

If
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\kappa_{i}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}.

(1.4)

Note that the exponent
\gamma_{i}
may be both positive and negative;
see the examples in Section
14
.
Note also that the various exponents in (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) are
explicitly given in Section
2.4
, but the limiting random variables
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}

are known only in some special cases; in general they are unfortunately unknown.

The virtue of Theorem
1.8
is that it is very general,
but as discussed in the remaks below, more precise results are known in some special cases.

In Theorem
1.8
, the main case is

(i)
,
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, and the reader should focus on this case.
The case
\widehat{\lambda}=0
is more special and of less interest for applications, but
it is included for completeness; by (
2.10
) and (
2.7
), this case
occurs when
\xi_{ii}=0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
. (We might call such urns

strictly triangular
.)

Another major result in the present paper (Theorem
12.5
)
says in particular that in the special case of balanced triangular urns,
if the replacements have finite moments, then
the a.s. limits in Theorem
1.8
hold also in

L^{p}
for any
p<\infty
, and thus moments converge.
(It is an open problem whether this extends to some class of unbalanced
triangular urns.)

Remark 1.9
.

Theorem
1.8
describes the first-order asymptotics of the urn.
We will see in Section
7
that
the limiting random variable
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is deterministic (i.e., a constant) in
some cases, but not in general.
In cases where Theorem
1.8
yields a limit
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
that
is deterministic (and perhaps also otherwise),
it is interesting to study fluctuations
(i.e., second order terms) and try to find limits
(e.g. in distribution, after a suitable normalization)
for the difference of the two sides of (
1.3
) or (
1.4
).
Such results in some cases are given in
[
19
]
,

[
28
]
and
[
1
]
;
see the examples in Section
14
, but we will not pursue this problem here,
and leave it as an open problem.

\triangle

Remark 1.10
.

Convergence almost surely implies convergence in distribution. Thus, as a
corollary, (
1.3
) holds also with convergence in distribution.
However, our proof does not seem to
provide a method to find the limit distribution,
i.e. the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
, except in some very simple cases.
Moreover, the limits
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
are (in general) dependent.

For
q=2
and deterministic
\xi_{ij}
, limit distributions were
given in
[
28
]
. (Sometimes degenerate, sometimes not.) The
results there thus describe the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
in this case,
although the descriptions in some cases are complicated.
Some further examples of known limit distributions are given in some examples
in Section
14
.
We leave the general case as another open problem.

\triangle

The proof of Theorem
1.8
is given in Sections
3
–
5

below, after some preliminaries in Section
2
.
The proof is based on the embedding by
Athreya and Karlin 1968

of a Pólya urn into a continuous-time multitype
branching process (Section
2.6
);
we then apply martingale methods to obtain a continuous-time version of
Theorem
1.8
(Theorem
4.1
); finally, this implies results for the embedded
discrete-time urn.
The proof is generalized to urns with subtraction in Section
8
, and to urns
with a weaker moment condition in Appendix
B
.
Since the proofs are rather long and technical, we prefer to first present
the proof in the basic case Theorem
1.8
(which is enough for most applications)
and later discuss the modifications required for the extensions,
instead of proving the most general results immediately.

Remark 1.11
.

Gouet 1989
;
Gouet 1993
and

Bose, Dasgupta, and Maulik 2009
, in special cases (see Example
14.3
and Example
14.12
),
instead study
X_{ni}
directly
and use martingale metods in discrete time. It seems that this approach
(also used by several authors for non-triangular urns) works well for
balanced urns, but that the embedding into continuous time works better for
unbalanced urns.

\triangle

Remark 1.12
.

We consider in this paper only triangular Pólya urns.
Another important class of urns consists of the irreducible urns.
In this case a.s. convergence (under some technical conditions)
was shown by

Athreya and Karlin 1968
, see also

[
4
, Section V.9.3]
and

[
27
, Theorem 3.21]
.

It might be possible to combine the methods of the present paper and the
methods for irreducible urns to obtain results on a.s. convergence for all
types of Pólya urns (under some technical conditions),
see Remark
2.3
,
but the present
paper is long as it is and we leave this as a speculation for future
research. (Note also the counterexamples Example
14.14
and
14.15
, showing
that some conditions are needed even in the triangular case.)

\triangle

1.1.
Contents

Section
2
contains preliminaries, including some definitions and notation.
Section
3
consists of a series of lemmas that comprise the main technical
part of our proofs. They lead to the main theorem for continuous time in
Section
4
, which in turn is used to prove Theorem
1.8
in Section
5
.
Sections
6
(continuous time) and
7
(discrete time) contain results
on whether the limit random variable are degenerate (i.e., constant) or not,
and some related results.

Section
8
extend the previous results to urns with subtracion, where we
allow
\xi_{ii}=-1
. With some extra technical conditions, the previous
results hold in this case too, with only minor modifications of the proofs.

The following sections contain some complements.
Section
9
is a short section comparing random and non-random replacements
with the same means.

Section
10
contains some general results on balanced urn,
mainly as preliminaries to the following sections.

Section
11
considers the number of times a given colour is drawn;
it is shown that the results of earlier sections extend to this case.

Section
12
contains results on convergence in
L^{2}
and in

L^{p}
, and closely related results on convergence of moments
in, for example, Theorem
1.8
.
For the main result (discrete-time), we have to assume that urn is balanced,
and we state an open problem for more general urns.

Section
13
discusses briefly another open problem (rates of convergence).

Section
14
contains a number of examples that illustrate the results and
their limitations, and also give connections to previous literature.

Finally, Appendix
A
contains some simple general lemmas on absolute continuity
that we believe are known, but for which we were unable to find references.
Appendix
B
gives proofs of
L^{p}
versions of
L^{2}
estimates used in the
main part of the paper; this yields both the extension of Theorem
1.8

mentioned above, and a proof of the results in Section
12
.
Appendix
C
gives a rather technical proof of one claim in Example
14.14
.

2.
Some notation and other preliminaries

We use throughout the paper the notation
\mathsf{Q}
,
q
,

\mathbf{X}_{n}=(X_{ni})_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
,
a_{i}
, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}=(\xi_{ij})_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}

introduced in the introduction.

2.1.
Standing assumptions

In the rest of the paper we assume

(A0)

The Pólya urn is triangular.

(Unless we explicitly say so, for example when we discuss this property in
Section
2.3
.)
For the central part of the paper (Sections
2
–
7
)
we make also some standing technical assumptions:

(A1)

The initial urn
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is non-random.
Moreover, each
X_{0i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum a_{i}X_{0i}>0
.
(The results may be extended to random
\mathbf{X}_{0}
by conditioning on
\mathbf{X}_{0}
.)

(A2)

If
a_{i}=0
, then
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}=0
, i.e.,
\xi_{ij}=0
for every
j\in\mathsf{Q}
.
(This is without loss of generality, since
a_{i}=0
means that balls of
colour
i
never are drawn, and thus
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
does not matter.)

(A3)

For every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, either
X_{0i}>0
, or there exists
j\neq i
such that

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ji}>0)>0
(or both).
(This too is without loss of generality, since otherwise balls of colour
i

can never appear, so
X_{ni}=0
a.s. for all
n
, and we may remove the
colour
i
from
\mathsf{Q}
.)

(A4)

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}^{2}<\infty
for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
.

(A5)

\xi_{ij}\geqslant 0
(a.s.) for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
.

Note that
(A5)
implies that every
X_{ni}
is (weakly) increasing in

n
. In particular, the urn never gets empty. Combined with
(A1)
we see
that
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{ni}>0
for every
n
, and thus the
probabilities (
1.1
) and the urn process are well
defined.
We discuss extensions to urns not satisfying
(A5)
(i.e., urns with
subtractions) in Section
8
; see Theorems
8.4
–
8.6
.

Remark 2.1
.

The second moment condition
(A4)
is for technical convenience.
In fact, the results (including Theorem
1.8
) hold
assuming only
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}^{p}<\infty
for some
p>1
.
However, this adds further arguments to an already long proof, so
we assume second moments in the main part of the paper and show the
extension to
p>1
in Appendix
B
.

It seems possible that the results could extend further by
assuming only that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}\log\xi_{ij}<\infty
,
as for diagonal urns in
[
2
]

using related results for (single-type) branching processes

[
4
, Theorem III.7.2]
.
(The results do not extend without modifications to cases
without this assumption, see Example
14.16
.)
We have not pursued this, and leave it as an open problem.

\triangle

2.2.
General notation

As usual, we ignore events of probability 0. We often write “almost
surely” or “a.s.” for emphasis, but we may also tacitly omit this.

We use “increasing” in the weak sense. Similarly, “positive function”
means in
the weak sense, i.e.
\geqslant 0
.
(However, “positive constant” always means strictly positive; we sometimes
add “strictly” for emphasis, but not always.)

We let
s\land t:=\min\{s,t\}

and
s\lor t:=\max\{s,t\}
.

We let
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}:=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{+}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
.

We use
\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}
,
\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\longrightarrow}
, and
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}
to denote
convergence almost surely, in probability, and in distribution, respectively.

“Absolutely continuous”
(for a probability distribution in
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
means with respect to Lebesgue measure.
(Except in Appendix
A
when a reference measure is explicitly specified.)
We may say that a random variable is absolutely continuous when its distribution is.

We use some standard probability distributions:

\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)
is an exponential distribution with mean
\lambda>0
;
we may also say with
rate

1/\lambda
.

\Gamma(\alpha,b)
is a Gamma distribution. (Thus
\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)=\Gamma(1,\lambda)
.)

\operatorname{Be}(p)
is a Bernoulli distribution.

\operatorname{NegBin}(r,p)
is a negative binomial distribution.
\operatorname{Ge}(p)
is a geometric distribution on
\{1,2,\dots\}
.

For a random variable
W
, we let

{\mathcal{L}}(W)
denotes the distribution of
W
, and, for any
p>0
,

\displaystyle\lVert W\rVert_{p}:=\bigl(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}|W|^{p}\bigr)^{1/p}.

(2.1)

L^{p}
denotes the set of all random variables
W
such that
\lVert W\rVert_{p}<\infty
.

C
denotes unspecified constants that may vary from one occurrence to the
next.
They may depend on
the activities
a_{i}
and the distributions of the replacements

\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
, and perhaps on other parameters clear from the context,
but they never depend on
n
or
t
.

Let

\displaystyle r_{ij}:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}.

(2.2)

Thus the matrix
(r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
is the mean replacement matrix.
Note that
r_{ij}
exists and is finite by
(A4)
.
By
(A5)
, we have
r_{ij}\geqslant 0
, and
r_{ij}=0\iff\xi_{ij}=0
a.s.

We occasionally
(when we discuss two different urns at the same time)
denote a Pólya urn by
{\mathcal{U}}
;
we then (somewhat informally) mean both the
urn process
\mathbf{X}_{n}
and its continuous-time version
\mathbf{X}(t)
defined below,
and also the
colour set
\mathsf{Q}
and the replacement matrix
(\xi_{ij})
.

2.3.
The colour graph

(In this subsection, the urn does not have to be triangular.)
Recall that
\mathsf{Q}
is the set of colours. As said in the introduction, we
allow
\mathsf{Q}
to be any finite set, although it is possible to assume

\mathsf{Q}=\{1,\dots,q\}
without loss of generality when this is convenient.

We regard the set
\mathsf{Q}
of colours as a directed graph,
called the
colour graph
,
where for any distinct
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
there is an edge
i\to j

if and only if
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ij}\neq 0)>0
.
In other words,
i\to j
means that if a ball of colour
i
is drawn, it is
possible (with positive probability) that some balls of colour
j
are
added. Note that by
(A2)
,
i\to j
entails
a_{i}>0
, so balls of
colour
i
may (and will) actually be drawn;
furthermore,
by
(A5)
,

\displaystyle i\to j\iff r_{ij}>0\text{ and }i\neq j.

(2.3)

We say, again for two distinct colours
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
, that
i
is
an
ancestor
of
j
, and
j
a
descendant
of
i
,
if there exists a directed path in
\mathsf{Q}
from
i
to
j
; we denote this by

i\prec j
.
In other words,
i\prec j
means that if we start the urn with only a ball
of colour
i
, then it is possible that balls of colour
j
are added at
some later time.

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, let
\mathsf{P}_{i}:=\{j\in\mathsf{Q}:j\to i\}
, the set of colours
different from
i
whose drawings may cause addition of balls of colour
i
.
We say that the colour
i
is
minimal

if
\mathsf{P}_{i}=\emptyset
.
We denote the set of minimal colours by
\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
.
Note that
(A3)
can be formulated as:
X_{0i}>0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
.

We say that the urn is
triangular
, if there exists a
(re)labelling of the colours by
1,\dots,q
that makes the matrix

(\xi_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
triangular a.s.
(Assuming
(A5)
, this is equivalent to
the mean replacement matrix
(r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
being triangular, cf.
(
2.3
).)
In other words, the urn is triangular if there exists a total ordering
<

of the colours such that

\displaystyle i>j\implies\xi_{ij}=0\quad\text{a.s.}

(2.4)

Using the definitions above to rewrite (
2.4
),
we see that the urn is triangular if and only there
exists a total ordering
<
such that, for
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
,

\displaystyle i\to j\implies i<j.

(2.5)

Furthermore, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle i\prec j\implies i<j.

(2.6)

Proposition 2.2
.

The following are equivalent.

(i)

The urn is triangular.

(ii)

The colour graph is acyclic.

(iii)

The relation
\prec
on
\mathsf{Q}
is a partial order.

Proof.

(i)
implies
(ii)
and
(iii)

as a consequence of (
2.5
) and (
2.6
).

(ii)
\iff
(iii)
is easily seen.

Finally, any partial order can be extended to a total order.
Thus, if
(iii)
holds, we may extend
\prec
to a total order
<
, which
means that (
2.6
) holds.
∎

Note that if the urn is triangular,
a colour
i
is minimal if and only if it is minimal
in the partial order
\prec
.

Remark 2.3
.

We may also note that a Pólya urn is irreducible if and only if
its colour graph is strongly connected.
Given any Pólya urn, we may decompose its
colour graph into its strongly
connected components, which are linked by the remaining edges in an acyclic way.
Hence the urn can be regarded as an acyclic directed network of
irreducible urns. This suggests, as mentioned in Remark
1.12
,
that the methods in the present paper perhaps might be combined with methods for
irreducible urns to obtain results for general urns.

\triangle

2.4.
More notation

Let, for
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}:=a_{i}r_{ii}=a_{i}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0.

(2.7)

In the continuous-time version introduced below,

\lambda_{i}
is the rate of additions to colour

i
by drawings of the same colour.
Since colours also may be added by drawings of another colour, we define
further

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{i}:=\max\bigl\{\lambda_{j}:j\preceq i\bigr\}\geqslant 0.

(2.8)

In other words,
\lambda^{*}_{i}
is the largest
\lambda_{j}
for a colour
j
such that
there exists a path (possibly of length 0) in
\mathsf{Q}
from
j
to
i
.
Such a path may contain several colours
k
with the same, maximal,
\lambda_{k}
,
and we denote the largest number of them in a single path by
1+\kappa_{i}
; i.e.,

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}:=\max\bigl\{\kappa:\exists i_{1}\prec i_{2}\prec\dots\prec i_{\kappa+1}\preceq i\text{ with }\lambda_{i_{1}}=\dots=\lambda_{i_{\kappa+1}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}\bigr\}\geqslant 0.

(2.9)

Define further

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\max\{\lambda_{i}:i\in\mathsf{Q}\}\geqslant 0,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{\kappa}

\displaystyle:=\max\{\kappa_{i}:i\in\mathsf{Q}\text{ and }\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}\}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\max\bigl\{\kappa:\exists i_{1}\prec i_{2}\prec\dots\prec i_{\kappa+1}\text{ with }\lambda_{i_{1}}=\dots=\lambda_{i_{\kappa+1}}=\widehat{\lambda}\bigr\}\geqslant 0.

(2.11)

If
\widehat{\lambda}=0
(i.e., if
\lambda_{i}=0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
), let further

\displaystyle\widehat{\kappa}_{0}

\displaystyle:=1+\max\bigl\{\kappa_{i}:i\in\mathsf{Q}\text{ with }a_{i}>0\bigr\}\geqslant 1.

(2.12)

If
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, define also

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}:=\kappa_{i}-\widehat{\kappa}\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda},\qquad i\in\mathsf{Q}.

(2.13)

2.5.
Stochastic processes

All our continuous-time stochastic processes are defined on
[0,\infty)
and are
assumed to be càdlàg (right-continuous with left limits).

We consider martingales without explicitly specifying the filtration; this
will always be the natural filtration

(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{t}
is
generated by “everything that has happened up to time
t
”.
If
T
is a stopping time, then
\mathcal{F}_{T}
denotes the corresponding
\sigma
-field
generated by all events up to time
T
.

Given a stochastic process
W=(W(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
, we define its maximal process by

\displaystyle W^{*}(t):=\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}|W(s)|,\qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\infty.

(2.14)

(We consider only
s<\infty
, also when
t=\infty
.)
We further define

\displaystyle\Delta W(t):=W(t)-W(t-),\qquad 0\leqslant t<\infty,

(2.15)

where
W(t-)
is the left limit at
t
, with
W(0-):=0
.

If
W
is a process with locally bounded variation, we may define its

quadratic variation

by

\displaystyle[W,W]_{t}:=\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}\bigl\lvert\Delta W(s)\bigr\rvert^{2},\qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant\infty,

(2.16)

(summing over
s<\infty
if
t=\infty
),
noting that the sum always really is countable.
(We have no need for the definition for general semimartingales, see e.g.

[
32
, p. 519 and Theorem 26.6]

or
[
42
, Section II.6]
.)
Recall

[
42
, Corollary 3 to Theorem II.6.27, p. 73]

that if
M
is a local martingale and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}<\infty
for some
t<\infty
,
then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}|M(t)|^{2}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t},

(2.17)

and as a consequence, if
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{\infty}<\infty
, then

M
is an
L^{2}
-bounded martingale and thus
M(\infty):=\lim_{{t\to\infty}}M(t)
exists
a.s., and (
2.17
) holds for all
t\leqslant\infty
.

Recall also Doob’s inequality
[
32
, Proposition 7.16]

which as a special case yields, combined with (
2.17
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}M^{*}(t)^{2}\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}|M(t)|^{2}=C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t},

(2.18)

provided
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}<\infty
.
(Here
C=4
, but we will not use this.)

2.6.
Continuous-time urn

We will use the standard method of embedding the discrete-time urn in a
continuous-time process, due to
Athreya and Karlin 1968
,
see also
[
4
, §V.9]
,
and after them used in many papers.
We thus define the
continuous-time urn
as a vector valued Markov
process
\mathbf{X}(t)=((X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
with given initial value

\mathbf{X}(0):=\mathbf{X}_{0}
such
that, for each
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
“a ball of colour
i
is drawn”
with intensity
a_{i}X_{i}(t)
;
when a ball of colour
i
is drawn, we add to
\mathbf{X}(t)

a copy of
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
(independent of the history).
In the classical case (e.g.
[
3
]
) when each
X_{i}(t)
is
integer valued,
\mathbf{X}(t)
is a
multitype continuous-time Markov branching process;
in general (allowing any real
X_{i}(t)\geqslant 0
),

\mathbf{X}(t)
is a (vector-valued)
continuous-time
continuous-state branching process
(abbreviated
CB process
)
as defined by
Jiřina 1958
, see also e.g.
Li 2011
.
(The process
\mathbf{X}(t)
is of jump-type, as in
[
30
, Section 3]
.)
Note that
(A4)
implies
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}<\infty
for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
, which is
a well-known sufficient condition for non-explosions; i.e., there exists
such a process
\mathbf{X}(t)
with
\mathbf{X}(t)
finite for all
t\in[0,\infty)
.

Since
a_{i}X_{i}(0)>0
for some
i
by
(A1)
, and
X_{i}(t)
is
increasing by
(A5)
, there will a.s. be infinitely many draws in the urn.
We let
\widehat{T}_{n}
be the
n
th time that a ball is drawn, with
\widehat{T}_{0}:=0
.
Then the discrete-time urn
\mathbf{X}_{n}
in Section
1
can be realized as

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{n}:=\mathbf{X}(\widehat{T}_{n}).

(2.19)

We assume (
2.19
) throughout the paper.

Since
\mathbf{X}(t)
does not explode, there is a.s.
only a finite number of draws up to
any finite time
t
, and thus
\widehat{T}_{n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\infty
as
{n\to\infty}
.
Note that we denote the discrete-time urn by
\mathbf{X}_{n}=(X_{ni})_{i}
and
the continuous-time urn by
\mathbf{X}(t)=(X_{i}(t))_{i}
.

Of course, the continuous-time urn can also be studied for its own sake,
see for example
[
13
]
.

3.
Analysis of one colour

In this section, we study one fixed colour
i\in\mathsf{Q}
in the continuous-time urn.

Recall that
X_{i}(t)
is the number (amount) of balls of colour
i
at time
t

in the continuous-time urn.
There are several possible sources of these balls:
some may be there from the beginning, some may be added when a ball of some
other colour
j
is drawn (with
j\to i
and thus
j\prec i
), and in both
cases these balls of colour
i
may later be drawn and produce further
generations of balls of colour
i
(provided
\lambda_{i}>0
).

We begin by considering, in the following two subsections,
two simpler special cases. We will then in the final subsection, rather easily,
treat the general case
by combining these special cases.

We first state some simple general results; these are more or less known,
see e.g.
[
27
, Lemma 9.3]
for a related result,
but for convenience we give full proofs.

Fix a colour
j
.
Let
0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\dots
be the times that a ball of
colour
j
is drawn, and let
N(t):=\bigl\lvert\{k:T_{k}\leqslant t\}\bigr\rvert
denote the
corresponding counting process;
i.e.,
N(t)
is the number of draws of colour
j
up to time
t
.
These draws occur with intensity
a_{j}X_{j}(t)
, which means that

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}(t):=N(t)-a_{j}\int_{0}^{t}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geqslant 0,

(3.1)

is a local martingale. In fact,
\widetilde{N}(t)
is a martingale, which we verify by
the following simple lemma. Recall the notation (
2.14
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{j}(t)<\infty
for some
t\in[0,\infty)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t)

\displaystyle<\infty,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{N}^{*}(t)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sup_{s\leqslant t}|\widetilde{N}(s)|<\infty.

(3.3)

In particular, if
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{j}(t)<\infty
for every
t<\infty
,
then the local martingale
\widetilde{N}(t)
is a martingale.

Proof.

By the definition of local martingale, there exists an increasing sequence
of stopping times
\tau_{m}
,
m\geqslant 1
, such that
\tau_{m}\nearrow\infty
a.s.
as
{m\to\infty}
, and
\widetilde{N}(t\land\tau_{m})
is a martingale for each
m
.
In particular, since
\widetilde{N}(0)=0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t\land\tau_{m})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{N}(t\land\tau_{m})+a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{m}}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t\land\tau_{m}}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.4)

Since
N(t)
and
X_{j}(t)
are increasing positive
functions of
t
, we may use monotone convergence and let
{m\to\infty}
to obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t)=a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[tX_{j}(t)\bigr]<\infty.

(3.5)

The monotonicity of
N(t)
further implies

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}^{*}(t)\leqslant N(t)+a_{j}\int_{0}^{t}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant N(t)+a_{j}tX_{j}(t),

(3.6)

and thus (
3.3
) follows by (
3.5
).

The final statement follows since a local martingale with integrable maximal
function is a martingale.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{j}(t)<\infty
for some
t\in[0,\infty)
.

(i)

Let
f
be a positive or bounded measurable function on
[0,t]
.
Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}N(s)=a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.7)

(ii)

Let also
(\eta_{k})_{1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of identically distributed
random variables with finite mean
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\eta_{1}

such that
\eta_{k}
is independent of
T_{k}
.
Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})\eta_{k}=a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\eta_{1}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.8)

Proof.

Step 1
. The left and middle terms in (
3.7
) are the same, since

\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}N(s)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})
by the definition of
N
.

Step 2
. Suppose that
f(s)=\boldsymbol{1}_{\{a<s\leqslant b\}}
, the indicator function of an
interval
(a,b]\in[0,t]
.
Then, by (
3.1
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}N(s)-a_{j}\int_{0}^{t}f(s)X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}f(s)\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{N}(s)=\widetilde{N}(b)-\widetilde{N}(a),

(3.9)

and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[\widetilde{N}(b)-\widetilde{N}(a)]=0
by Lemma
3.1
; hence (
3.7
) holds for
such
f
.

Step 3
. The monotone class theorem
[
21
, Theorem 1.2.3]
now shows that (
3.7
)
holds for the indicator function
f(s)=\boldsymbol{1}_{\{s\in A\}}
of any Borel set

A\in[0,t]
.

Step 4
. By linearity, (
3.7
) holds for any positive simple function
f
.
Then, by monotone convergence, (
3.7
) holds for any
positive measurable function, and by linearity again for any bounded
measurable function.
This proves
(i)
.

Step 5
. In
(ii)
, we may decompose
\eta_{k}
into its positive and negative
parts; thus it suffices to consider
\eta_{k}\geqslant 0
. Then the sum in
(
3.8
)
is well-defined, and

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})\eta_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})\eta_{k}\bigr]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\eta_{1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}f(T_{k})\bigr],

(3.10)

and (
3.8
) follows from (
3.7
).
∎

3.1.
A colour not influenced by others

In this subsection, we assume that
\xi_{ji}=0
a.s.
for all colours
j\neq i
. Equivalently,
j\not\to i
for
j\in\mathsf{Q}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{P}_{i}=\emptyset
;
in other words,
i
is a minimal colour.
This means that
X_{i}(t)
is affected only by
draws of the same colour
i
, and we may thus ignore all other colours
and regard
X_{i}(t)
as a continuous-time urn with a single colour.
In other words,
X_{i}(t)
is a one-dimensional
CB process,
starting at some given
X_{i}(0)
and
adding copies of
\xi_{ii}
with intensity
a_{i}X_{i}(t)
.
We write
X_{i}(0)=x_{0}
.
Note that our assumption
(A3)
means
x_{0}>0
, but for completeness
we allow also the trivial case
x_{0}=0
in the present subsection.
(In this subsection, we really use only the assumptions

(A1)
,
(A4)
, and
(A5)
, and only for the colour
i
.)
Note also that
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
by (
2.8
).

We will only need a few simple facts about CB processes;
see further e.g.

[
30
;
36
;
20
;
9
;
37
]

where many more results are given.

Recall that
\lambda_{i}=a_{i}r_{ii}
. If
\lambda_{i}=0
, then there are no additions at
all, and
X_{i}(t)=X_{i}(0)=x_{0}
is constant.

It is easy to see that since
r_{ii}<\infty
, the CB
process
X_{i}(t)
is well defined and non-explosive (i.e., finite for all
t
),
for any given
x_{0}\geqslant 0
.
Moreover

[
20
]
,
[
9
, Proposition 2.2]
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{i}(t)=e^{\lambda_{i}t}x_{0}.

(3.11)

This implies by conditioning and the Markov property that

[
4
, Theorem III.7.1]

\displaystyle{e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)}\quad\text{is a martingale}.

(3.12)

The following result too well known, but we include a proof for
completeness, and since we discuss modifications of it later.
See e.g.

[
4
, Theorem V.8.2]
and
[
27
, Lemma 9.5]
for a vector-valued extension.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
\xi_{ji}=0
a.s.
for all
j\neq i
. Then

e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)
is an
L^{2}
-bounded martingale,
and thus

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}

(3.13)

for some random variable
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
.
Furthermore, with
x_{0}=X_{i}(0)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{0},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}

\displaystyle=x_{0}\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}^{2},

(3.15)

where we interpret
\frac{0}{0}
as
0
.
Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\bigl[e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{Var}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\leqslant Cx_{0},

(3.16)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\Bigl\lvert\sup_{t\geqslant 0}e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\Bigr\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}^{2}\leqslant C(x_{0}+x_{0}^{2}).

(3.17)

Furthermore, if
x_{0}>0
, then
0<{\mathcal{X}}_{i}<\infty
a.s.

Proof.

The case
\lambda_{i}=0
is trivial, with
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=x_{0}
.
The same holds if
x_{0}=0
.
We may thus assume
\lambda_{i}>0
and
x_{0}>0
.

We argue as in
[
27
, Section 9]
.
As above (now taking
j=i
),
let
0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\dots
be the times that a ball of colour
i
is
drawn, and
let
N(t):=\bigl\lvert\{i:T_{i}\leqslant t\}\bigr\rvert
denote the corresponding counting process-
Furthermore, let
\eta_{k}:=\Delta X_{i}(T_{k})
be the number of balls of colour
i

added at the
k
-th draw.
Thus
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots
is a sequence of independent copies of

\xi_{ii}
.

Let
M(t):=e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)
. Then
M
is a martingale by (
3.12
), and
its quadratic variation is by (
2.16
) given by

\displaystyle[M,M]_{t}=\sum_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}|\Delta M(s)|^{2}=x_{0}^{2}+\sum_{T_{k}\leqslant t}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}|\Delta X_{i}(T_{k})|^{2}=x_{0}^{2}+\sum_{T_{k}\leqslant t}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\eta_{k}^{2}.

(3.18)

Hence, it follows from Lemma
3.2
(ii)
that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}=x_{0}^{2}+a_{i}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[\xi_{ii}^{2}]\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda_{i}s}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{i}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.19)

Hence, writing
\beta:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}^{2}
, (
3.11
) yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}=x_{0}^{2}+a_{i}\beta x_{0}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}\,\mathrm{d}s=x_{0}^{2}+x_{0}\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}\beta\bigl(1-e^{-\lambda_{i}t}\bigr).

(3.20)

This shows by (
2.17
) that
M
is an
L^{2}
-bounded martingale; thus
(
3.13
) holds for some
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=M(\infty)
. Clearly
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\geqslant 0
.
We have
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}M(\infty)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}M_{0}=x_{0}
, which yields (
3.14
).
Furthermore, (
3.15
) holds by (
2.17
) and (
3.20
) again,
together with (
3.14
); this yields also (
3.16
).
Doob’s inequality (
2.18
) yields (
3.17
).

Finally, the distribution of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
depends on
x_{0}
; thus let us denote

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
by

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x_{0})
. The CB property implies that if
x,y\geqslant 0
, then

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x+y)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x)+{\mathcal{X}}_{i}^{\prime}(y)
, where
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}^{\prime}(y)
is a copy of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(y)

independent of

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x)
. Let
p(x):=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\bigl({\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x)=0\bigr)\in[0,1]
.
It follows that for any
x,y\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle p(x+y)=p(x)p(y),

(3.21)

and thus there exists
c\in[0,\infty]
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}(x)=0)=p(x)=e^{-cx},\qquad x>0.

(3.22)

We must have
c>0
, since otherwise
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
a.s. for any
x_{0}
, which
contradicts (
3.14
).
The Markov property and (
3.22
) yield, for any

t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\bigl({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0\mid X_{i}(t)\bigr)=e^{-cX_{i}(t)}.

(3.23)

Thus, taking the expectation,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\bigl({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0\bigr)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}e^{-cX_{i}(t)}.

(3.24)

It is clear that if
x_{0}>0
and

\lambda_{i}>0
, so
a_{i}>0
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}>0
, then there is a.s.
an infinite number of draws, and the (standard) law of large numbers shows
that
X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\infty
. Consequently, letting
{t\to\infty}
in (
3.23
) yields,
by dominated convergence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)=0.

(3.25)

∎

By (
3.15
), the limit
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is degenerate only in the trivial cases when

x_{0}=0
or
a_{i}=0
. We note that except in these cases,
the limit has an absolutely continuous distribution.

Lemma 3.4
.

In Lemma
3.3
, suppose further that
\lambda_{i}>0
and
x_{0}>0
.
Then the distribution of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is absolutely continuous.

Proof.

Let
T_{1}
be the first time that a ball of colour
i
is drawn; then

T_{1}\in\operatorname{Exp}(1/(a_{i}x_{0}))
. The distribution of the balls added at
T_{1}
is independent of
T_{1}
, and
thus
X_{i}(T_{1})
is independent of
T_{1}
.
It follows by the strong Markov property that the stochastic process

Y(t):=X_{i}(T_{1}+t)
,
t\geqslant 0
, is independent of
T_{1}
.
By (
3.13
), we have as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{i}t}Y(t)=e^{\lambda_{i}T_{1}}e^{-\lambda_{i}(T_{1}+t)}X_{i}(T_{1}+t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Y}}:=e^{\lambda_{i}T_{1}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i},

(3.26)

and it follows that
{\mathcal{Y}}
is independent of
T_{1}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{1}}{\mathcal{Y}},

(3.27)

where the two factors on the right-hand side are independent.
The result follows from (
3.27
) since

e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{1}}
has an absolutely continuous distribution
and

{\mathcal{Y}}>0
a.s. (because
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s. by Lemma
3.3
).
∎

3.2.
A colour only produced by one other colour

We continue to consider a fixed colour
i
.
In this subsection we assume that
|\mathsf{P}_{i}|=1
; thus
there is exactly one colour
j\in\mathsf{Q}
such that
j\to i
.
We assume also that
X_{i}(0)=0
, so there are initially no balls of colour
i
.
Recall that the assumption
j\to i
means
r_{ij}>0
, and thus implicitly

a_{j}>0
.

Since the colour graph is acyclic, there is no feedback from colour
i
to
colour
j
; thus we may regard the entire
process
X_{j}(t)
,
t\in[0,\infty)
, as known, and consider only its
effect on
X_{i}(t)
.

We may regard
X_{i}(t)
as a CB process with immigration.
Let again
0<T_{1}<T_{2}<\dots
be the times that a ball of colour
j
is
drawn, and
let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots
be the corresponding number (amount) of balls of
colour
i
that are added.
In case there is only a finite number
K
of times that a ball of colour
j
is
drawn, we define for completeness
T_{k}=\infty
for
k>K
, and pick
\eta_{k}

with the correct distribution and independent of everything else.
We regard each
\eta_{k}
as an immigrant coming at
T_{k}
.
(Some
\eta_{k}
may be 0; this is no problem.)
We can separate the descendants of each of these immigrants, and write

\displaystyle X_{i}(t)=\sum_{k:T_{k}\leqslant t}Y_{k}(t-T_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}Y_{k}(t-T_{k})

(3.28)

where each
Y_{k}(t)
, conditioned on
\eta_{k}
,
is a copy of the single-colour CB process in Section
3.1

with
Y_{k}(0)=\eta_{k}
. Furthermore, conditionally on
(\eta_{k})_{k}
,
the processes
Y_{k}(t)
are independent.
Recall that
the additions
\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots

are i.i.d. copies of
\xi_{ji}
; in particular, they have expectations

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\eta_{k}=r_{ji}
.
The times
T_{k}
are stopping times; moreover, the process
Y_{k}
is
independent of
T_{k}
and the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{T_{k}}
.

The following lemma contains much of the technical parts of our argument.
It makes an assumption on the growth of
X_{j}(t)

that later will be justified by an induction argument.

We define, for
m\in\mathsf{Q}
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{m}(t)

\displaystyle:=t^{-\kappa_{m}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{m}t}X_{m}(t),\qquad 0<t<\infty,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{**}_{m}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\bigl\{(t+1)^{-\kappa_{m}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{m}t}X_{m}(t)\bigr\}.

(3.30)

We use powers of
t
in (
3.29
) and of
t+1
in (
3.30
) for technical
convenience below (and we therefore use a notation with
∗∗
instead of

∗
);
the difference is not important since we are mainly
interested in limits as
{t\to\infty}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
Suppose that
there is exactly one colour
j\in\mathsf{Q}
such that
j\to i
,
and that
X_{i}(0)=0
.
Suppose further that

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(3.31)

for some random variable
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\geqslant 0
, and

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(3.32)

Then

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(3.33)

for some
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\geqslant 0
and

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(3.34)

Furthermore, a.s.,
if
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
.

Moreover, if
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
, and thus
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}
, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},&\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{j},\\
\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\kappa_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},&\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}.\end{cases}

(3.35)

Proof.

Using (
3.28
), and recalling
Y_{k}(0)=\eta_{k}
, we make the decomposition

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\bigl(e^{-\lambda_{i}(t-T_{k})}Y_{k}(t-T_{k})-Y_{k}(0)\bigr)

(3.36)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}(\eta_{k}-r_{ji})

(3.37)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+r_{ji}\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}a_{j}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigr)

(3.38)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+r_{ji}a_{j}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

(3.39)

\displaystyle=:Z_{1}(t)+Z_{2}(t)+r_{ji}Z_{3}(t)+a_{j}r_{ji}Z_{4}(t),

(3.40)

say.
We consider the four processes
Z_{\ell}(t)
in (
3.40
) separately
(initially, at least).
Moreover,
recalling (
2.8
) and (
2.9
),
we consider sometimes separately the three cases:

(i)

\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
: then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
\kappa_{i}=0
.

(ii)

\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}
:
then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
, and
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{j}+1
.

(iii)

\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{j}
:
then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}
, and
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{j}
.

We define, in analogy with (
3.29
)–(
3.30
) (but note the different
exponents
\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i}
),
for
\ell=1,2,3,4
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle:=t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})t}Z_{\ell}(t),

(3.41)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\bigl\{(t+1)^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})t}|Z_{\ell}(t)|\bigr\}.

(3.42)

Then (
3.40
) yields

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)

\displaystyle={\widetilde{Z}}_{1}(t)+{\widetilde{Z}}_{2}(t)+r_{ji}{\widetilde{Z}}_{3}(t)+a_{j}r_{ji}{\widetilde{Z}}_{4}(t),

(3.43)

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{1}+\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{2}+C\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{3}+C\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{4}.

(3.44)

We will prove, for
\ell=1,\dots,4
and some
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(3.45)

\displaystyle\lVert\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle<\infty,

(3.46)

and then (
3.33
)–(
3.34
) follow from (
3.43
)–(
3.44
).

We treat
Z_{1}(t),\dots,Z_{4}(t)
in (
3.39
) in reverse order,
partly because
Z_{4}(t)
will turn out to be the main term
(sometimes at least).

Step 1:
Z_{4}
. By (
3.39
) and (
3.30
),

\displaystyle 0\leqslant Z_{4}(t)

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leqslant\begin{cases}C\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}=C(t+1)^{\kappa_{i}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j},&\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j},\\
(t+1)^{\kappa_{j}+1}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}=(t+1)^{\kappa_{i}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j},&\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j},\\
C(t+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})t}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j},&\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{j}.\end{cases}

(3.47)

It follows that in all three cases
(i)
–
(iii)
,

\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{4}\leqslant C\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}
. In particular, (
3.32
) implies

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{4}\bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(3.48)

For (
3.45
) we treat the three cases
(i)
–
(iii)
separately,
in each case using (
3.41
) and (
3.29
)–(
3.30
).
First, in case
(i)
, we let
{t\to\infty}
in (
3.39
) and (
3.2
) and
conclude that
a.s.

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{4}(t)=Z_{4}(t)\to Z_{4}(\infty):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant C\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}<\infty.

(3.49)

Hence

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{4}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}_{4}=Z_{4}(\infty).

(3.50)

Note that if
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
, then
X_{j}(t)>0
for large
t
, and thus

{\mathcal{Z}}_{4}=Z_{4}(\infty)>0
by
(
3.49
).

In case
(ii)
, we have for
t\geqslant 1
,
using the change of variables
s=xt
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{4}(t)

\displaystyle=t^{-\kappa_{i}}Z_{4}(t)=t^{-\kappa_{j}-1}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\kappa_{j}}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=t^{-\kappa_{j}-1}\int_{0}^{1}s^{\kappa_{j}}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{1/t}^{1}x^{\kappa_{j}}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(xt)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\to 0+\int_{0}^{1}x^{\kappa_{j}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}x=(\kappa_{j}+1)^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}=\kappa_{i}^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},

(3.51)

by (
3.31
) and dominated convergence, which applies since
(
3.29
)–(
3.30
) show that

\sup_{s\geqslant 1}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(s)\leqslant 2^{\kappa_{j}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}<\infty
a.s.

In case
(iii)
, similarly, now with
s=t-u
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}_{4}(t)

\displaystyle=t^{-\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{j})t}\int_{0}^{t}s^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})s}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\frac{t-u}{t}\Bigr)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{-(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})u}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(t-u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})u}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}u=(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},

(3.52)

using dominated convergence again,
justified by, for
t\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\Bigl(\frac{t-u}{t}\Bigr)^{\kappa_{j}}{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(t-u)\leqslant\Bigl(\frac{t-u+1}{t}\Bigr)^{\kappa_{j}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\leqslant 2^{\kappa_{j}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}<\infty.

(3.53)

We have thus shown (
3.45
) and (
3.46
) for
\ell=4
in all cases,
with
{\mathcal{Z}}_{4}>0
when
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
.
Furthermore, in cases
(ii)
and
(iii)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}_{4}=\begin{cases}\kappa_{i}^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},&\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j},\\
(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{j},&\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{j}.\end{cases}

(3.54)

Step 2:
Z_{3}
, first part
. We have, recalling (
3.1
),

\displaystyle Z_{3}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{N}(s),

(3.55)

which is a local martingale since
\widetilde{N}(t)
is;
in fact it is a martingale since

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{*}(t)\leqslant N(t)+a_{j}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant N(t)+tX_{j}(t),

(3.56)

and thus
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}Z_{3}^{*}(t)<\infty
by Lemma
3.1
, noting that the assumption
(
3.32
) implies
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{j}(t)<\infty
for all
t\geqslant 0
.
Since
Z_{3}(t)
has locally bounded variation and jumps only at the times

T_{k}
,
with
\Delta Z_{3}(T_{k})=e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}
,
its quadratic variation is

\displaystyle[Z_{3},Z_{3}]_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda_{i}s}\,\mathrm{d}N(s).

(3.57)

The draws
T_{k}
occur with rate
a_{j}X_{j}(t)
,
and it follows by (
3.57
) and Lemma
3.2

that, recalling (
3.30
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}|Z_{3}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{3},Z_{3}]_{t}=a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\lambda_{i}s}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leqslant a_{j}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.58)

The rest of the argument will be the same for
Z_{2}
and
Z_{1}
, so we first
consider them.

Step 3:
Z_{2}
, first part
. Each term in the sum (
3.37
) is a martingale, since
T_{k}
is a stopping
time, and
\eta_{k}-r_{ji}
has mean 0 and
is independent of the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}_{T_{k}}
.
Hence, the sum
Z_{2}(t)
is at least a local martingale (since stopping at
any
T_{k}
yields a finite sum and thus a martingale).
Since
Z_{2}(t)
jumps only at the times
T_{k}
, and is constant in between,
its quadratic variation is given by

\displaystyle[Z_{2},Z_{2}]_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}(\eta_{k}-r_{ji})^{2}.

(3.59)

Hence, again using the independence between
\eta_{k}
and
T_{k}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{2},Z_{2}]_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\bigr]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[(\eta_{k}-r_{ji})^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Var}[\eta_{1}]\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}}.

(3.60)

We recognize the final sum from (
3.57
), and conclude

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{2},Z_{2}]_{t}

\displaystyle=C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{3},Z_{3}]_{t}.

(3.61)

Step 4:
Z_{1}
, first part
.
We write the sum in (
3.36
) as
Z_{1}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}Z_{1}^{(k)}(t)
, with

\displaystyle Z^{(k)}_{1}(t):=\boldsymbol{1}_{\{t\geqslant T_{k}\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\bigl(e^{-\lambda_{i}(t-T_{k})}Y_{k}(t-T_{k})-Y_{k}(0)\bigr).

(3.62)

It is easily seen that
each
Z^{(k)}_{1}(t)
is a martingale,
since
T_{k}
is a stopping time
and
e^{-\lambda_{i}t}Y_{k}(t)-Y_{k}(0)
is a martingale starting at 0,
which furthermore is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{T_{k}}
.
Hence, for every finite
m\geqslant 1
, the finite sum

\displaystyle Z^{[\leqslant m]}_{1}(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{m}Z^{(k)}_{1}(t)

(3.63)

is a martingale, and thus

Z_{1}(t\land T_{m})=Z^{[\leqslant m]}_{1}(t\land T_{m})
is a martingale.
Consequently,
Z_{1}
is a local martingale.
Furthermore, conditioned on all
T_{k}
and
\eta_{k}
, the
processes
Z_{1}^{(k)}(t)
are independent and thus a.s.
they jump at different times. (Note that
Z_{1}^{(k)}(0)=0
.)
Hence, by (
2.16
),

\displaystyle[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}[Z_{1}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}]_{t}

(3.64)

and thus, using (
3.62
) and (
2.17
) again together with (
3.16
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}\mid(T_{k},\eta_{k})_{1}^{\infty}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[[Z_{1}^{(k)},Z_{1}^{(k)}]_{t}\mid(T_{k},\eta_{k})_{1}^{\infty}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\operatorname{Var}\bigl[e^{-\lambda_{i}(t-T_{k})}Y_{k}(t-T_{k})\mid T_{k},\eta_{k}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}C\eta_{k}.

(3.65)

This yields, by taking the expectation,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}.

(3.66)

This is the same sum as in (
3.57
), and we conclude

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{3},Z_{3}]_{t}.

(3.67)

Step 5:
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}
, final part
.
Let
\ell\in\{1,2,3\}
.
In all three cases,

Z_{\ell}(t)
is a local martingale such that,
by (
3.61
), (
3.67
), and (
3.2
),
for
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{t}\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{3},Z_{3}]_{t}\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.68)

In particular, (
3.68
) shows that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{t}<\infty
for every
t<\infty
,
and thus
Z_{\ell}(t)
is a square
integrable martingale,
and
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}|Z_{\ell}(t)|^{2}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{t}
.

We now consider another partition into three separate cases for
\lambda^{*}_{j}
and

\lambda_{i}
.

(i
′
)
\lambda^{*}_{j}<2\lambda_{i}
.
Then we can let
{t\to\infty}
in (
3.68
) and obtain

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{\infty}<\infty
. Consequently,
Z_{\ell}(t)
is an
L^{2}
-bounded
martingale, and thus

\displaystyle Z_{\ell}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}Z_{\ell}(\infty)<\infty,\qquad\text{as ${t\to\infty}$},

(3.69)

and

Z_{\ell}^{*}(\infty):=\sup_{t\geqslant 0}|Z_{\ell}(t)|\in L^{2}
.
Furthermore (as always),
\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant\lambda_{i}
, and thus
(
3.42
) implies
\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\leqslant Z^{*}_{\ell}(\infty)
; consequently,

\lVert\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}<\infty
.

We consider two subcases, recalling the cases discussed at the beginning of
the proof:

(i
′
a)

Case
(i)
,
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
.

Then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
\kappa_{i}=0
;
hence (
3.41
)
yields
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)=Z_{\ell}(t)
and thus (
3.69
) yields

{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}:=Z_{\ell}(\infty)
a.s.

(i
′
b)

Case
(ii)
or
(iii)
,
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
.

Then
\lambda^{*}_{i}>\lambda_{i}
or
\kappa_{i}\geqslant 1
and thus
(
3.41
) and (
3.69
) yield

{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}:=0
a.s.

(ii
′
)
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 2\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda^{*}_{j}>0
.
Let, for
n\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{n-1\leqslant t\leqslant n}(t+1)^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})t}|Z_{\ell}(t)|.

(3.70)

It follows from Doob’s inequality (
2.18
) and (
3.68
) that,
with
\overline{C}:=C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cn^{-2\kappa_{i}}e^{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})n}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}Z^{*}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cn^{-2\kappa_{i}}e^{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})n}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{\ell},Z_{\ell}]_{n}

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{C}n^{-2\kappa_{i}}e^{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})n}\int_{0}^{n}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{C}n^{-2\kappa_{i}}e^{2(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})n}n^{\kappa_{j}+1}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})n}

\displaystyle=\overline{C}n^{\kappa_{j}+1-2\kappa_{i}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda^{*}_{i})n}.

(3.71)

We have always
\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
, and thus, in the present case,

\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda^{*}_{i}\leqslant-\lambda^{*}_{j}<0
.
Hence (
3.2
) yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}<\infty.

(3.72)

Consequently, a.s.
{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)\to 0
as
{n\to\infty}
, which by (
3.41
) and
(
3.70
)
means
{\widetilde{Z}}_{\ell}(t)\to 0
as
{t\to\infty}
.
Moreover, (
3.42
) implies

\displaystyle\bigl(\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\bigr)^{2}\leqslant\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\dagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2},

(3.73)

and thus (
3.72
) implies also
\lVert\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\rVert_{2}<\infty
.

(iii
′
)
\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda_{i}=0
.
In this case we have
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
and
\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{j}+1
. Let, for
n\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{2^{n-1}\leqslant t\leqslant 2^{n}}t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{(\lambda_{i}-\lambda^{*}_{i})t}|Z_{\ell}(t)|=\sup_{2^{n-1}\leqslant t\leqslant 2^{n}}t^{-\kappa_{j}-1}|Z_{\ell}(t)|.

(3.74)

Similarly to (
3.2
),
it follows from Doob’s inequality and (
3.68
) that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{C}2^{-2(\kappa_{j}+1)n}\int_{0}^{2^{n}}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\overline{C}2^{-(\kappa_{j}+1)n}.

(3.75)

Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}<\infty,

(3.76)

and the rest of the argument is as in the preceding case,
now using

\displaystyle\bigl(\widetilde{Z}^{**}_{\ell}\bigr)^{2}\leqslant{Z_{\ell}^{*}(1)}^{2}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

(3.77)

and noting that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{Z_{\ell}^{*}(1)}^{2}<\infty
by (
2.18
) and
(
3.68
).

We have shown that (
3.45
) and (
3.46
) hold for
\ell\leqslant 3
in all
cases,
with
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}=0
except in the case
(i)
.

Step 6: Conclusion
.
We have shown that (
3.45
)–(
3.46
) hold for every
\ell
;
consequently, (
3.33
)–(
3.34
) hold by (
3.43
)–(
3.44
).

Moreover, in cases
(ii)
and
(iii)
,
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\ell}=0
for
\ell=1,2,3
,
and thus
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=a_{j}r_{ji}{\mathcal{Z}}_{4}
; this
yields (
3.35
) by (
3.54
), which in particular shows
that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
when
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
in these cases.

It remains to show
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
when
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0

in the case
(i)
, i.e., when
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
.
In this case
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
\kappa_{i}=0
,
and thus by (
3.29
) and (
3.28
),

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)=e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda_{i}t}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}Y_{k}(t-T_{k}).

(3.78)

Moreover, if
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
, then
\liminf_{t\to\infty}X_{j}(t)>0
and thus a.s. there
is an infinite number of draws of colour
j
, and thus all
T_{k}
are finite.
Let
K
be the (random) smallest
k
such that
\eta_{k}>0
; such
k
exist
a.s. since

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\eta_{k}=r_{ji}>0
by assumption.
Then (
3.78
) implies

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\geqslant e^{-\lambda_{i}T_{K}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{t\geqslant T_{K}\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}(t-T_{K})}Y_{K}(t-T_{K}),

(3.79)

which
a.s. has a strictly positive limit
by conditioning on
K
and applying Lemma
3.3
to
Y_{K}
.
We have already shown that the limit
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
in (
3.33
) exists a.s., and
(
3.79
) then shows
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s.
∎

If
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is determined by
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
, see
(
3.35
).
On the contrary, if
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}

is not determined by
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
, as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}

in Lemma
3.5
,
then the distribution of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}

conditioned on
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
is non-degenerate.
In fact, then
the conditional distribution
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{j})
is a.s. absolutely
continuous.

Proof.

Since
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
, we have (
3.78
). Condition on the entire process

(X_{j}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
and on all
T_{k}
and
\eta_{k}
;
then the terms in the sum in (
3.78
)
are independent, and, as
{t\to\infty}
, each term converges a.s. by Lemma
3.3
to
a limit that by
Lemma
3.4
has an absolutely continuous distribution
when
\eta_{k}>0
.
Since a.s.
\eta_{k}>0
for infinitely many
k
,
it follows that
the limit is a.s. (conditionally) absolutely continuous.
Since
(X_{j}(t))_{t}
determines
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
,
this implies (by Lemma
A.1
)
that the distribution a.s. is absolutely continuous also if we condition
only on
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
.
∎

3.3.
The general case for a single colour

We have in the preceding subsections considered two special cases. We
consider now a single colour
i
in a general triangular urn.
We continue to use the notations (
3.29
)–(
3.30
).
Recall also that
\mathsf{P}_{i}:=\{j\in\mathsf{Q}:j\to i\}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, and assume that for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
,
we have

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{j}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(3.80)

for some random variable
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}\geqslant 0
, and

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(3.81)

Then

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(3.82)

for some
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\geqslant 0
and

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(3.83)

Furthermore, a.s.,
if
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
,
then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
.

Proof.

If
\mathsf{P}_{i}=\emptyset
, we are in the situation of Lemma
3.3
. Moreover, in this
case
X_{i}(0)>0
by our standing assumption
(A3)
.
Consequently, in this case the result follows from Lemma
3.3
.
(Note that
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
and
\kappa_{i}=0
by (
2.8
) and (
2.9
).)

In general,
we separate the balls of colour
i
according to their original reason for
existing.
Formally, we split the colour
i
and replace it by several
“subcolours” (or shades); we define one subcolour labelled
i_{0}
,
and an additional subcolour
i_{j}
for each
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
.
These subcolours have the same replacement vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
as
i
, with the
modification that new balls of colour
i
always get the same subcolour as the
drawn ball.
Also, balls of colour
i
that are added when a ball of some
other colour
j
is drawn get the subcolour
i_{j}
.
Furthermore, all balls of colour
i
at time 0 get subcolour
i_{0}
.
In other words,
i_{0}
is used for
descendants of the balls with colour
i
in the urn at the beginning, and

i_{j}
are used for balls of colour
i
that eventually descend from a ball
of colour
j
. Note that
i_{0}
is minimal, while
\mathsf{P}_{i_{j}}=\{j\}
.

We thus have

\displaystyle X_{i}(t)=X_{i_{0}}(t)+\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}}X_{i_{j}}(t).

(3.84)

Moreover, in the modified urn with subcolours instead of colour
i
,
the subcolour
i_{0}
is of the type in Section
3.1
(possibly with
x_{0}=0
), and
each subcolour
{i_{j}}
(
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
)
is of the type in Section
3.2
.
Hence, Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
apply, using our assumptions
(
3.80
)–(
3.81
).
It follows from (
2.8
) that

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{i_{0}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i},

(3.85)

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}\lor\lambda^{*}_{j},\qquad j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}.

(3.86)

In particular,
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}
,
we have
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}
.
Furthermore, if
\mathsf{P}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\lor\max\{\lambda^{*}_{j}:j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\}=\max\{\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}:j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\}.

(3.87)

Moreover, (
2.9
) yields
\kappa_{i_{0}}=0
, and implies also that if

\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
, then
\kappa_{i_{j}}\leqslant\kappa_{i}
.
Hence,
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}
,
and for all large
t
(at least),

\displaystyle t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}\leqslant t^{-\kappa_{i_{j}}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}t}.

(3.88)

Consequently, it follows from
(
3.84
) and the definition (
3.29
) that,
at least for large
t
,

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\leqslant{\widetilde{X}}_{i_{0}}(t)+\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}}{\widetilde{X}}_{i_{j}}(t),

(3.89)

and furthermore, using also (
3.13
) and (
3.33
),

\displaystyle{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}:=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{(\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}},\kappa_{i_{j}})=(\lambda^{*}_{i},\kappa_{i})\}}.

(3.90)

This shows (
3.82
).

Similarly,

(t+1)^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}\leqslant C(t+1)^{-\kappa_{i_{j}}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}t}

for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}
and every
t\geqslant 0
,
and thus (
3.84
) and (
3.30
) imply

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\leqslant C\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i_{0}}+C\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i_{j}}.

(3.91)

Hence,
(
3.83
) follows from (
3.17
) and (
3.34
).

Finally, assume
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
.
If
\mathsf{P}_{i}=\emptyset
, then, as remarked above,

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s. by
(A3)
and Lemma
3.3
.
On the other hand, if
\mathsf{P}_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}>0
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
by Lemma
3.5
.
By (
3.87
) and (
2.9
), there exists some
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
such that

(\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}},\kappa_{i_{j}})=(\lambda^{*}_{i},\kappa_{i})
, and thus (
3.90
) implies

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\geqslant{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}>0

(3.92)

a.s., which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

If
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
, then

\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\kappa_{i_{j}}=0=\kappa_{i}

for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}
, and thus
(
3.90
) yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}.

(3.93)

On the other hand, suppose that

\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
for some
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
. Then either

\lambda^{*}_{i}>\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i_{0}}
, or
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}
for some
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
,
and in the
latter case
\kappa_{i}\geqslant 1+\kappa_{j}>\kappa_{i_{0}}=0
. Hence, in both cases,

(\lambda^{*}_{i_{0}},\kappa_{i_{0}})\neq(\lambda^{*}_{i},\kappa_{i})
, and thus
the sum in
(
3.90
) is really only over (some)
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
.

\triangle

4.
The main theorem for continuous time

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a continuous-time
triangular Pólya urn satisfying the
conditions
(A1)
–
(A5)
.
Then, for every colour
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,

\displaystyle t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty},

(4.1)

for some random variable
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
with
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s.

Proof.

We may choose a total order
<
of the colours such that (
2.5
) holds.
Taking the colours in this order, we see by Lemma
3.7
and induction that
(
3.82
) and (
3.83
) hold for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, with
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s.
Recalling (
3.29
), we see that (
3.82
) is the same as (
4.1
).
∎

The limits
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
are strictly positive.
We will see in Theorem
6.4
that they are
non-degenerate when
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
;
however, in general some of them will be
linear combinations of others.
We discuss dependencies between the limits in Section
6
.

5.
Proof of the main theorem for discrete time

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

We modify the urn by adding a new colour, 0 say, to
\mathsf{Q}
;
let
\mathsf{Q}^{+}:=\mathsf{Q}\cup\{0\}
be the new set of colours.
All activities
a_{i}
and replacements

\xi_{ij}
with
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
remain unchanged.
Balls of colour 0 have activity
a_{0}:=0
, and thus they are never drawn; in
accordance with
(A2)
we define
\xi_{0j}:=0
for every
j\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
.
We further let
\xi_{i0}:=1
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
with
a_{i}>0
.
For
i\in\mathsf{Q}
with
a_{i}=0
we let
\xi_{i0}:=0
, again in accordance with

(A2)
.
We let
X_{00}=X_{0}(0):=0
, so there are initially no balls of colour 0.
Note that the extended urn too satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
.

Since balls of colour 0 never are drawn, we may ignore them and recover the
original urn. In other words,
the new urn will be the original urn with “dummy balls” of colour 0
added.
We may assume that the old and new urn are coupled in
this way, which means that for
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, the number of balls of colour
i

at any time is the same in both urns, so we may unambiguously use
the notations
X_{ni}
and
X_{i}(t)
for both urns.
Moreover, there are initially no balls of colour
0
, but we add exactly 1
ball of colour 0 every time a ball is drawn. Hence,
in the discrete-time version, the number of dummy balls at time

n
equals
n
; thus
X_{n0}=n
.
In the continuous-time model,
X_{0}(t)
equals the number of
draws up to time
t
, and in particular, cf. (
2.19
),

\displaystyle X_{0}(\widehat{T}_{n})=X_{n0}=n.

(5.1)

The new urn is obviously also triangular, with
i\to 0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}

with
a_{i}>0
.
We have
\lambda_{0}=0
.
Since every
\lambda_{i}\geqslant 0
, and we have
i\to 0
when
\lambda_{i}>0

(and thus
a_{i}>0
by
(A2)
), it follows from (
2.8
) and
(
2.10
) that

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{0}=\max\{\lambda_{j}:j\in\mathsf{Q}\}=\widehat{\lambda}.

(5.2)

If
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then
is further easily seen from (
2.9
) and (
2.4
) that

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}=\max\{\kappa_{i}:i\in\mathsf{Q}\text{ and }\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}\}=\widehat{\kappa}.

(5.3)

If
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, which means that
\lambda_{i}=0
for every
i
, we have by
(
2.9
) and (
2.12
) instead

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}

\displaystyle=\max\bigl\{\kappa:\exists i_{1}\prec i_{2}\prec\dots\prec i_{\kappa+1}=0\text{ in }\mathsf{Q}^{+}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\max\bigl\{\kappa:\exists i_{1}\prec i_{2}\prec\dots\prec i_{\kappa}\text{ with }a_{i_{\kappa}}>0\text{ in }\mathsf{Q}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=1+\max\bigl\{\kappa_{j}:j\in\mathsf{Q}\text{ with }a_{j}>0\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\kappa}_{0}.

(5.4)

We apply Theorem
4.1
to the new urn.
In particular,
taking
i=0
in (
4.1
) yields,
using (
5.1
) and (
5.2
)–(
5
)
and recalling that
\widehat{T}_{n}\to\infty
a.s.,

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}^{-\kappa_{0}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}}n=\widehat{T}_{n}^{-\kappa_{0}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}}X_{0}(\widehat{T}_{n})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\qquad\text{as }{n\to\infty}.

(5.5)

Recall that
0<{\mathcal{X}}_{0}<\infty
a.s.

Case 1:
\widehat{\lambda}>0
.

Consider first the case
\widehat{\lambda}>0
; then
\kappa_{0}=\widehat{\kappa}
by (
5.3
).
It follows from (
5.5
) that a.s., as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\log n-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}-\widehat{\kappa}\log\widehat{T}_{n}\to\log{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

(5.6)

which yields, since
\widehat{T}_{n}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{\log n}{\widehat{T}_{n}}

\displaystyle\to\widehat{\lambda},

(5.7)

and thus

\displaystyle\log\log n-\log\widehat{T}_{n}

\displaystyle\to\log\widehat{\lambda}.

(5.8)

Using (
5.8
) in (
5.6
) yields, a.s.,

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}=\frac{1}{\widehat{\lambda}}\bigl(\log n-\widehat{\kappa}\log\log n+\widehat{\kappa}\log\widehat{\lambda}-\log{\mathcal{X}}_{0}+o(1)\bigr).

(5.9)

Now let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
. Then (
5.9
) yields, as
{n\to\infty}
, a.s.,

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}^{\kappa_{i}}e^{\lambda^{*}_{i}\widehat{T}_{n}}\sim\widehat{\lambda}^{-\kappa_{i}+\widehat{\kappa}\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}(\log n)^{\kappa_{i}-\widehat{\kappa}\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}.

(5.10)

Consequently, taking
t=\widehat{T}_{n}
in (
4.1
) yields, as
{n\to\infty}
,
using the notation (
2.13
),

\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}\log^{\gamma_{i}}n}=\frac{X_{i}(\widehat{T}_{n})}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}(\log n)^{\kappa_{i}-\widehat{\kappa}\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}

(5.11)

with

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\kappa_{i}+\widehat{\kappa}\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\gamma_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(5.12)

This shows (
1.3
).

Case 2:
\widehat{\lambda}=0
.

In the case
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, we have instead
\kappa_{0}=\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
by (
5
).
Moreover, (
5.5
) now yields that as
{n\to\infty}
, a.s.,

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}\sim{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-1/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}n^{1/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}

(5.13)

and thus (
4.1
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\kappa_{i}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}=\frac{X_{i}(\widehat{T}_{n})}{n^{\kappa_{i}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}:={\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-\kappa_{i}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(5.14)

This shows (
1.4
), and completes the proof of Theorem
1.8
.
∎

The limits
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
are all strictly positive.
We return to the question whether they are degenerate in Section
7
.

Remark 5.1
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, so that (
5.11
) holds. As a sanity check, we
note that (
5.11
) and (
5.12
) imply,
recalling (
2.10
) and (
2.4
),
that
if we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{*}:=\{i\in\mathsf{Q}:\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}\text{ and }\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}\},

(5.15)

which is nonempty,
then
