\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\begin{cases}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}={\mathcal{X}}_{i}/{\mathcal{X}}_{0}&\text{if }i\in\mathsf{Q}_{*},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.16)

Hence, the total number of balls grows linearly, as should be expected;
moreover, the distribution of colours in the urn is asymptotically
concentrated on
\mathsf{Q}_{*}
.

Note also that (
3.90
)
for the dummy colour 0 yields,
recalling (
5.2
)–(
5.3
) and using (
3.35
),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}_{*}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0_{j}}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}_{*}}\frac{a_{j}}{\lambda^{*}_{j}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}_{*}}a_{j}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}.

(5.17)

By (
5.16
) and (
5.17
), we have for the total activity in the urn

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}a_{i}X_{ni}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{Q}_{*}}a_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}.

(5.18)

\triangle

Remark 5.2
.

If we instead suppose
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, which implies
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
for every
i
,
then (
5.14
) similarly shows that
if we now define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}_{*}:=\{i:\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}_{0}\},

(5.19)

then (
5.16
) still holds.
Note that it is quite possible that
\mathsf{Q}_{*}
is empty;
this happens precisely when
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}>\widehat{\kappa}
, which by (
5
) happens when
some
i
with
\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}
has activity
a_{i}>0
.
(Such a colour cannot have any descendants, since all colours have the same

\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
; hence we must have
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}=0
a.s., which means that such colours
will be drawn, but nothing happens to the urn at these draws.)
In this case the total
number of balls is a.s.
o(n)
, and the colour distribution is
asymptotically concentrated on the colours
i
such that

\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}=\widehat{\kappa}_{0}-1
.

On the other hand, if
\mathsf{Q}_{*}\neq\emptyset
, which means that
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}=\widehat{\kappa}
,
and thus by (
5
)
a_{i}=0
for every
i
with
\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}
,
let
\mathsf{Q}_{*-}:=\{j:\kappa_{j}=\widehat{\kappa}-1\}
.
Then (
3.90
) and (
3.35
) yield,
since
j\to 0
if and only if
a_{j}>0
, and then
\kappa_{0_{j}}=\kappa_{j}+1
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{P}_{0}\cap\mathsf{Q}_{*-}}{\mathcal{X}}_{0_{j}}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}_{*-}}\frac{a_{j}}{\widehat{\kappa}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}=\widehat{\kappa}^{-1}\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}_{*-}}a_{j}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}.

(5.20)

\triangle

6.
Dependencies between the limits

The limits
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
in Theorem
4.1
are non-degenerate except in extreme cases,
as shown below,
but there are frequently linear dependencies between them.
To explore this, we introduce more terminology.

We say that a colour
i
is a
leader
if
\lambda_{j}<\lambda_{i}
for every

j\prec i
,
and a
follower
otherwise.
(In particular, a minimal colour
i
is a leader.)
We have, recalling (
2.8
),

\displaystyle i\text{ is a leader}

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(j\prec i\implies\lambda_{j}<\lambda_{i}\Bigr)\iff\Bigl(j\to i\implies\lambda^{*}_{j}<\lambda_{i}\Bigr),

(6.1)

\displaystyle i\text{ is a follower}

\displaystyle\iff\Bigl(\exists j\prec i\text{ with }\lambda_{j}\geqslant\lambda_{i}\Bigr)\iff\Bigl(\exists j\to i\text{ with }\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant\lambda_{i}\Bigr).

(6.2)

By (
2.8
)–(
2.9
),

\displaystyle i\text{ is a leader}\iff\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\text{ and }\kappa_{i}=0.

(6.3)

If
i
is a follower,
let
\mathsf{A}_{i}
be the set
of ancestors of
i
that have maximal
\lambda_{j}
,
i.e.,
\lambda_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
,
and such that furthermore there is a path from
j
to
i
with the
maximum number
\kappa_{i}+1
of colours
\ell
with
\lambda_{\ell}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
.
(Recall (
2.8
) and (
2.9
).) Thus

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{i}:=\{j\prec i:\exists i_{1}=j\prec i_{2}\prec\dots\prec i_{\kappa_{i}+1}\preceq i\text{ with }\lambda_{i_{1}}=\dots=\lambda_{i_{\kappa+1}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}\}.

(6.4)

Note that
\mathsf{A}_{i}
is non-empty for every follower
i
.
Moreover, it is easily seen from (
6.1
)–(
6.2
) and (
6.4
)
that every
j\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
is a leader.
We may say that
i
follows the leaders in
\mathsf{A}_{i}
; note that a follower may
follow several leaders.
For completeness, we define
\mathsf{A}_{i}:=\{i\}
when
i
is a leader.
Note that, by (
6.3
) and (
6.4
),

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i}\qquad\text{for every $j\in\mathsf{A}_{i}$}.

(6.5)

We show first that the variables
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
are determined by the ones for
leaders
i
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is a linear combination

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\sum_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}c_{ik}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}

(6.6)

with strictly positive coefficients
c_{ik}
.

Proof.

Note first that (
6.6
) is trivial when
i
is a leader, with

c_{ii}=1
.
We may thus suppose that
i
is a follower.

By induction on the colour
i
, we may assume that the formula
(
6.6
) holds for every colour that is an ancestor of
i
,
and in particular for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
.

By Remark
3.8
, we only have to consider
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
in (
3.90
).
Let

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{i}^{\prime}:=\bigl\{j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}:(\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}},\kappa_{i_{j}})=(\lambda^{*}_{i},\kappa_{i})\bigr\},

(6.7)

so that the sum in (
3.90
) really is over
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}^{\prime}
.
By (
6.3
), since we now assume that
i
is a follower,
either
\lambda^{*}_{i}>\lambda_{i}
or
\kappa_{i}>0
(or both).
Hence,
if
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}^{\prime}
, then either
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}>\lambda_{i}
or
\kappa_{i_{j}}=\kappa_{i}>0
;
since
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda_{i}\lor\lambda^{*}_{j}
,
it follows in both cases (using (
2.9
) in the latter)
that
\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}\geqslant\lambda_{i}
and thus
by (
3.35
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}=c^{\prime}_{ij}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}

(6.8)

for some constant
c^{\prime}_{ij}>0
.
Moreover, we have

\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{j}\lor\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}

and it follows from (
6.7
) and (
2.9
) that

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}=\kappa_{i_{j}}=\kappa_{j}+\boldsymbol{1}_{\{\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}\}}.

(6.9)

If
j\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{i}
is a leader, so
\lambda_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\kappa_{j}=0
by
(
6.3
),
it follows from (
6.4
) and (
6.9
) that
j\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
.
Similarly, if
j\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{i}
is a follower, then it follows
from (
6.4
) and (
6.9
) that
if
k\in\mathsf{A}_{j}
, then a chain from
k
to
j
of the (maximal) type
in (
6.4
) can be
extended to a chain from
k
to
i
of the same type, and thus
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
;
consequently, if
j\in\mathsf{P}^{\prime}_{i}
is a follower, then
\mathsf{A}_{j}\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{i}
.
The result (
6.6
), with some
c_{ij}\geqslant 0
, now follows by (
3.90
),
(
6.8
), and induction.

Finally, if
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
,
let
i_{1}=k,\dots,i_{\kappa_{i}+1}\preceq i
be as in (
6.4
), choose a path
in
\mathsf{Q}
from
k
to
i
that contains all
i_{\ell}
, and let
j
be the last
colour in this path before
i
.
Then it follows from (
6.4
) that
k\in\mathsf{A}_{j}
, and thus by induction

c_{jk}>0
, which implies
c_{ik}\geqslant c^{\prime}_{ij}c_{jk}>0
.
∎

Motivated by (
6.6
), we turn to considering
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
for leaders
i
.
We first consider a trivial exceptional case.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
i
be a colour with
\lambda_{i}=0
. Then
i
is a leader if and only if

i
is minimal, i.e., there is no colour
j
with
j\to i
.
In this case
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=X_{i}(0)
is a deterministic positive constant.

Proof.

We have already remarked that a minimal colour is a leader.
Conversely,
if
i
is a leader with
\lambda_{i}=0
, then (
6.1
) shows that
j\to i

is impossible; hence
i
is minimal.
In this case, no balls of colour
i
are added by draws of balls
of other colours. Furthermore,
since
\lambda_{i}=0
, also no balls of colour
i
are added by
draws of colour
i
. Consequently,
X_{i}(t)=X_{i}(0)
for all
t\geqslant 0
,
and (
3.80
) holds trivially with
{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)=X_{i}(t)
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=X_{i}(0)
.
∎

We next show that except for the case in Lemma
6.2
, the distribution of

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
for a leader
i
is absolutely continuous, and thus non-degenerate.

Lemma 6.3
.

(i)

Let
i
be a leader with
\lambda_{i}>0
, and let
\mathsf{E}
be a set of colours
that contains neither
i
nor any descendant of
i
, i.e.,
if
j\in\mathsf{E}
then
j\not\succeq i
. Then
the conditional distribution
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{j},j\in\mathsf{E})
is
a.s. absolutely continuous.
In particular, the distribution of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is absolutely continuous.

(ii)

More generally, let
\mathsf{L}
be a (non-empty) set of leaders with
\lambda_{i}>0
for
every
i\in\mathsf{L}
, and
suppose that
\mathsf{L}
is an anti-chain in
\mathsf{Q}
, i.e.,
i\not\prec j
when

i,j\in\mathsf{L}
.
Let further
\mathsf{E}
be a set of colours such that if
j\in\mathsf{E}
,
then
j\not\succeq i
for every
i\in\mathsf{L}
.
Then the joint conditional distribution

{\mathcal{L}}(({\mathcal{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}}\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{j},j\in\mathsf{E})
is a.s. absolutely continuous.
(This is a distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathsf{L}|}
.)

Proof.

It suffices to consider
6.3
.
The conclusion
(conditional absolute continuity) is preserved if we reduce
\mathsf{E}
to a
smaller set, see Lemma
A.1
and Remark
A.2
.
Thus we may assume that
\mathsf{E}
is maximal, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}=\{j\in\mathsf{Q}:j\not\succeq i,\forall i\in\mathsf{L}\}.

(6.10)

Note that,
if
i\in\mathsf{L}
and
j\prec i
, then we cannot have
j\succeq k
for any

k\in\mathsf{L}
, since this would imply
k\prec i
, contradicting the assumption
that
\mathsf{L}
is an antichain.
Consequently, by (
6.10
), if
i\in\mathsf{L}
and
j\prec i
, then
j\in\mathsf{E}
.

We argue as in the proof of Lemma
3.6
, and condition on the entire
processes
(X_{j}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
,
j\in\mathsf{E}
, and also on the times of all
draws of a color
j\in\mathsf{E}
, and on all replacement vectors
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{j}^{(n)}

(
n\geqslant 1
,
j\in\mathsf{E}
).

Let
i\in\mathsf{L}
. If
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
, so
j\to i
, then
\lambda^{*}_{j}<\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
by the
assumption that
i
is a leader.
We use again the decomposition (
3.84
), and note that each

e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i_{j}}(t)

(
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
) can be written as a sum as in (
3.78
) with (conditionally)
independent terms, and it follows as in the proof of Lemma
3.6
that each

{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}
has (conditionally) an absolutely continuous distribution.
Furthermore,
{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{0}}
is independent of conditioning on colours
j\in\mathsf{E}
, and if
X_{i}(0)>0
, then its distribution too is absolutely continuous by
Lemma
3.4

(since we assume
\lambda_{i}>0
).
Moreover (still conditionally), all
{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{0}}
(
i\in\mathsf{L}
,

j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
) are independent.

Since
i
is a leader,
Remark
3.8
shows that (
3.93
) holds. We have shown that (conditionally)
the terms in the sum in (
3.93
) are independent, and all are absolutely continuous
except
{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{0}}
when
X_{i}(0)=0
.
Thus our assumption
(A3)
implies that the sum contains at least one
absolutely continuous summand,
and hence
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is (conditionally) absolutely continuous.

Moreover, this argument shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
for different
i\in\mathsf{L}
are
(conditionally) independent, and since each has an absolutely continuous distribution,
their joint distribution is (conditionally) absolutely continuous.

Finally, Lemma
A.1
(again with Remark
A.2
) shows that
the same holds if we instead condition on

{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
,
j\in\mathsf{E}
.
∎

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\mathsf{L}
be a set of leaders such that
\lambda_{i}>0
for every
i\in\mathsf{L}
.
Then the joint distribution
{\mathcal{L}}\bigl(({\mathcal{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}}\bigr)

is absolutely continuous.

Proof.

Order the elements of the set
\{\lambda_{i}:i\in\mathsf{L}\}
as

\lambda_{(1)}<\dots<\lambda_{(r)}
. Let

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\{i\in\mathsf{L}:\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{(\ell)}\},\qquad 1\leqslant\ell\leqslant r,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{\leqslant\ell}

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{k:=1}^{\ell}\mathsf{L}_{k},\qquad 0\leqslant\ell\leqslant r.

(6.12)

(Thus,
\mathsf{L}_{\leqslant 0}=\emptyset
.)
Let
\ell\in\{1,\dots,r\}
.
If
i,j\in\mathsf{L}_{\ell}
, then (
6.1
) shows that
j\not\prec i
, and thus

\mathsf{L}_{\ell}
is an antichain.
Furthermore, if
i\in\mathsf{L}_{\ell}
and
j\in\mathsf{L}_{\leqslant\ell-1}
, then

\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda_{j}<\lambda_{i}
, and thus
i\not\preceq j
.
Consequently, Lemma
6.3
6.3
shows that the conditional distribution
of
({\mathcal{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}_{\ell}}
given
({\mathcal{X}}_{j})_{j\in\mathsf{L}_{\leqslant\ell-1}}
is a.s. absolutely continuous.

It now follows from Lemma
A.3
by induction that the distribution of

({\mathcal{X}}_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}_{\leqslant\ell}}
is absolutely continuous for
\ell=1,\dots,r
.
Taking
\ell=r
yields the result, since
\mathsf{L}_{r}=\mathsf{L}
.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
be any colour.

(i)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is a deterministic positive constant.

(ii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
has an absolutely continuous distribution.

Proof.

(i)
:
Every
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
is a leader with
\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
. Thus Lemma
6.2
shows
that
{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
is deterministic for
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
. Consequently, (
6.6
)
shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
too is deterministic.

(ii)
:
Every
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
is a leader with
\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
. Thus Theorem
6.4
shows
that the joint distribution of
({\mathcal{X}}_{k})_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}
is absolutely continuous.
It follows from (
6.6
) that the distribution of
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is absolutely continuous.
(See Lemma
A.4
.)
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, then
the coefficients
c_{ik}
in Lemma
6.1
are uniquely determined.

Proof.

Suppose, on the contrary, that (
6.6
) holds a.s. for two different
sets of coefficients
(c_{ik})_{k}
and
(c_{ik}^{\prime})_{k}
.
Let
b_{k}:=c_{ik}-c^{\prime}_{ik}
.
Then
\sum_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}b_{k}{\mathcal{X}}_{k}=0
a.s., and thus
({\mathcal{X}}_{k})_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}

a.s. lies in the certain hyperplane orthogonal to
(b_{k})
.
However, this contradicts Theorem
6.5
which says that the distribution of

({\mathcal{X}}_{k})_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}
is absolutely continuous.
(See also Lemma
A.4
.)
This contradiction proves the claim.
∎

The coefficients
c_{ik}
in (
6.6
) can be found by the recursive
procedure in the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
We proceed to show that they also can be found as eigenvectors of suitable
submatrices of
the weighted mean replacement matrix
(a_{i}r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
.
In the sequel, we let
c_{ik}
be the coefficient given by the inductive
proof of Lemma
6.1
; this determines
c_{ik}
uniquely for
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}

also when
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
.
We further define
c_{ik}:=0
if
k\notin\mathsf{A}_{i}
.

Let
\nu
be a leader, and let

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\nu}

\displaystyle:=\{i:\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{\nu}\},

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}

\displaystyle:=\{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}:\kappa_{i}=\kappa\},\qquad\kappa=0,1,\dots.

(6.14)

(The sets
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
are empty from some
\kappa
on; we only consider
\kappa
with

\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\neq\emptyset
. Note also that
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}=\mathsf{D}_{\nu^{\prime}}
if
\nu^{\prime}
is
another leader with
\lambda_{\nu^{\prime}}=\lambda_{\nu}
.)
Note that if
c_{i\nu}>0
, then
\nu\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
, and thus
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
, and suppose first that
\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{\nu}
.
Then it
follows from Remark
3.8
that in (
3.90
), we only have to sum over

j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
. Moreover, if
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
and
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
, then

\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\kappa_{i_{j}}=\kappa_{j}
. Hence, in (
3.90
) we
only have to sum over
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cap\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
.
Since
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\iff j\to i\iff r_{ji}>0
and
j\neq i
by (
2.3
),
it follows from (
3.90
)
and (
3.35
)
that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\cap\mathsf{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i_{j}}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\setminus\{i\}}\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}.

(6.15)

Consequently, the coefficient
c_{i\nu}
in (
6.6
) is given by

\displaystyle c_{i\nu}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\setminus\{i\}}\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i}}c_{j\nu}.

(6.16)

Since
j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
implies
\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda_{\nu}
, (
6.16
) yields

\displaystyle(\lambda_{\nu}-\lambda_{i})c_{i\nu}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\setminus\{i\}}{a_{j}r_{ji}}c_{j\nu},

(6.17)

for every
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
with
\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{i}
.

On the other hand, suppose that
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{\nu}
.
If
j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}\setminus\{i\}
and
r_{ji}>0
, then
\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda_{\nu}=\lambda^{*}_{i}

and
j\to i
,
and thus
\kappa_{i}\geqslant\kappa_{j}+1
, since a maximal chain in the definition
(
2.9
) of
\kappa_{j}
can be extended by
i
. Hence, if
j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\setminus\{i\}
, then
r_{ji}=0
.
It follows that (
6.17
) holds trivially
for
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
.

Consequently, (
6.17
) holds for every
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
, and thus,
recalling
\lambda_{i}=a_{i}r_{ii}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}{a_{j}r_{ji}}c_{j\nu}=(\lambda_{\nu}-\lambda_{i})c_{i\nu}+a_{i}r_{ii}c_{i\nu}=\lambda_{\nu}c_{i\nu},\qquad i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}.

(6.18)

We summarize, and elaborate, this as a lemma.
We say that
i\in\mathsf{Q}
is a
subleader
if
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
but
i
is not
a leader;
recalling (
6.3
) we thus have

\displaystyle i\text{ is a subleader}\iff\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\text{ and }\kappa_{i}\geqslant 1.

(6.19)

Lemma 6.7
.

For any leader
\nu
, and any
\kappa\geqslant 0
such that

D_{\nu}^{\kappa}\neq\emptyset
,
we have (
6.18
), and thus

(c_{i\nu})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
is a left eigenvector of the
triangular matrix

(a_{i}r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
for its largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\nu}
.

For
\kappa=0
,
the value
c_{i\nu}
for a leader
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{0}
is determined by

c_{i\nu}=\delta_{i\nu}
(i.e.,
1
when
i=\nu
and
0
otherwise),
and these values for the
leaders determine the eigenvector
(c_{i\nu})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
uniquely.

For
\kappa\geqslant 1
,
the value
c_{i\nu}
for a subleader
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
is determined
recursively from the values
c_{j\nu}
for
j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa-1}
by

\displaystyle c_{i\nu}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa-1}}\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\kappa}c_{j\nu},

(6.20)

and these values for the
subleaders determine the eigenvector
(c_{i\nu})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
uniquely.

Proof.

We have shown that (
6.18
) holds, and thus

(c_{i\nu})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}

is a left eigenvector.

Since the matrix
A_{\kappa}:=(a_{j}r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
is triangular, its
eigenvalues are its diagonal elements
a_{j}r_{jj}=\lambda_{j}
,
j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
.
The definition (
2.9
) of
\kappa_{i}
implies that if
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}\neq\emptyset
,
then there exists
i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
, i.e. a leader (if

\kappa=0
) or a subleader (if
\kappa\geqslant 1
). Since
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{\nu}
for every

i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
, it follows that
\lambda^{*}_{\nu}
is the largest eigenvalue, and that
its multiplicity equals the number of leaders or subleaders in
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
.

Let
\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}
be the set of leaders or subleaders in
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
, and consider
the projection
\Pi_{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{{\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}}
mapping

(x_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}\mapsto(x_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}}
. Let
V_{\kappa}
be the left eigenspace of
the matrix
A_{\kappa}
for its largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\nu}
.
The recursion in the proof of Lemma
6.1
and the calculations
(
6.15
)–(
6.17
) show, more generally, that any vector

(x_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}}
can be extended to a vector

(x_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}}
that belongs to
V_{\kappa}
. In other words, the projection

\Pi_{\kappa}
maps
V_{\kappa}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}}
. These spaces
have the same dimension
|\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}|
, and thus

\Pi_{\kappa}:V_{\kappa}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{L}_{\kappa}}
is a bijection.
Consequently, an eigenvector is determined by its values for leaders or
subleaders.

It is trivial that
c_{i\nu}=\delta_{i\nu}
for a leader
i
.

For
\kappa\geqslant 1
, let
i
be a subleader in
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa}
, so
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
.
Since
\kappa_{i}=\kappa>0
, it follows from Remark
3.8
that
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}
for some

j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
, and furthermore that
\kappa_{j}+1\leqslant\kappa_{i}
for every
such
j
.
Since then also
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\kappa_{i_{j}}=\kappa_{j}+1
, it
follows that
in (
3.90
) we only have to sum over

j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa-1}
, which together with (
3.35
) yields (
6.20
).
∎

Remark 6.8
.

It is well known that
the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the
weighted mean replacement matrix
(a_{i}r_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}

are important for the asymptotics of Pólya urns in general;
see for example

[
4
, Section V.9.3]
and

[
27
]

for irreducible urns.
Lemma
6.7
, where we consider eigenvectors of certain submatrices, is inspired
by a special case in
[
12
]
, see Example
14.12
.

\triangle

7.
Degenerate limits in discrete time

Consider now the limits
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
in Theorem
1.8
for the discrete-time urn.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.

(i)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, then
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is a positive constant.

(ii)

If
0<\lambda^{*}_{i}<\widehat{\lambda}
, then
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
has an absolutely continuous distribution.

(iii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is either constant or
has an absolutely continuous distribution; both alternatives are possible.

Proof.

Consider the urn with an added dummy colour 0 as in Section
5
.

(i)
:
In this case, Theorem
6.5
shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is a positive constant.
If
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then
(
5.12
) shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\kappa_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is a constant.
If
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, then (
5.2
) and Theorem
6.5
show that also
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a
positive constant; thus (
5.14
) shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is a constant.

(ii)
:
By (
5.2
), we have
\lambda^{*}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}>0
.
By Lemma
6.1
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is a linear combination (
6.6
) of
{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
for
leaders
k
with
\lambda^{*}_{k}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a similar linear combination,
now for leaders
k
with
\lambda^{*}_{k}=\lambda^{*}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}
.
The two sets of leaders are disjoint, and thus Theorem
6.4
implies, using
Lemma
A.4
, that the distribution of

({\mathcal{X}}_{i},{\mathcal{X}}_{0})
is absolutely continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It is then easily seen from (
5.12
) that the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is
absolutely continuous.

(iii)
:
In this case, Lemma
6.1
shows that both
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
are linear
combinations (
6.6
) of
{\mathcal{X}}_{k}
for
leaders
k
with
\lambda^{*}_{k}=\widehat{\lambda}
.
If the two vectors of coefficients of these linear combinations are
proportional, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
are proportional; since now
(
5.12
) shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}=c{\mathcal{X}}_{i}/{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
for some constant
c
, it follows
that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}
is constant.
On the other hand, if the vectors of coefficients are not proportional, then
Lemma
A.4
shows that the distribution of

({\mathcal{X}}_{i},{\mathcal{X}}_{0})
is absolutely continuous in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and as in
(ii)
, it follows
easily from (
5.12
) that the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is absolutely continuous.
∎

An example where
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is absolutely continuous is given by the
classical Pólya urn, see Example
14.1
. Many examples with degenerate

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
are provided by the following theorem;
see also the examples in Section
14
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Suppose that there is only one leader
\nu
with
\lambda_{\nu}=\widehat{\lambda}
.
(This is equivalent to
\lambda_{\nu}=\widehat{\lambda}
and that
i\succeq\nu
for
every colour
i
with
\lambda_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
.)
Then
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is deterministic for every
i
with
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
.

Proof.

If
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, this follows from Theorem
7.1
(i)
.

Suppose now
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, and
consider the urn with an added dummy colour 0 as in Section
5
.
Then

\lambda^{*}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}
by (
5.2
).
Furthermore,

\lambda_{\nu}=\widehat{\lambda}>0
and thus
a_{\nu}>0
;
hence
\nu\to 0
and consequently

\nu
is still the only leader

k
with
\lambda_{k}=\widehat{\lambda}
.
In particular,
\mathsf{A}_{0}=\{\nu\}
.
Lemma
6.1

shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=c_{i\nu}{\mathcal{X}}_{\nu}
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
with

\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
, and also that
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=c_{0\nu}{\mathcal{X}}_{\nu}
. Hence,
the result follows from
(
5.12
).
∎

Even when the limits
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
are not deterministic, there are
generally
linear dependencies between them just as for
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
.
In fact, in the main case
\widehat{\lambda}>0
,
we have the following analogue of Lemmas
6.1
and
6.7
.

Lemma 7.3
.

If
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=\sum_{k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}}\widehat{c}_{ik}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{k},

(7.1)

where

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{ik}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\kappa_{i}}c_{ik}>0.

(7.2)

Moreover, Lemma
6.7
holds also for
\widehat{c}_{ik}
, with
the only difference that (
6.20
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\widehat{c}_{i\nu}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{D}_{\nu}^{\kappa-1}}\frac{a_{j}r_{ji}}{\kappa\widehat{\lambda}}\widehat{c}_{j\nu}.

(7.3)

Proof.

The expansion (
7.1
)–(
7.2
) follows from (
6.6
) and
(
5.12
),
since
k\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
implies
\lambda^{*}_{k}=\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\kappa_{k}=0
.
The final claim follows by (
7.2
) and Lemma
6.7
.
∎

We leave the corresponding result for the less interesting case
\widehat{\lambda}=0
to
the reader.

8.
Urns with subtractions

We have so far assumed
(A5)
:
\xi_{ij}\geqslant 0
for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
.
In many applications, there are urns with subtractions, where we allow

\xi_{ij}<0
. In the present paper, we are only interested in cases where the
urn allows an infinite number of drawings according to the rules in
Section
1
; in other words we want (
1.1
) to make sense as
probabilities for all
n
, and thus we require that the urn is such
that
X_{ni}\geqslant 0
for all
n
,
and also
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{ni}\neq 0
.
Such urns are often called
tenable
.
(See e.g.
[
38
]
for a discussion and examples.)
A standard way to ensure that

X_{ni}\geqslant 0
without assuming
\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
is to assume that
X_{0i}

and every
\xi_{ji}
always is an integer,
so that
X_{ni}
is an integer,
and that
\xi_{ii}\geqslant-1
while
\xi_{ji}\geqslant 0
for

j\neq i
.
Typically, this is done for all
i
. (Thus
\mathbf{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{q}
; we may then
regard the urn process as drawings without replacement followed by adding

\xi_{ij}+\delta_{ij}\geqslant 0
balls of each colour
j
.) However, we will be
more flexible and allow a combination with this assumption
for some colours
i
, and

\xi_{ji}\geqslant 0
(as in earlier sections) for the others. We therefore assume:

(A5
′
)

For each colour
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, we have either (or both)

(a)

\xi_{ji}\geqslant 0
a.s. for all
j\in\mathsf{Q}
,
or

(b)

\xi_{ii}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}\cup\{-1\}
a.s. and

\xi_{ji}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
a.s. for all
j\neq i
,
and
X_{i}(0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.

Note that
(A5)
is
(A5′)(a)
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
Note also that
(A5′)
implies

\xi_{ji}\geqslant 0
a.s. for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
with
i\neq j
.
We let

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}^{-}:=\bigl\{i\in\mathsf{Q}:\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ii}<0)>0\bigr\}=\bigl\{i\in\mathsf{Q}:\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ii}=-1)>0\bigr\}

(8.1)

denote the set of colours where
(A5′)(b)
but not
(A5′)(a)
applies.
Note that
(A2)
implies that
a_{i}>0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
.

Remark 8.1
.

More generally, we may assume that, for a given
i
,

\xi_{ii}
may take some
negative value
-b
, provided
it does not take any other negative value, and
X_{i}(0)
and all
\xi_{ji}

(
j\in\mathsf{Q}
) a.s. are multiples of
b
.
This case is easily reduced to the case
b=1
in
(A5′)
by dividing
all
X_{ni}
,
X_{i}(t)
, and
\xi_{ji}
by
b
and multiplying the activity

a_{i}
by
b
.
(See Example
14.8
for an example where we, however, use
a slightly different alternative.)
For convenience, and thus without real loss of generality, we will assume

(A5′)
, where the only allowed negative replacement is
-1
.

\triangle

Remark 8.2
.

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}\cap\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
, then
X_{i}(t)

is not influenced by the other colours,
and
(A5′)(b)
implies that

X_{i}(t)
is integer-valued and a classical continuous-time
Markov branching process of the
type studied in e.g.
[
4
, Chapter III]
.
(As noted above, we have
a_{i}>0
by
(A2)
.)
Recall that
[
4
, Section III.7]

in the subcritical and critical cases
\lambda_{i}\leqslant 0
,
this process a.s. dies out, i.e.,
X_{i}(t)=0
for all sufficiently large
t
,
while in the supercritical case
\lambda_{i}>0
,
the process survives for ever with positive probability
(assuming
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}\log\xi_{ii}<\infty
, as we do);
this probability
is strictly less than 1, since the process always may die out when

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ii}=-1)>0
.

\triangle

We continue to use the definitions above, in particular
(
2.7
)–(
2.13
).
Note that we now may have
r_{ii}<0
, and thus
\lambda_{i}<0
;
we may also have
\lambda_{i}=r_{ii}=0
without
\xi_{ii}=0
a.s.

We will see that the results in the previous sections
are valid with minor changes also if we
replace
(A5)
by
(A5′)
,
at least under some further technical assumptions

(A6)
–
(A8)
given below.
However, in order to get more general results,
these will be assumed only when needed.
Hence,
we assume throughout this section
(A1)
–
(A4)
and
(A5′)
, with
the following further assumptions
added explicitly when needed.

(A6)

\sum_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}a_{i}X_{i}(t)>0
a.s. for all
t\geqslant 0
.

(A7)

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, then
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
.

(A8)

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}
is a minimal colour, then
\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
a.s. (i.e.,
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
).

In other words,
(A7)
says
that if
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
then either
\lambda_{i}>0
or there exists
j\in\mathsf{Q}
with
j\prec i
and
\lambda_{j}>0

(or both).
In particular, if
i
is a minimal colour, then either
\lambda_{i}>0
or

\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
a.s.

Note that
(A8)
implies that
X_{i}(t)\geqslant X_{i}(0)
when
i
is minimal;
since
(A1)
–
(A3)
imply that there exists a minimal
i
with
a_{i}>0

and
X_{i}(0)>0
,
(A8)
together with
(A1)
–
(A3)
imply
(A6)
.

8.1.
Some motivation for
(A6)
–
(A8)

The assumption
(A5′)
implies that the continuous-time urn

\mathbf{X}(t)=(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}

is well-defined for
t\in[0,\infty)
, with
X_{i}(t)\geqslant 0
for all
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
However, the urn might possibly reach a state where
X_{i}(t)=0
for all
i

with
a_{i}>0
, and thus
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{i}(t)=0
; such a state is absorbing and
no more draws will be made, and then the total number of draws is finite
and the discrete-time urn is not well defined for all
n
.
For results on the discrete-time urn, we therefore further assume

(A6)
.
Since
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{i}(t)
is constant between the jumps,

(A6)
implies that waiting times
\widehat{T}_{n+1}-\widehat{T}_{n}
are finite a.s.,
and thus there is a.s. an infinite
number of draws; hence
\widehat{T}_{n}<\infty
for every
n
, and the discrete-time
urn
(\mathbf{X}_{n})_{1}^{\infty}
is well defined by (
2.19
).

Next, we note that a colour
i
with
\lambda^{*}_{i}<0
will die out:

Lemma 8.3
.

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}<0
, then a.s.
X_{i}(t)=0
for all large
t
, and thus

X_{ni}=0
for all large
n
.

Proof.

Since every
j\to i
has
\lambda^{*}_{j}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}<0
, we can use induction and
assume that the statement holds for every
j\to i
.
Then there exists a.s. a (random) time
T
such that no colour
j
with

j\to i
exists for times
t\geqslant T
. Hence, for
t\geqslant T
,
X_{i}(t)
evolves
as a single colour urn with only colour
i
;
we have
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}<0
, and thus, as in Remark
8.2
,
this process is a subcritical Markov branching process, which dies out a.s.
∎

Hence, if
\lambda^{*}_{i}<0
, we can wait until colour
i
and all its ancestors have
disappeared, and we may then regard the urn as restarted at that time;
then we have an urn with fewer colours. Consequently, we may without loss of
generality assume
\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant 0
for every colour
i
.
Note that it follows from the definition (
2.8
) that

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant 0\text{ for every colour }i\iff\lambda_{j}\geqslant 0\text{ for every minimal colour }j.

(8.2)

Moreover, also the case
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
may be problematic when
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
and we will actually make a stronger assumption than (
8.2
). There are
two reasons.

First, suppose that
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
is a minimal colour (and thus
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
).
Then, as said in Remark
8.2
,

X_{i}(t)
is a Markov branching process which dies out also in the critical case

\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
,
so as above we may in this case
wait until colour
i
has disappeared and consider
an urn with fewer colours.

Secondly, and more importantly,
if
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
is not minimal and
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, there are examples where
(after normalization)

X_{i}(t)
converges in distribution but
not
a.s.,
and similarly for
X_{ni}
; see Examples
14.14

and
14.15
.
(In Example
14.15
,
X_{ni}
does not even converge in distribution.)

We therefore exclude these cases and assume
(A7)
.
Note that if
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, then
\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant\lambda_{i}\geqslant 0
.
Hence,
(A7)
implies that

\lambda^{*}_{i}\geqslant 0
for every
i
, and thus
both conditions in (
8.2
) hold.
(So we do not have to assume this explicitly.)

Even with these assumptions,
one complication remains.
If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
is a minimal colour, then, as noted in Remark
8.2
,
X_{i}(t)
is a
branching process, and even in the supercritical case
\lambda_{i}>0
,
it dies out with positive probability.
We will accept this complication, but note that it may be eliminated by
the additional assumption
(A8)
, which we only assume when needed.

8.2.
Main results for urns with subtractions

With the assumptions above, our main theorems for discrete and continuous
time still hold:

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a discrete-time
triangular Pólya urn satisfying the
conditions
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
–
(A8)
.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.8
hold.

We do not explicitly assume
(A6)
in Theorem
8.4
, but it is implicit
since it follows from
(A8)
and the other assumptions, as noted above.

Without
(A8)
, we get a more complicated partial result
(where
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is as in Section
5
); it is particularly useful
in cases where
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s. for some other reason, for example by
Lemma
10.9
below in the balanced case.
(See Example
14.8
.)

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a discrete-time
triangular Pólya urn satisfying the
conditions
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.8
hold
a.s. on the event
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0\}

(which has positive probability),
except that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=0
is possible,
with
0\leqslant\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=0\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0)<1
.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a continuous-time
triangular Pólya urn satisfying the
conditions
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
4.1
hold,
except that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
is possible,
with
0\leqslant\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0\mid{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0)<1
;
moreover, we have
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0\;\forall i\in\mathsf{Q})>0
.

If furthermore
(A8)
holds, then the all conclusions of Theorem
4.1
hold.

Remark 8.7
.

In Theorems
8.5
and
8.6
, if the limit
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=0
or
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
, then
the result (
1.3
), (
1.4
) or (
4.1
) does not give the
correct growth of
X_{ni}
or
X_{i}(t)
. In such cases, it might be
possible to find more precise results using our methods on suitable
subsets of the colours;
it seems that this can be done very generally, at least on a
case-by-case basis. We give one example in Example
14.13
but do not attempt
to state any general theorem.

\triangle

Remark 8.8
.

Also the results in Section
6
–
7
still hold
(with the same proofs)
under the assumptions
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
–
(A8)
, as the reader might verify.

If we do not assume
(A8)
and assume only
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
, then the results on
absolute continuity do not hold as stated, since typically the limits may be
0 with positive probability. (We conjecture that these results might hold
conditioned on the variables being non-zero, but we have not pursued this.)
The remaining results in Section
6
–
7
still hold.

\triangle

Note that
(A5)
implies
(A5′)
,
(A7)
, and
(A8)

(with
\mathsf{Q}^{-}=\emptyset
), and thus
(A1)
–
(A5)
imply also
(A6)
;
hence these theorems (strictly) extend Theorems
1.8
and
4.1
.

We prove Theorems
8.4
–
8.6
in the remainder of this section.
As in Section
3
, we first study a single colour, and as there we split the
discussion into several subsections.

First note that we used that
X_{j}(t)
is
increasing in Lemma
3.1
and its proof.
This is no longer necessarily true, but
we may replace
X_{j}(t)
by
X^{*}_{j}(t)
with no further
consequences. Hence, Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
hold with the assumption
modified to
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{j}^{*}(t)<\infty
.

8.3.
A colour not influenced by others

Consider the situation in Section
3.1
, where
\xi_{ji}=0
for
j\neq i
,
i.e.,
i\in\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
.
If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
then
X_{i}(t)
is, as noted in Remark
8.2
, a Markov branching process.

Lemma
3.3
extends to this case, with some modifications.

Lemma 8.9
.

Suppose that
(A1)
–
(A4)
and
(A5′)
hold and that
i\in\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
.

(a)

If

i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
(i.e.,
\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
a.s.),
then Lemma
3.3
still holds.

(b)

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
then Lemma
3.3
holds with the following modifications:

(i)

If
\lambda_{i}>0
,
the only difference is that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
is possible with positive
probability. If
x_{0}>0
, then
0<\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)<1
.

(ii)

If
\lambda_{i}=0
, then
X_{i}(t)
is a martingale with

\displaystyle X_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0,\qquad\text{as ${t\to\infty}$},

(8.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[X_{i}(t)\bigr]

\displaystyle=x_{0},

(8.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\bigl[X_{i}(t)\bigr]

\displaystyle=Cx_{0}t,

(8.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl(\sup_{0\leqslant t\leqslant u}X_{i}(t)\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cx_{0}^{2}+Cx_{0}u,\qquad\text{ for every $u<\infty$}.

(8.6)

Furthermore, for every
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl(\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\{e^{-\delta t}X_{i}(t)\}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle<\infty.

(8.7)

(iii)

If
\lambda_{i}<0
, then

\displaystyle X_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0,\qquad\text{as ${t\to\infty}$},

(8.8)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\bigl[X_{i}(t)\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant Cx_{0}e^{\lambda_{i}t},

(8.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl(\sup_{0\leqslant t<\infty}X_{i}(t)\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant Cx_{0}^{2}.

(8.10)

Proof.

(a)
:
We thus assume
(A5)
=
(A5′)(a)
for the colour
i
,
and then the proof of Lemma
3.3
still holds.
(Recall that for simplicity we had

(A1)
–
(A5)
as standing assumptions in Section
3
,
including
(A5)
for all colours;
however,
as remarked before Lemma
3.3
,
this is needed only for the colour
i
in
that subsection, and in particular for Lemma
3.3
and its proof.)

(b)
:
Now assume
(A5′)(b)
.
By
(A2)
, we also have
a_{i}>0
.

We argue as in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, with the following differences.
Note that
x_{0}
is assumed to be an integer,
and the case
x_{0}=0
is trivial; we thus may assume
x_{0}\geqslant 1
.
It is still true that
M(t):=e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)
is a martingale, and
(
3.20
) holds if
\lambda_{i}\neq 0
.

Now, however, as said in Remark
8.2
, with positive probability,

X_{i}(t)=0
for all large
t
;
moreover, in the critical and subcritical cases
(ii)
and
(iii)
,
this happens a.s.

We study the three cases separately:

(i)
:
The proof of Lemma
3.3
still holds, except for the final part yielding
(
3.25
).

By Remark
8.2
,
with positive probability

X_{i}(t)
dies out and thus
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
.
Moreover,
by (
3.14
),

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=x_{0}>0
.
Consequently,
0<\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)<1
.

(ii)
:
By Remark
8.2
,
a.s.
X_{i}(t)=0
for all large
t
, which gives (
8.3
).

For
\lambda_{i}=0
, (
3.12
) says that

X_{i}(t)=M(t)
is a martingale, which implies (
8.4
).
Furthermore, (
3.20
) in this case yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{i}(t)^{2}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}M(t)^{2}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}=x_{0}^{2}+a_{i}\beta x_{0}t,

(8.11)

which gives (
8.5
) and, together with Doob’s inequality, (
8.6
).
To prove (
8.7
), we note that (
8.6
) implies

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\Bigl(\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\{e^{-\delta t}X_{i}(t)\}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-2\delta n}\Bigl(\sup_{n\leqslant t\leqslant n+1}X_{i}(t)\Bigr)^{2}\leqslant\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-2\delta n}O(n+1)<\infty.

(8.12)

(iii)
:
As for
(ii)
,
Remark
8.2
shows that
a.s.
X_{i}(t)=0
for all large
t
, and thus (
8.8
) holds.
Since
\lambda_{i}<0
, we obtain from (
3.20
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}M(t)^{2}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[M,M]_{t}\leqslant x_{0}^{2}+Cx_{0}e^{-\lambda_{i}t}

(8.13)

and thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}\bigl[X_{i}(t)\bigr]=e^{2\lambda_{i}t}\operatorname{Var}\bigl[M(t)\bigr]\leqslant Cx_{0}e^{\lambda_{i}t},

(8.14)

showing (
8.9
). Furthermore, (
8.13
) and Doob’s inequality
yield, for any
m\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[\sup_{m-1\leqslant t\leqslant m}X_{i}(t)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{2\lambda_{i}(m-1)}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[\sup_{m-1\leqslant t\leqslant m}M(t)^{2}\bigr]\leqslant Ce^{2\lambda_{i}m}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[M(m)^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant Ce^{\lambda_{i}m}x_{0}^{2}.

(8.15)

Since
\lambda_{i}<0
, the sum over all
m\geqslant 1
is
\leqslant Cx_{0}^{2}
, which implies
(
8.10
).
∎

Remark 8.10
.

In fact, it follows from
[
4
, Theorem III.7.2]
(using
(A4)
) that
if
i\in\mathsf{Q}_{\min}\cap\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, then

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
occurs a.s. exactly when

X_{i}(t)=0
for large
t
so that the branching process dies out in finite time.
Thus,
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)
equals the
probability that the continuous-time branching process
X_{i}(t)
dies out.
Considering only the times that a ball of colour
i
is drawn, we obtain an
embedded
random walk with i.i.d. increments distributed as
\xi_{ii}
;
thus
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)
also equals the probability that this random walk hits 0.
(It is easy to see that this also equals the probability of extinction for a
Galton–Watson process with offspring distribution
1+\xi_{ii}
started with
x_{0}
individuals.)

\triangle

8.4.
A colour only produced by one other colour

Consider the situation in Section
3.2
, where
there is a single colour
j
such that
j\to i
.

We used that
X_{j}(t)
is increasing in (
3.56
);
this is no longer necessarily true, but
we may replace
X_{j}(t)
by
X^{*}_{j}(t)
there with no further
consequences.

Lemma
3.5
holds with the following minor modifications.
We assume
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
, since otherwise
j
eventually becomes extinct
by Lemma
8.3
,
and after that
X_{i}(t)
evolves as in Lemma
8.9
.
(We are not really interested in this case, as discussed earlier.)
Note that we also exclude the case
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
.
(For good reasons, see Examples
14.14
and
14.15
).

Lemma 8.11
.

Suppose that
(A1)
–
(A4)
and
(A5′)
hold. Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
, and
suppose that
there is exactly one colour
j\in\mathsf{Q}
such that
j\to i
,
and that
X_{i}(0)=0
.
Suppose also that one of the following holds:

(a)

i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
(i.e.,
\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
a.s.), and
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
.

(b)

i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, and

\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
.

Then Lemma
3.5
still holds, i.e.,
if
(
3.31
)–(
3.32
) hold,
then we have the conclusions
(
3.33
)–(
3.35
) and
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0\implies{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s.

Proof.

(a)
:
Recall again that we had

(A1)
–
(A5)
as standing assumptions in Section
3
,
including
(A5)
for all colours;
however, it is easily verified that
the proof of Lemma
3.5
does not use this for other
colours than
i
, except
in (
3.56
), where we now replace
X_{j}(t)
by
X^{*}_{j}(t)
as discussed
above, and to see that
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
.
Hence, in the present setting where we assume
(A1)
–
(A4)
and
(A5′)
, and also explicitly
assume
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
,
if
\xi_{ii}\geqslant 0
a.s.,
then the proof of Lemma
3.5
still holds.

(b)
:
We now assume
(A5′)(b)
for
i
.
Most of the proof of Lemma
3.5
remains the same.
The main difference in the proof comes in
Steps
3.2
–
3.2
, where
we used Lemma
3.3
in (
3.2
), but now instead use Lemma
8.9
(b)
.
We consider three cases:

(1)

\lambda_{i}>0
: Then (
3.2
)
still holds by Lemma
8.9
,
and thus all estimates
in Steps
3.2
–
3.2
hold.

(2)

\lambda_{i}=0
: Then (
3.2
) is replaced by, using (
8.5
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}\mid(T_{k},\eta_{k})_{1}^{\infty}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}\operatorname{Var}\bigl[Y_{k}(t-T_{k})\mid T_{k},\eta_{k}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}C(t-T_{k})\eta_{k}.

(8.16)

Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle=C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}(t-T_{k})\leqslant Ct\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t).

(8.17)

Using Lemma
3.2
(or Lemma
3.1
),
it follows that (
3.68
) for
\ell=1
is replaced by (with
\lambda_{i}=0
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant Ct\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}t\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(8.18)

with an extra factor
t
.
We assume
0<\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\lor\lambda^{*}_{j}
and
\lambda_{i}=0
, and thus

\lambda^{*}_{j}>0=\lambda_{i}
, so we are in Case
3.2
of Step
3.2
;
(
3.2
) now holds with an insignificant extra factor
n

and thus (
3.72
)–(
3.73
) still hold and the conclusions
of Step
3.2

remain the same.

(3)

\lambda_{i}<0
: Then we obtain instead of (
3.2
), now using
(
8.9
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl[[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}\mid(T_{k},\eta_{k})_{1}^{\infty}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-2\lambda_{i}T_{k}}Ce^{-\lambda_{i}(t-T_{k})}\eta_{k}

\displaystyle=C\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}t-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\eta_{k}.

(8.19)

Thus, by Lemma
3.2
(ii)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}e^{-\lambda_{i}t-\lambda_{i}T_{k}}\eta_{k}=Ce^{-\lambda_{i}t}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\lambda_{i}s}X_{j}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(8.20)

It follows that (
3.68
) for
\ell=1
is replaced by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}e^{-\lambda_{i}t}\int_{0}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(8.21)

However,
for
t\leqslant 1
, (
8.21
) is trivially equivalent to (
3.68
),
and for
t\geqslant 1
, (
8.21
) implies,
using
\lambda^{*}_{j}-\lambda_{i}\geqslant-\lambda_{i}>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[Z_{1},Z_{1}]_{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}(t+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})t}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{j}\int_{t-1}^{t}(s+1)^{\kappa_{j}}e^{(\lambda^{*}_{j}-2\lambda_{i})s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(8.22)

Hence (
3.68
) holds for all
\ell\leqslant 3
also in the present setting.
By our assumption
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\lor\lambda^{*}_{j}>0
we have
\lambda^{*}_{j}>0
,
so we are again in Case
3.2
of Step
3.2
;
(
3.2
)–(
3.73
) still hold and the conclusions
of Step
3.2

remain the same.

In all cases, the conclusions (
3.33
)–(
3.35
) of Step
3.2

follow as for Lemma
3.5
.

Finally, as for Lemma
3.5
, if
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{j}
, then (
3.35
) shows that

{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0\implies{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
. If
\lambda_{i}>\lambda^{*}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
, then we use again
(
3.78
), where still a.s. all
T_{k}
are finite.
Let
{\mathcal{Y}}_{k}:=\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\lambda_{i}t}Y_{k}(t)
.
In contrast to Lemma
3.5
,
{\mathcal{Y}}_{k}=0
is now possible also if
\eta_{k}>0
, but

0<\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{Y}}_{k}=0)<1
by Lemma
8.9
. We now let
K
be the smallest
k
such that

{\mathcal{Y}}_{k}>0
, and conclude as in Section
3
that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
.
∎

Remark 8.12
.

In Lemma
8.11
(b)

we have excluded the case

\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
, since then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
. In this case
we are in Case
3.2
of Step
3.2

of the proof of Lemma
3.5
.
As mentioned above, there is now an extra factor
t
in (
3.68
), and
therefore
we obtain instead of (
3.75
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\widetilde{Z}}^{\ddagger}_{\ell}(n)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{C}2^{-\kappa_{j}n}.

(8.23)

Hence, if furthermore
\kappa_{j}\geqslant 1
, then (
3.76
) still holds, and the rest
of the proof of Lemma
3.5
applies.
Consequently, Lemma
8.11
(b)
holds also in the case
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
and

\kappa_{i}\geqslant 1
.

Example
14.14
gives an example with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0=\kappa_{i}

where the conclusion of Lemma
8.11
does not hold.
For simplicity we have in Lemma
8.11
(b)
excluded all cases
with
\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
(and thus
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
).

Similarly, Example
14.15

gives an example with
\lambda_{i}<0=\lambda^{*}_{j}=\kappa_{i}

where the conclusion of Lemma
8.11
does not hold.
We have excluded all cases
with
\lambda_{i}<\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
; it seems likely that Lemma
8.11
(b)

might hold also in
this case if
\kappa_{i}\geqslant 1
, but we have not investigated this further.

\triangle

We need also a coarse estimate in the case excluded in Lemma
8.11
(b)
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose that
(A1)
–
(A4)
and
(A5′)
hold and that
i\in\mathsf{Q}
is such that

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{ii}=-1)>0
.
Assume that
there is exactly one colour
j\in\mathsf{Q}
such that
j\to i
,
and that
X_{i}(0)=0
.
Assume also that
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
.

Assume further that
(
3.31
)–(
3.32
) hold.
Then, for every
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle e^{-\delta t}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}0,

(8.24)

and

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\bigl\{e^{-\delta t}X_{i}(t)\bigr\}\Bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(8.25)

Proof.

We have
\lambda_{i}\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
and, by
(A2)
,
a_{i}>0
.
Thus
r_{ii}=\lambda_{i}/a_{i}\leqslant 0
.
Furthermore,
\xi_{ii}\geqslant-1
a.s., and thus

r_{ii}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}\geqslant-1
.
Choose
p\in(0,1)
such that
0<r_{ii}+2p<\delta/a_{i}
.

Modify the urn by increasing
\xi_{ii}
to
\widecheck{\xi}_{ii}:=\xi_{ii}+2\zeta
,
where
\zeta\in\operatorname{Be}(p)

is independent of

\xi_{ii}
;
all other
\xi_{k\ell}
and initial conditions
X_{k}(0)
remain the same.
We denote quantities for the new urn by adding
\widecheck{\ }
.
The modification does not affect any ancestors of
i
; thus

\widecheck{X}_{j}(t)=X_{j}(t)
and
\widecheck{\lambda}^{*}_{j}=\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
.
On the other hand, by our choice of
p
,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}=a_{i}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widecheck{\xi}_{ii}=a_{i}(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}+2\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\zeta)=a_{i}(r_{ii}+2p)\in(0,\delta).

(8.26)

Consequently,
\widecheck{\lambda}^{*}_{i}=\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}\lor\widecheck{\lambda}^{*}_{j}=\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}>0

and thus Lemma
8.11
(b)
applies to the modified urn.
(The other conditions of Lemma
8.11
(b)
obviously hold.)

The modification adds extra balls of colour
i
, and these may get
descendants of colour
i
and they may disappear again, but we may separate
these extra balls of colour
i
from the original ones, and thus couple the
two versions such that
\widecheck{X}_{i}(t)\geqslant X_{i}(t)
for all
t\geqslant 1
.
Consequently, noting that

\widecheck{\lambda}^{*}_{i}=\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}
and
\widecheck{\kappa}_{i}=0
,
Lemma
8.11
yields, from (
3.33
)–(
3.34
) and (
3.29
)–(
3.30
),

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to\infty}e^{-\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\leqslant\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}t}\widecheck{X}_{i}(t)=\widecheck{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}<\infty\quad\text{a.s.},

(8.27)

and

\displaystyle\Bigl\lVert\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\bigl\{e^{-\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\bigr\}\Bigr\rVert_{2}\leqslant\Bigl\lVert\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\bigl\{e^{-\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}t}\widecheck{X}_{i}(t)\bigr\}\Bigr\rVert_{2}<\infty.

(8.28)

The results follow, since
\widecheck{\lambda}_{i}<\delta
by (
8.26
).
∎

8.5.
The general case for a single colour

Lemma 8.14
.

Suppose that
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
hold.
Then Lemma
3.7
still holds,
except for the last sentence.

The last sentence remains valid if
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, and also if
i\notin\mathsf{Q}_{\min}
.
(In particular, it is valid if
(A8)
holds.)
In the remaining case, when
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
and is minimal, then
0<\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0)<1
.

Proof.

Recall that the assumption
(A7)
implies that
\lambda^{*}_{j}\geqslant 0
for every

j\in\mathsf{Q}
.

We follow the proof of Lemma
3.7
and split the colour
i
into subcolours

i_{0}
and
i_{j}
,
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}

(also if
i
is minimal so
\mathsf{P}_{i}=\emptyset
);
then (
3.84
) holds, and also
(
3.85
)–(
3.89
) and (
3.91
).

If
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, then the proof is exactly as for Lemma
3.7
, now using
Lemmas
8.9
(a)
and
8.11
(a)

instead of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
.

Assume thus in the sequel of the proof that instead

i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
.
(Thus,
(A5′)(b)
holds.)
Then
by
(A7)
, we have
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
.
However, it is possible that
\lambda_{i_{0}}=\lambda_{i}\leqslant 0
or that

\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}=\lambda_{i}\lor\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
for some
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
.

For
i_{0}
, we now apply Lemma
8.9
(b)
.
If
\lambda_{i}>0
, then
Lemma
8.9
(b)
(i)

shows that
X_{i_{0}}
can be treated as before in (
3.89
) and (
3.91
).
If
\lambda_{i}\leqslant 0
, we replace, as we may,
{\widetilde{X}}_{i_{0}}(t)
by
e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i_{0}}(t)

in the estimate (
3.89
), cf. (
3.84
) and (
3.29
).
By (
8.3
) and (
8.8
), this term contributes 0 to the limit
(
3.82
).
Similarly, in (
3.91
) we replace
\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i_{0}}

by
\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\{e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i_{0}}(t)\}
,
which gives a finite contribution to (
3.83
) by
(
8.7
) and (
8.10
).

Similarly,
for each
i_{j}
(
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
), we now apply
Lemma
8.11
(b)
if
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}>0
;
otherwise, i.e., if
\lambda_{i}\leqslant 0
and
\lambda^{*}_{j}=0
,
we apply Lemma
8.13
(with
\delta:=\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
).
In the latter case we replace

{\widetilde{X}}_{i_{j}}(t)
by
e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i_{j}}(t)

in the estimate (
3.89
) and use (
8.24
),
and we replace

\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i_{j}}
by
\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\{e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i_{j}}(t)\}

in (
3.91
) and use (
8.25
).

This proves, in all cases, that (
3.82
) and (
3.83
) hold.
Moreover, for each
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}\cup\{0\}
such that we apply
Lemma
8.9
(b)
(ii)
,
(iii)
or Lemma
8.13
to
i_{j}
,
the contribution to the limit (
3.82
) is 0,
and so is the contribution to the right-hand side of (
3.90
) since
in these cases
\lambda^{*}_{i_{j}}\leqslant 0<\lambda^{*}_{i}
by (
3.85
)–(
3.86
).
Hence, (
3.90
) still holds.

It remains to consider the final sentence in Lemma
3.7
.
First, if
\mathsf{P}_{i}\neq\emptyset
,
then it follows as in the proof of Lemma
3.7
that if

{\mathcal{X}}_{j}>0
for every
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
, then
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
.
In the remaining case,
i
is minimal.
Note that then
X_{i}(0)>0
by
(A3)
.
If
i
is minimal and
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{-}
,
then a.s.
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
by Lemma
3.3
.
On the other hand,
if
i
is minimal and
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{-}
, then
Lemma
8.9
(b)
(i)

shows that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}({\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0)\in(0,1)
.
∎

8.6.
Proofs of Theorems
8.4
–
8.6

Proof of Theorem
8.6
.

As in Section
4
, now using Lemma
8.14
; we use again induction on the colour

i
and conclude that (
3.82
) and (
3.83
) hold for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
although now
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
is possible.

The processes
X_{i}(t)
for
i\in\mathsf{Q}_{\min}

are independent, and thus so are their limits
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
.
Each is strictly positive with positive probability, by
Lemma
8.9
or
8.14
, and thus
there is a positive probability that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
for every minimal
i
.
Moreover, if
(A8)
holds, then this probability is 1.
Finally, it follows by Lemma
8.14
by induction on the colour
i

that a.s. if
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
for every minimal
i
, then

{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
for every
i
.
∎

Proof of Theorems
8.4
and
8.5
.

As in Section
5
, now using Theorem
8.6
and considering only the event

\{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0\}
.
Note that Theorem
8.6
, applied to the extended urn, shows that
with positive probability
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
for all
i\in\mathsf{Q}
;
furthermore, in Theorem
8.4
, where we assume
(A8)
, this holds a.s.

Note also that since we assume
(A6)
, we have a.s.
\widehat{T}_{n}<\infty
for
every
n
, and thus the
discrete-time process
\mathbf{X}_{n}
is well-defined.
∎

9.
Random vs non-random replacements

Consider a Pólya urn
{\mathcal{U}}

with a random replacement matrix
(\xi_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
.
For simplicity we study only the case when all
\xi_{ij}\geqslant 0
, and we thus
assume
(A1)
–
(A5)
.
Consider also another urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
with the same colours
\mathsf{Q}
,
the same initial vector
\mathbf{X}_{0}
,
and the same
activities
a_{i}
, but non-random replacements
r_{ij}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}
.
We thus replace the replacement matrix by its mean, and we may call
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}

the
mean urn
corresponding to
{\mathcal{U}}
.
Both urns have the same
r_{ij}
, and thus the same colour graph,
\lambda_{i}
,

\lambda^{*}_{i}
,
\kappa_{i}
,
\widehat{\lambda}
,
\widehat{\kappa}
and
\gamma_{i}
,
see (
2.3
) and (
2.7
)–(
2.13
).
The new urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}

also satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
, and Theorems
1.8
and
4.1
show that we
have the same qualitative asymptotic behaviour for both urns, with the same
normalization factors. However,
the limits
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
are in
general
not
the same for the two urns

{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
,
and thus the asymptotic
distributions may be be different; see Example
14.5
.

We note that the constants
c_{ik}
in Section
6
by Lemma
6.7
are the same
for the two urns.
This yields one simple case. (We assume
\widehat{\lambda}>0
for simplicity, and leave a
study of the case
\widehat{\lambda}=0
to the reader.)

Theorem 9.1
.

Assume
\widehat{\lambda}>0
.
If
i
is a colour such that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is deterministic for one of the two
urns, then
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is the same constant for both urns.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.1
, there are two cases: either
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, or
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
.

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
,
then (
6.5
) and
Lemma
6.2
show that
for every
j\in\mathsf{A}_{i}
,

{\mathcal{X}}_{j}=X_{j}(0)
is a constant, the same for both urns.
Hence, Lemmas
6.1
shows,
since the constants
c_{ik}
are the same for both urns,
that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is the same constant for both urns.
Consequently, (
5.12
) shows that

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\kappa_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
is the same constant for both urns.

Assume now
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}
. If we add dummy balls to both urns as in
Section
5
, then, by Lemma
6.1
and (
5.2
), for any of the urns,
both
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
are linear combinations

\sum_{j\in J}c_{ij}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
and
\sum_{j\in J}c_{0j}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}
,
where
J
is the set of leaders with
\lambda^{*}_{j}=\widehat{\lambda}
.
The joint distribution of
({\mathcal{X}}_{j})_{\in J}
is absolutely continuous by Theorem
6.4
, and
since (
5.12
) yields
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-\gamma_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}/{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
,
it follows that if
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is deterministic in one of the two urns, then
the vectors
(c_{ij})_{j\in J}
and
(c_{0j})_{j\in J}
are proportional.
Since these vectors are the same for both urns, it follows that then

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
is the same constant in both urns.
∎

10.
Balanced urns

Many papers on Pólya urns assume that urn is balanced (as defined below);
while this is quite restrictive, it is partly justified by the fact that
Pólya urns that appear in applications often are balanced.
We have not needed this assumption in the preceding sections, but it will be
used for some results in the following sections (in particular
Section
12
).
The present section contains the definition and some
simple results for later use.

In the standard case when all activities
a_{i}=1
, a Pólya urn is said to
be
balanced
if we add the same total number of balls at each draw;
in other words, if the replacement matrix
(\xi_{ij})_{i,j\in\mathsf{Q}}
satisfies

\sum_{j}\xi_{ij}=b
a.s., for some
b\in\mathbb{R}
and every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
In general, with possibly different activities
a_{i}
, it is not the total
number of balls but their total activity that is important, and we make the
following general definition.
(Definition
10.1
and Lemma
10.2
– Remark
10.6

below apply to all Pólya urns, triangular or not.)

Definition 10.1
.

A Pólya urn is
balanced
if there exists a constant
b\in\mathbb{R}

(called the
balance
) such that for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
with
a_{i}>0
, we
have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}a_{j}\xi_{ij}=b\qquad\text{a.s.}

(10.1)

With
\mathbf{a}:=(a_{j})_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}
, the vector of activities, (
10.1
) may be
written
\mathbf{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}=b
for all
i
with
a_{i}>0
.

Suppose that the urn is balanced as in Definition
10.1
.
It follows from (
1.2
) (since colours
i
with
a_{i}=0
will never be drawn)
that a.s.

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{n}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}a_{j}X_{nj}=\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{0}+nb.

(10.2)

Hence, the denominator in (
1.1
) is deterministic.
This has been used in several ways in many papers by different authors.
(In particular, it makes it possible to use martingale methods
for the discrete-time urn,
similar to the continuous-time arguments in the present paper;
see Remark
1.11
and e.g.
[
12
]
.)
Here we note some simple consequences.

Note first that if the balance
b<0
, then (
10.2
) shows that
the activity
\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{n}
becomes negative for large
n
; this is clearly
impossible and shows that the discrete-time urn process must stop and cannot be
continued for ever. In the present paper, we are not interested in this
case, so we must have
b\geqslant 0
, and we thus assume this
in the sequel (whether it is said explicitly or not).
In this case, there are no problems.

Lemma 10.2
.

A balanced urn with balance
b\geqslant 0
which satisfies
(A1)

and
(A5)
or (more generally)
(A5′)
is well-defined for all
discrete or continuous times, and thus satisfies
(A6)
.

Proof.

By (
10.2
), we have, for every
n
, a.s.

\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{n}\geqslant\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{0}>0
.
Hence the discrete-time process
\mathbf{X}_{n}
is well-defined, and also

\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}(t)>0
for every
t
.
∎

We assume
(A1)
and
(A5)
or
(A5′)
below; thus
(A6)
holds.

Remark 10.3
.

The case
b=0
is trivial. First, if
(A5)
holds, so there are no
subtractions, then the only possibility is
\xi_{ij}=0
a.s. for all

i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
; in other words, we draw from the urn but nothing happens.
If we allow subtractions as in
(A5′)(b)
, there may be a few
initial draws that wipe out some colours, but nothing more will happen;
furthermore, such urns violate
(A7)
.

\triangle

Lemma 10.4
.

If a Pólya urn is balanced, then the random processes
(\mathbf{X}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
and

(\widehat{T}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
are independent.

Proof.

Let
n\geqslant 0
.
Stop the continuous-time process
\mathbf{X}(t)
at
\widehat{T}_{n}
, i.e., at the
n
th draw.
Conditionally on everything that has happened so far,
the waiting time
\widehat{T}_{n+1}-\widehat{T}_{n}
until the next draw is an exponential
random variable with rate
\sum_{i}a_{i}X_{ni}
, which by (
10.2
) is the
constant
\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{0}+nb
.
Hence,
\widehat{T}_{n+1}-\widehat{T}_{n}
is independent of
\mathbf{X}_{n}
,
and thus also of the colour of the drawn ball, say
i
,
and of
\mathbf{X}_{n+1}-\mathbf{X}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}^{(n)}
, recall (
1.2
).
Consequently, the processes
(\widehat{T}_{n})_{n}
and
(\mathbf{X}_{n})_{n}
evolve independently.
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

If a Pólya urn is balanced, then the
distribution of the random sequence
(\widehat{T}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}

depends only on the balance
b
and the initial activity
\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{0}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
10.4
,
\widehat{T}_{n+1}-\widehat{T}_{n}
is exponential distributed
with rate
\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{0}+nb
, and these waiting times are independent.
∎

Remark 10.6
.

The distribution of
(\widehat{T}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is thus the same as for the single-colour
urn with initial value
x_{0}:=\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{o}
, activity
a_{1}=1
, and
replacement matrix
(b)
.
For
b>0
, this is the more or less trivial case
q=1
of
Example
14.1
, and it is well known that
(b\widehat{T}_{n})_{1}^{\infty}

are the jump times of a Yule process started with
x_{0}/b
individuals.

\triangle

In the remaining part of the section we consider, as in the rest of the paper,
triangular urns.

Lemma 10.7
.

Consider a triangular balanced urn with balance
b\geqslant 0
,
and suppose that the urn satisfies
(A1)
–
(A4)
.
Then
\widehat{\lambda}=b
.
Furthermore, if
b>0
, then
\widehat{\kappa}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}:=\{i\in\mathsf{Q}:a_{i}>0\}
.
Then
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
by
(A1)
.
There are three possibilities for a colour
i\in\mathsf{Q}
:

(i)

If
i\notin\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
, then
a_{i}=0
and thus
\lambda_{i}=0
.

(ii)

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
and
i
is maximal in
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
for the order
\prec
, i.e.,

i\not\to j
for every
j\in\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
,
then
\xi_{ij}=0
a.s. when
j\in\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}\setminus\{i\}
, and

a_{j}=0
for every
j\notin\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
; hence
a_{j}\xi_{ij}=0
a.s. for all colours

j\neq i
. Consequently,
(
10.1
) yields

\displaystyle a_{i}\xi_{ii}=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{Q}}a_{j}\xi_{ij}=b

(10.3)

a.s., and thus, taking the expectation,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=a_{i}r_{ii}=a_{i}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}=b.

(10.4)

(iii)

If
i\in\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
is not maximal in
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
, then there exists
j\in\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
with

i\to j
and thus
r_{ij}>0
.
Taking expectations in (
10.1
) yields

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=a_{i}r_{ii}=b-\sum_{j\neq i}a_{j}r_{ij}\leqslant b-a_{j}r_{ij}<b.

(10.5)

It follows that
\widehat{\lambda}:=\max_{i}\lambda_{i}=b
.
Furthermore, if
b>0
, then the maximum is attained precisely in Case

(ii)
, i.e., for
i
that are maximal in
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
.
It follows that for every such
i
we have
\lambda_{j}<b
for all
j\in\mathsf{P}_{i}
,
and thus
\kappa_{i}=0
. Hence,
\widehat{\kappa}=0
.
∎

Remark 10.8
.

The proof shows also that
if a balanced triangular urn has
b>0
and all activites
a_{i}=1
,
then
\xi_{ii}=r_{ii}=\lambda_{i}=b
a.s. for every
i
that is maximal in
\mathsf{Q}^{\prime}
,
but

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii}=r_{ii}=\lambda_{i}<b
for all other colours
i
.

\triangle

For urns with subtractions as in Section
8
, there are further
simplifications when the urn is balanced.

Lemma 10.9
.

Consider a triangular balanced urn with balance
b\geqslant 0
,
and suppose that the
urn satisfies
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
.
Then, with notation as in Section
5
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s.

Proof.

The case
b=0
is trivial by Remark
10.3
, so we may assume
b>0
.

For convenience, denote the urn by
{\mathcal{U}}
.
Lemma
10.5
and Remark
10.6
show that
\widehat{T}_{n}
are (jointly) distributed as
for the one-colour urn
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
with replacement matrix
(b)
,
activity
a=1
, and the same
initial total activity. Hence we may couple the urns such that they have the
same
\widehat{T}_{n}
. (Or we may simply define the content of

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
as being the total activity in the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
.)
The urn
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
obviously satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
, and thus (
5.5
) applies to
it, with
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s. Moreover, (
5.5
) applies also to the original
urn
{\mathcal{U}}
by the same argument in Section
5

(as in the proof of Theorems
8.4
and
8.5
in Section
8.6
);
note that the two urns
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
have the same
\widehat{\lambda}=b
and

\kappa_{0}=0
by Lemma
10.7
and (
5.3
).
Consequently, the two urns
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
have the same
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.
∎

In fact, in Lemma
10.9
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
has a Gamma distribution by
the proof and (
14.2
).

11.
The drawn colours

We have so far studied
\mathbf{X}_{n}
and
\mathbf{X}(t)
, the numbers of balls of each
colour in the urn. It is also of interest to study the number of times each
colour is drawn.
(See Examples
14.7
–
14.9
for an application.)
For
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
we denote the number of times that a ball of colour
i
is drawn up to time
n

in the discrete-time urn by
N_{ni}
, and the number of times up to time
t
in
the continuous-time urn by
N_{i}(t)
; thus

\displaystyle N_{ni}=N_{i}(\widehat{T}_{n}),\qquad i\in\mathsf{Q},\;n\geqslant 0.

(11.1)

We state first a continuous-time and then a discrete-time result;
both are similar to the results for
\mathbf{X}(t)
and
\mathbf{X}_{n}
earlier,
but note that exponents change when
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
.
(Proofs are given later in this section.)

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a continuous-time
triangular Pólya urn satisfying
either

(A1)
–
(A5)
, or (more generally)
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
.
Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.

(i)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, then, as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}N_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda^{*}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(11.2)

(ii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, then, as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle t^{-\kappa_{i}-1}N_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\frac{a_{i}}{\kappa_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(11.3)

Furthermore, if
(A5)
or
(A8)
holds,
then
\mathcal{N}_{i}>0
a.s.

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a discrete-time
triangular Pólya urn
and suppose that it satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
.
Alternatively, suppose that the urn satisfies

(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
, and also either satisfies
(A8)
or is balanced.
Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.

(i)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, then as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{ni}}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}\log^{\gamma_{i}}n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}:=\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda^{*}_{i}}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}.

(11.4)

(ii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
and
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{ni}}{\log^{\kappa_{i}+1}n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}:=\frac{a_{i}}{(\kappa_{i}+1)\widehat{\lambda}}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}.

(11.5)

(iii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\widehat{\lambda}=0
, then as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{ni}}{n^{(\kappa_{i}+1)/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}:=\frac{a_{i}}{\kappa_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-1/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}.

(11.6)

Furthermore, if
(A5)
or
(A8)
holds, then
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}>0
a.s.

Remark 11.3
.

If we assume only
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
in Theorem
11.2
, then the results hold on the
event
\{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0\}
, by the same proof and Theorem
8.5
.

\triangle

We can also state the results as the following
simple a.s. limit results for the ratios

N_{ni}/X_{ni}
and
N_{i}(t)/X_{i}(t)
;
in particular, if
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
(the main case), these ratios
converge a.s. to a positive constant.

Theorem 11.4
.

Let
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathsf{Q}}
be a discrete-time
triangular Pólya urn
and suppose that it satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
or (more generally)
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
–
(A8)
.
Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.

(i)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}>0
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{N_{ni}}{X_{ni}}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{N_{i}(t)}{X_{i}(t)}=\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda^{*}_{i}}\qquad\textrm{a.s.}

(11.7)

(ii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, then, as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{i}(t)}{tX_{i}(t)}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{a_{i}}{\kappa_{i}+1}.

(11.8)

(iii)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
and
\widehat{\lambda}>0
, then, as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{ni}}{X_{ni}\log n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{a_{i}}{(\kappa_{i}+1)\widehat{\lambda}}.

(11.9)

(iv)

If
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
and
\widehat{\lambda}=0
, then, as
{n\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{ni}}{X_{ni}n^{1/\kappa_{0}}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{a_{i}}{\kappa_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{-1/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}.

(11.10)

Note that the limit in all cases is a strictly positive constant,
in case
(iv)
by Theorem
6.5
(and Remark
8.8
).

Proof of Theorem
11.1
.

We use (as in the proof of the corresponding result in
[
27
]
)
dummy balls similarly to the proof in Section
5
, but now we use one
dummy ball for each colour.

It suffices to consider one colour at a time, so we fix
i\in\mathsf{Q}
;
we assume
a_{i}>0
since otherwise
N_{ni}=N_{i}(t)=0
a.s. and the results
are trivial.
Denote the urn by
{\mathcal{U}}
.
We consider one new colour, which we denote by
\iota
, and let

\mathsf{Q}^{+}:=\mathsf{Q}\cup\{\iota\}
be the new set of colours.
Balls of colour
\iota
have activity
a_{\iota}:=0
and are thus never drawn,
and we let
\xi_{\iota j}:=0
for all
j\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
;
we further let
\xi_{i\iota}:=1
and
\xi_{j\iota}:=0
for every
j\neq i
,
and we start with
X_{0\iota}:=0
.

Consequently, the new urn, which we denote by
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
,
differs from the old one
{\mathcal{U}}
only in that one
additional ball of colour
\iota
is added each time a ball of colour
i
is
drawn.
We may thus assume that the two urns are coupled such that they have the
same
X_{j}(t)
for all
j\in\mathsf{Q}
and
t\geqslant 0
, and then

\displaystyle N_{i}(t)=X_{\iota}(t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad N_{ni}=X_{n\iota}.

(11.11)

The new urn
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
is also triangular and satisfies
(A1)
–
(A5)
, or
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
, if

{\mathcal{U}}
does.
In
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
we have
\lambda_{\iota}=0
and,
using (
2.8
) and (
2.9
),

\displaystyle\lambda^{*}_{\iota}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{*}_{i},

(11.12)

\displaystyle\kappa_{\iota}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\kappa_{i},&\lambda^{*}_{i}>0,\\
\kappa_{i}+1,&\lambda^{*}_{i}=0.\end{cases}

(11.13)

(i)
:
By Theorem
4.1
or Theorem
8.6
applied to the new urn
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
,
we have (
4.1
) for
the colour
\iota
, and thus, using (
11.11
) and (
11.12
)–(
11.13
),

\displaystyle t^{-\kappa_{i}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}N_{i}(t)=t^{-\kappa_{\iota}}e^{-\lambda^{*}_{\iota}t}X_{\iota}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{\iota}.

(11.14)

Furthermore, Lemma
3.5
or Lemma
8.11
(a)

applies to
\iota\in\mathsf{Q}^{+}
(with
i
replaced by
\iota
and

j
by
i
), and thus (
3.35
) yields, since
\lambda_{\iota}=0<\lambda^{*}_{i}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{\iota}=\frac{a_{i}r_{i\iota}}{\lambda^{*}_{i}-\lambda_{\iota}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\frac{a_{i}}{\lambda^{*}_{i}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(11.15)

Hence, (
11.2
) follows, with
\mathcal{N}_{i}:={\mathcal{X}}_{\iota}
.

(ii)
:
Similar, now with
\kappa_{\iota}=\kappa_{i}+1
by (
11.13
), and using the second
alternative in (
3.35
) which gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i}:={\mathcal{X}}_{\iota}=\frac{a_{i}r_{i\iota}}{\kappa_{\iota}}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=\frac{a_{i}}{\kappa_{i}+1}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(11.16)

The final sentence follows from Theorems
4.1
and
8.6
, which yield

{\mathcal{X}}_{\iota}>0
a.s.
∎

Proof of Theorem
11.2
.

We add a dummy colour
\iota
as in the proof of Theorem
11.1
, and note that
if the original urn
{\mathcal{U}}
satisfies
(A8)
or is balanced, then the same
holds for the new urn
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
.
(Note that dummy colours with activity 0 are ignored in Definition
10.1
.)

By Theorem
1.8
, Theorem
8.4
, or Theorem
8.5
together with Lemma
10.9
,
the conclusions
of Theorem
1.8
hold for
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
,
except that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\iota}=0
is possible unless we have
(A5′)
or
(A8)
.
(However, our assumptions yield
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s. in all cases.)

We argue similarly to the proof of Theorem
11.1
, now using (
1.3
) or
(
1.4
) for the dummy colour
\iota
in
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
,
together with (
11.11
) and (
11.12
)–(
11.13
).
This yields
a.s. convergence to
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}:=\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{\iota}
in (
11.4
), (
11.5
), or
(
11.6
) (depending on
\lambda^{*}_{i}
and
\widehat{\lambda}
);
note that
\widehat{\lambda}\geqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}
,
that (
2.13
) yields
\gamma_{\iota}=\gamma_{i}
in
(i)
and

\gamma_{\iota}=\kappa_{\iota}=\kappa_{i}+1
in
(ii)
,
and that (
2.12
) shows that
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
is the same for
{{\mathcal{U}}}^{+}
as for
{\mathcal{U}}
.

Finally, the formulas for
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}_{i}
follow from
(
11.15
)–(
11.16
)
and
(
5.12
) or (
5.14
).
∎

Alternatively, we may prove Theorem
11.2
from Theorem
11.1
by adding a dummy
colur 0 as in Section
5
. In any case, the proof is really based on adding
two dummy colours 0 and
\iota
to the continuous-time urn.

Proof of Theorem
11.4
.

The results for the continuous-time urn in
(i)
and
(ii)
follow
by comparing the results of Theorem
11.1

with the limits for
X_{i}(t)
in Theorem
4.1
or Theorem
8.6
,
recalling that
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}>0
a.s. as stated in Theorem
4.1
.
The result for
N_{ni}/X_{ni}
in
(i)

then follows by (
11.1
) and (
2.19
).
Similarly, (
11.9
) and (
11.10
) follow from (
