11.8
) and
(
5.9
) or (
5.13
).
(Alternatively, the results for
N_{ni}/X_{ni}
follow by comparing the
results in Theorem
11.2
and Theorem
1.8
.)
∎

Remark 11.5
.

In Theorem
11.4
, if we assume only
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
but not
(A8)
,
then the conclusions hold on the event

\{\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}>0\}
for
N_{i}(t)
and on

\{\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}>0,{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0\}
for
N_{ni}
.

\triangle

12.
Moment convergence

We have so far considered convergence a.s., which as always implies
convergence in distribution.
We consider in this section whether we also have convergence of moments, or
equivalently convergence in
L^{p}
;
recall the general fact that for a sequence of positive random variables
converging a.s. (as we have here),
convergence of the
p
th moment is equivalent to convergence in
L^{p}
, for
any (real)
p>0
, see e.g.
[
21
, Theorem 5.5.2]
.

Unlike earlier sections, there seems to be an important
difference between the discrete-time and continuous-time cases.

For a continuous-time urn,
we will see below (Theorem
12.2
) that
we always have convergence in
L^{2}

in Theorems
4.1
and
8.6
;
hence the mean and variance converge in these results.
This extends to convergence
in
L^{p}
for any
p>2
, and thus convergence of any moment,
assuming a corresponding moment condition for the replacements
\xi_{ij}

(Theorem
12.3
).

The situation for discrete-time urns is more complicated.
We will only consider balanced urns, and then
prove
L^{2}
-convergence, and thus convergence of mean and variance;
we also extend this to
L^{p}
and higher moments under the
corresponding moment condition for the replacements
\xi_{ij}

(Theorem
12.5
).
It seems likely that this result extends to a wider class of triangular urns.
However, it does
not
hold for all triangular urns.
Example
14.2
gives a simple example where the a.s.
limit does not have a finite mean,
and thus we cannot have even
L^{1}
-convergence in Theorem
1.8
.

Remark 12.1
.

The counterexample in Example
14.2
is rather special (a diagonal urn);

[
28
, Theorem 1.6]
shows that for a class of more typical
unbalanced triangular urns, the a.s. limit in Theorem
1.8
has moments of all
orders. This does not prove moment convergence, but we see no reason against it,
and we conjecture that for these urns, and many others,
we have convergence in
L^{p}
for all
p>0

(Problem
12.6
).

\triangle

L^{p}
-convergence or moment convergence (usually
as part of a proof of convergence in distribution by the method of moments)
have been proved earlier by different methods
for some discrete-time Pólya urns, as far as we know
all of them balanced.
This includes balanced triangular urns with

q=2
or 3 and
deterministic integer-valued replacements
by
Flajolet, Dumas and Puyhaubert 2006
,
[
43
]
(see Examples
14.3
and
14.11
),
and, for
L^{2}
only, more general
balanced triangular urns with deterministic replacements by
Bose, Dasgupta, and Maulik 2009

(Example
14.12
).
Further examples where moment convergence has been shown earlier
are discussed in
Examples
14.6
,
14.7
,
14.8
,
and

14.10
.

12.1.
Continuous-time urns

Theorem 12.2
.

In Theorems
4.1
and
8.6
,
the limit (
4.1
) holds also in
L^{2}
.
In particular, mean and variance converge.

Proof.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
The proofs of Theorems
4.1
and
8.6
show that (
3.83
) holds.
Thus, by the definitions (
3.29
)–(
3.30
),

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geqslant 1}\bigl\lvert{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\bigr\rvert^{2}\leqslant\bigl\lvert 2^{\kappa_{i}}\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\bigr\rvert^{2}\in L^{1}.

(12.1)

Hence, the collection
\{\lvert{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\rvert^{2}:t\geqslant 1\}
is uniformly integrable,
and consequently the a.s. convergence
{\widetilde{X}}_{i}(t)\to{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
implies
convergence in
L^{2}

[
21
, Theorems 5.4.4 and 5.5.2]
.
∎

Theorem
12.2
extends to
L^{p}
for all
p\geqslant 2
as follows.
The proof is based on the arguments in Section
3
combined with the
Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequalities which enable us to replace
L^{2}

estimates by
L^{p}
estimates. The details are quite long, however, so we
postpone them to Appendix
B
.

Theorem 12.3
.

Let
p\geqslant 2
.
In Theorems
4.1
and
8.6
,
assume also
\xi_{ij}\in L^{p}
for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
.
Then, for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
the limit (
4.1
) holds also in
L^{p}
.
Moreover,
\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\in L^{p}
.

Remark 12.4
.

In Appendix
B
, we further show that Theorem
12.3

holds for any
p>1
, also without the
L^{2}
condition
(A4)
.
The proofs below then show that the same holds
for all
L^{p}
results in this section.

\triangle

12.2.
Balanced discrete-time urns

Theorem 12.5
.

Consider a balanced triangular urn statisfying
(A1)
–
(A5)
or
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
.

(i)

In Theorems
1.8
and
8.4
–
8.5
,
the limit (
1.3
)
or (
1.4
)
holds also in
L^{2}
.
In particular, mean and variance converge.

(ii)

Moreover, if
p\geqslant 2
and
\xi_{ij}\in L^{p}
for all
i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
,
then, for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
,
the limit (
1.3
) or (
1.4
) holds also in
L^{p}
.
Hence all moments of order
\leqslant p
converge.

As said above,
this has been shown earlier in some special cases and examples,
in particular
[
17
]
,
[
43
]
(
q=2,3
), and

[
12
]
(
p=2
);
see also the examples in Section
14
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
is a special case of
(ii)
, so we show only the latter.

Note first that
(A6)
holds by Lemma
10.2
,
and that Lemma
10.7
shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s.;
thus the conclusion (
1.3
) or (
1.4
) of Theorem
1.8
holds
by Theorem
1.8
or
8.5
. (Or Theorem
8.4
when it is applicable.)

Let
c>0
be so small that
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(X_{0}>c)>\frac{1}{2}
.
Our assumptions imply (as in the proof of Theorems
8.4
and
8.5
)
that (
5.5
) holds, by the proof in Section
5
.
This implies, by our choice of
c
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\Bigl(\widehat{T}_{n}^{-\kappa_{0}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}}n>c\Bigr)>\frac{1}{2}

(12.2)

for all large
n
. By decreasing
c

(or just by ignoring some small
n
in the sequel),
we may assume that (
12.2
) holds for all
n\geqslant 1
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{n}
denote the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}:=\bigl\{\widehat{T}_{n}^{-\kappa_{0}}e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}}n>c\bigr\},

(12.3)

so that (
12.2
) reads
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\mathcal{E}_{n})>\frac{1}{2}
.

Let
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.
By Theorem
12.3
,
\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i}\in L^{p}
.
Hence, by (
2.19
) and (
3.30
),

\displaystyle Y_{n}:=\left\lvert\frac{X_{ni}}{\bigl(1+\widehat{T}_{n}\bigr)^{\kappa_{i}}e^{\lambda^{*}_{i}\widehat{T}_{n}}}\right\rvert^{p}=\left\lvert\frac{X_{i}(\widehat{T}_{n})}{\bigl(1+\widehat{T}_{n}\bigr)^{\kappa_{i}}e^{\lambda^{*}_{i}\widehat{T}_{n}}}\right\rvert^{p}\leqslant(\widetilde{X}^{**}_{i})^{p}\in L^{1}.

(12.4)

Consequently, the sequence
Y_{n}
is uniformly integrable.
It follows from this and (
12.2
) that the sequence of conditioned
random variables

(Y_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})

also is uniformly integrable.
We consider two cases:

Case 1:
\widehat{\lambda}>0
.

In this case, Lemma
10.7
shows, using (
5.3
) and (
2.13
),
that
\kappa_{0}=\widehat{\kappa}=0

and
\gamma_{i}=\kappa_{i}
.
Hence, the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
means

{e^{-\widehat{\lambda}\widehat{T}_{n}}n>c}
,
and thus
\widehat{T}_{n}\leqslant t_{n}:=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}(\log n+C)
.
Consequently,
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
we have (for
n\geqslant 2
)

\displaystyle Z_{n}:=\left\lvert\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}\log^{\gamma_{i}}n}\right\rvert^{p}=\left\lvert\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\lambda^{*}_{i}/\widehat{\lambda}}\log^{\kappa_{i}}n}\right\rvert^{p}\leqslant C\left\lvert\frac{X_{ni}}{t_{n}^{\kappa_{i}}e^{\lambda^{*}_{i}t_{n}}}\right\rvert^{p}\leqslant CY_{n}

(12.5)

and thus
the uniform integrability of
(Y_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})

implies uniform integrability of

(Z_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})
.
However, by Lemma
10.4
and (
12.3
),
X_{ni}
is
independent of the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
; hence, so is
Z_{n}
and thus

(Z_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}Z_{n}
.
Consequently, the sequence
Z_{n}
is uniformly integrable.

In other words,
the left-hand side of (
1.3
) is uniformly

p
th power integrable. Hence, the a.s. convergence in (
1.3
) implies
convergence also in
L^{p}
.

Case 2:
\widehat{\lambda}=0
.

This is similar.
In this case, (
12.3
) means
\widehat{T}_{n}\leqslant Cn^{1/\kappa_{0}}
.
We now define

\displaystyle Z_{n}:=\left\lvert\frac{X_{ni}}{n^{\kappa_{i}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}}}\right\rvert^{p},

(12.6)

and note again that
Z_{n}
is independent of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
.
Since
0\leqslant\lambda^{*}_{i}\leqslant\widehat{\lambda}=0
we have
\lambda^{*}_{i}=0
, and

\widehat{\kappa}_{0}=\kappa_{0}
by (
5
); hence (
12.6
) and (
12.4
) show that
on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, we have
Z_{n}\leqslant CY_{n}
.
Consequently, we have again

Z_{n}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}(Z_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})\leqslant(CY_{n}\mid\mathcal{E}_{n})
, and it follows
that
Z_{n}
is uniformly integrable.
Hence the a.s. convergence (
1.4
) holds also in
L^{p}
.
∎

As said above,
Example
14.2
shows that Theorem
12.5
does not extend to all triangular
urns.
However, it seems likely that it extends to many unbalanced urns; we leave
this as an open problem.

Problem 12.6
.

Find more general conditions (including also some unbalanced urns)
for convergence in
L^{2}
or
L^{p}
in Theorems
1.8
and
8.4
.

12.3.
Moments for drawn colours

The results above on convergence in
L^{2}
, and thus convergence of mean and
variance, apply to the number of drawn balls with a given colour,
N_{ni}

and
N_{i}(t)
, since as shown in the proofs in Section
11
, they can be
regarded as
X_{n\iota}
and
X_{\iota}(t)
for an extended urn with a dummy colour

\iota
added.
Hence we obtain:

Theorem 12.7
.

In Theorem
11.1
, the a.s. limit (
11.2
) or (
11.3
)
holds also in
L^{2}
.
Moreover, if
p\geqslant 2
and
\xi_{ij}\in L^{p}

\forall i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
, then the
limit holds also in
L^{p}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
12.2
or
12.3
applied to
X_{\iota}(t)
.
∎

Theorem 12.8
.

In Theorem
11.2
, if the urn is balanced, then
the a.s. limit (
11.4
), (
11.5
), or (
11.6
)
holds also in
L^{2}
.
Moreover, if
p\geqslant 2
and
\xi_{ij}\in L^{p}

\forall i,j\in\mathsf{Q}
, then the
limit holds also in
L^{p}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
12.5
applied to
X_{n\iota}
.
∎

13.
Rates of convergence?

For classical Pólya urns
(Example
14.1
),
the rate of convergence for convergence in distribution in (
14.4
) or
(
14.5
) has been studied, for several different metrics; see

[
29
]
and the references there.
As noted in
[
29
, Remark 1.4]
, the rate of a.s. convergence is
slower, and is the same as in the law of large numbers for i.i.d. Bernoulli
variables, which is given by the law of iterated logarithm.

For other triangular urns,
we are not aware of any similar results on rates of convergence;
however,
[
17
]
gives upper bounds for the rate of convergence of moments and in a local limit theorem,
for some balanced triangular urns with deterministic replacements.
(Irreducible, and thus non-triangular, balanced urns with
q=2

and deterministic replacements
are studied in
[
34
]
.)

Problem 13.1
.

Study rates of convergence in e.g. (
1.3
) and (
4.1
),
both for the a.s. convergence and for convergence in distribution.

Note that this problem
is closely related to the problem
studying fluctuations from the limit, mentioned in Remark
1.9
.

14.
Examples

We consider several examples, many of which have been treated earlier from
different perspectives.
The purpose is to illustrate both the theorems above and (some of)
their relations to earlier literature.
We generally label the colours by
1,\dots,q
, and then
assume
\xi_{ij}=0
when
i<j
.
We assume that the initial composition
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is deterministic.
We write for convenience
x_{i}:=X_{i0}=X_{i}(0)

and
\mathbf{x}:=(x_{i})_{1}^{q}=\mathbf{X}_{0}
.
We denote the total number of balls in the urn after
n
draws by

|\mathbf{X}_{n}|:=\sum_{i=1}^{q}X_{ni}
.

When
q=2
we sometimes also call the
colours
white
and
black
(in this order;
thus a black draw may add only black balls:
“there is no escape from a black hole”).
We then may write

W_{n}:=X_{n1}
,
B_{n}:=X_{n2}
,
W(t):=X_{1}(t)
,
B(t):=X_{2}(t)
,

w_{0}:=x_{1}=X_{10}
,
b_{0}:=x_{2}=X_{20}
.

We usually describe the urns using the replacement matrix
(\xi_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{q}

where the rows are the replacement vectors.
(See Remark
1.4
.)

In all our examples, all activities
a_{i}=1
. Thus

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}=r_{ii}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ii},\qquad i\in\mathsf{Q}=\{1,\dots,q\}.

(14.1)

Example 14.1
.

The classical Pólya urn has balls of
q
colours; when a ball is drawn it is
replaced together with a fixed number
b>0
balls of the same colour.
Hence, the replacement matrix is deterministic and diagonal, with entries
b

on the diagonal. This urn is obviously balanced, and we have

\lambda_{i}=\lambda^{*}_{i}=b
and
\kappa_{i}=0
for every colour
i
.

This urn model was studied (for
q=2
)
already by
Markov 1917
,

Eggenberger and Pólya 1923
and
Pólya 1930
.
See also e.g.

Johnson and Kotz 1977
and
Mahmoud 2009
.

For this urn (as for any diagonal urn),
in the continuous-time version, the different colours evolve independently,
and each colour is version of the Yule process.
More precisely,
X_{i}(t)/b
is a Yule process started with
x_{i}/b

individuals, where each individual gets children at rate
b
;
thus
X_{i}(t/b)/b
is a Yule process with the standard rate 1.
(This is a classical branching process if
x_{i}/b
is an integer, and in
general a CB process.)
It is well-known that in this case
e^{-bt}X_{i}(t)/b\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\Gamma(x_{i}/b,1)

and thus

\displaystyle e^{-bt}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\in\Gamma(x_{i}/b,b);

(14.2)

furthermore,

{\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{q}
are independent, since the processes
X_{i}(t)
are
independent.
This is an example of Theorem
4.1
. Moreover, (
5.11
)–(
5.12
),
or (
5.16
) where now
\mathsf{Q}_{*}=\mathsf{Q}
,
show together with (
5.17
) that

\displaystyle\frac{X_{in}}{n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}:=b\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{q}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}}.

(14.3)

It follows that
the vector of proportions converges:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{X}_{n}}{|\mathbf{X}_{n}|}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{b}\widehat{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{X}}}}:=\frac{1}{b}\bigl(\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},\dots,\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{q}\bigr)=\frac{({\mathcal{X}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{X}}_{q})}{\sum_{j=1}^{q}{\mathcal{X}}_{j}},

(14.4)

where, as a consequence of (
14.2
), the limit vector
b^{-1}\widehat{\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{X}}}}

has a Dirichlet distribution
with parameter
\mathbf{x}/b
.
In particular, each marginal converges a.s. to a Beta distributed variable:

\frac{X_{ni}}{|\mathbf{X}_{n}|}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}b^{-1}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}\sim B\Bigl(\frac{x_{i}}{b},\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{x_{j}}{b}\Bigr).

(14.5)

These results are all well known; the limit (
14.5
) with convergence
in distribution was shown for
q=2
already in

[
39
]
and
[
41
]
,
and for general
q

in
[
10
]
(in a special case)
and
[
2
]
;
see also
[
31
, Section 6.3.3]

and
[
38
]
.
Furthermore,
a.s. convergence has been shown by a number of
methods, for example in

[
10
]
and
[
2
]
.

\triangle

Example 14.2
.

A
diagonal
Pólya urn has
\xi_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
; in other
words, all added balls have the same colour as the drawn ball.
This is a generalization of the classical Pólya urn in Example
14.1
,
but now the
diagonal elements
\xi_{ii}
can be random, and they may have different
distributions.
A.s. convergence for this urn
has been shown, under weak technical
conditions, by
Athreya 1969
;
see also
Aguech 2009
.

Consider for simplicity the case when the replacements are deterministic,
and assume to avoid trivialities that
r_{ii}=\xi_{ii}>0
for every
i\in\mathsf{Q}
.

As in Example
14.1
, in the continuous-time urn, the colours evolve as
independent Yule processes, now with possibly different rates
\lambda_{i}=\xi_{ii}
.
Hence, generalizing (
14.2
),

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}\in\Gamma(x_{i}/\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i}),

(14.6)

with all
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
independent.

Consider the simplest case:
q=2
, and assume

\lambda_{1}=\alpha
and
\lambda_{2}=\delta
with
\alpha>\delta>0
.
Then
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
,
\widehat{\lambda}=\alpha
,
\kappa_{i}=\widehat{\kappa}=\gamma_{i}=0
(
i=1,2
).
It follows from (
5.11
)–(
5.12
) and (
5.17
) (or directly from
(
14.6
))
that

\displaystyle n^{-\delta/\alpha}X_{n2}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\alpha^{\delta/\alpha}\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}^{\delta/\alpha}}.

(14.7)

Note that if
{\mathcal{X}}\sim\Gamma(a,b)
, then its moments (for arbitrary real
r
)
are given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\mathcal{X}}^{r}=\begin{cases}b^{r}\Gamma(a+r)/\Gamma(a)<\infty,&-a<r<\infty,\\
\infty,&r\leqslant-a.\end{cases}

(14.8)

In particular, since
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
are independent,
it follows from (
14.6
)–(
14.8
) that for
r>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}^{r}<\infty\iff\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}^{-r\delta/\alpha}<\infty\iff r\delta/\alpha<x_{1}/\alpha\iff r<x_{1}/\delta.

(14.9)

Consequently,
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}
does not have finite moments of all orders.
In particular, we cannot always have (finite) moment convergence in (
14.7
).
Taking, for example,
\alpha=2
,
\delta=1
, and
x_{1}=x_{2}=1
we see that not even
the mean
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}
is finite; hence we cannot have convergence in
L^{1}
or

L^{2}
in Theorem
1.8
.

As far as we know, it is an open problem to find asymptotics of moments

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}X_{n2}^{r}
for general
r>0
in this (simple) example.

\triangle

Example 14.3
.

Consider a two-colour urn with a deterministic replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\gamma\\
0&\alpha\end{pmatrix}.

(14.10)

(We have chosen a notation agreeing with

[
28
]
, although colours are taken in different order there and thus
the matrices are written differently.)
This urn (and special cases of it) have been studied in many papers; in
particular,
[
28
]
gives a detailed study of limits in distribution.
The balanced case
\alpha=\delta+\gamma
with integers
\alpha,\gamma,\delta

is studied by very different methods
(generating functions)
in
[
43
]
and

[
17
]
. A.s. convergence has been shown in special cases in

[
18
;
19
;
11
;
12
]
(
\alpha=\delta+\gamma
), and

[
1
]
(
\alpha\geqslant\delta
).

Suppose that
\delta>0
,
\gamma>0
,
w_{0}=x_{1}>0
, and
b_{0}=x_{2}\geqslant 0
;
suppose also either
\alpha\geqslant 0
, or
\alpha=-1
together with

\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
x_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.
Then the urn satisfies

(A1)
–
(A5)
if
\alpha\geqslant 0
,
and
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
–
(A8)
for all
\alpha
.
Hence,
Theorems
1.8
and
4.1
apply if
\alpha\geqslant 0
, and
Theorems
8.4
and
8.6
apply for any
\alpha
;
consequently, the conclusions of
Theorems
1.8
and
4.1
hold for all cases.

We have
\lambda_{1}=\delta
and
\lambda_{2}=\alpha
.
Furthermore, since
\gamma>0
, we have
1\to 2
.
(Thus 1 is the only minimal colour.)
Hence,
\lambda^{*}_{1}:=\delta
,
\lambda^{*}_{2}:=\alpha\lor\delta
and thus

\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\alpha\lor\delta
;
furthermore
\kappa_{1}=0
while

\kappa_{2}=1
when
\alpha=\delta
and
\kappa_{2}=0
otherwise.
We consider several cases.

Case 1
,
\alpha<\delta
:

Then
\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\widehat{\lambda}=\delta>0
;
furthermore,
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=0=\widehat{\kappa}
, and (
2.13
) yields

\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
. Consequently, Theorem
1.8
(i)
or Theorem
8.4

yields

\displaystyle\frac{X_{ni}}{n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i},\qquad i=1,2.

(14.11)

Furthermore, 1 is the only leader, and thus Theorem
7.2
shows that

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

are constants. To find them, we can use Lemma
6.7
. By Lemma
6.1

(simplifying the notation),
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=c_{i}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
, where obviously
c_{1}=1
,
and Lemma
6.7
gives the eigenvalue equation

\displaystyle(c_{1},c_{2})\begin{pmatrix}\delta&\gamma\\
0&\alpha\end{pmatrix}=\delta(c_{1},c_{2}),

(14.12)

i.e.,
\gamma+\alpha c_{2}=\delta c_{2}
,
with the solution

c_{2}=\gamma/(\delta-\alpha)
. In other words,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{2}=\frac{\gamma}{\delta-\alpha}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}.

(14.13)

This follows also directly from Lemma
3.5
and (
3.35
).

If we add a dummy colour 0 as in Section
5
, then (
5.17
) and
(
14.13
) yield

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\delta^{-1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{2})=\frac{1}{\delta}\frac{\gamma+\delta-\alpha}{\delta-\alpha}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}.

(14.14)

Hence, by (
5.12
),

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}=\frac{\delta(\delta-\alpha)}{\gamma+\delta-\alpha},

(14.15)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}=\frac{\delta\gamma}{\gamma+\delta-\alpha}.

(14.16)

Consequently, as
{n\to\infty}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n1}}{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}=\frac{\delta(\delta-\alpha)}{\gamma+\delta-\alpha},

(14.17)

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n2}}{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\frac{\delta\gamma}{\gamma+\delta-\alpha}.

(14.18)

This is in agreement with
[
28
, Theorem 1.3(i)-(iii) and Lemma 1.2]
,
which give the asymptotic distribution of the difference
X_{ni}-n\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}

divided by the correct normalization factor,
which implies
(and is much more precise than)
convergence in probability in (
14.17
)–(
14.18
).
(The normalization factor is

n^{1/2}
for
\alpha<\delta/2
,
(n\log n)^{1/2}
for
\alpha=\delta/2
and

n^{\alpha/\delta}
for
\delta/2<\alpha<\delta
. Moreover,
the distribution is
asymptotically normal for
\alpha\leqslant\delta/2
, but not for
\delta/2<\alpha<\delta
.
See
[
28
]
for details.)

Case 2
,
\alpha=\delta
:

Then
\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\widehat{\lambda}=\delta>0
;
furthermore,
\kappa_{1}=0
and
\kappa_{2}=1=\widehat{\kappa}
, and thus
(
2.13
) yields

\gamma_{1}=-1
and
\gamma_{2}=0
. Consequently,
Theorem
1.8
(i)
yields

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n1}}{n/\log n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},

(14.19)

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n2}}{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}.

(14.20)

Furthermore, also in this case, 1 is the only leader, and thus Theorem
7.2

shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}
are constants.
However, unlike Case
14.3
,
\lambda_{2}=\lambda^{*}_{2}
and thus 2 is now a subleader.
Again, Lemma
6.1
shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}=c_{2}{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
, where (
6.20
) in
Lemma
6.7
immediately yields

\displaystyle c_{2}=\frac{a_{1}r_{12}}{\kappa_{2}}c_{1}=\gamma.

(14.21)

Furthermore, (
5.15
) now yields
\mathsf{Q}_{*}=\{2\}
, and thus (
5.17
)
yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}=\delta^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}.

(14.22)

Consequently, recalling (
5.12
), the limits in (
14.19
)–(
14.20
)
are

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\lambda}\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}=\delta^{2}\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}=\frac{\delta^{2}}{c_{2}}=\frac{\delta^{2}}{\gamma},

(14.23)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}=\delta.

(14.24)

This is in agreement with the result on the asymptotic distribution
in
[
28
, Theorem 1.3(iv) and Lemma 1.2]
,
which implies convergence in probability in (
14.19
)–(
14.20
).

Case 3
,
\alpha>\delta
:

Then
0<\lambda^{*}_{1}=\delta<\lambda^{*}_{2}=\alpha=\widehat{\lambda}
;
furthermore,
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=0=\widehat{\kappa}
, and thus

\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=0
. Consequently,
Theorem
1.8
(i)
yields
(see also
[
1
]
)

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n1}}{n^{\delta/\alpha}}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},

(14.25)

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n2}}{n}

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}.

(14.26)

In this case, both colours 1 and 2 are leaders; hence Theorem
6.4
shows that

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
are absolutely continuous, also jointly.
Furthermore, Theorem
7.1
(ii)
shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
is absolutely continuous,
while Theorem
7.2
shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}
is deterministic.
We have again
\mathsf{Q}_{*}=\{2\}
, which by (
5.17
) now yields

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}=\alpha^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}.

(14.27)

Hence, (
5.12
) yields

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}^{\delta/\alpha}}=\alpha^{\delta/\alpha}\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\delta/\alpha}},

(14.28)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathcal{X}}_{2}}{{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}=\alpha.

(14.29)

However, the formula (
14.28
) for
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
does not seem to be of
much use to find the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
.
This distribution was found by other methods in

[
28
, Theorem 1.3(v)]
, which yields convergence in distribution of

X_{n1}/n^{\delta/\alpha}
; the a.s. convergence in (
14.25
) is a stronger
result, and the distribution of
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
is
thus the limit distribution found in

[
28
]
.
This limit distribution is characterized in
[
28
]
, but no simple form
is known in general; it is shown in
[
28
, Theorem 1.6]
that

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
has moments of all orders, and a complicated integral formula is
given for these moments.
Except in the balanced case below, it is, as far as we know, an open problem
whether moments converge in (
14.25
) (to these limits) or not.

Case 4
,
\alpha=\delta+\gamma
:

The balanced case of the two-colour urn studied here
is
\alpha=\delta+\gamma
; by our assumption
\gamma>0
, this is a special case of
Case
14.3
, and thus (
14.25
)–(
14.29
) hold.
In this special case,
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
can be characterized by its moments, for
which there is a simple formula,
see
(for integers
\alpha,\gamma,\delta,r
)

[
43
, Theorem 2.9]
,
[
17
, Proposition 17]
,
and (for the general case)

[
28
, Theorem 1.7]
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{r}=\delta^{r}\frac{\Gamma\bigl((x_{1}+x_{2})/\alpha\bigr)\Gamma(x_{1}/\delta+r)}{\Gamma(x_{1}/\delta)\Gamma\bigl((x_{1}+x_{2}+r\delta)/\alpha\bigr)},\qquad r>0.

(14.30)

It follows that the moment generating function
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}e^{t\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}}
is finite for all real
t
,
and thus the moments (
14.30
) (even for integer
r
)
determine the distribution.
Moreover,
Theorem
12.5
shows that all moments converge in (
14.25
) (to the limits
(
14.30
)); this was earlier shown in

[
17
;
43
]

in the case that the replacements
\delta,\gamma,\alpha
are integers.

If we further assume
x_{2}=0
, so we start only with white balls, then

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
has a density function that can be expressed using the density function
of a Mittag-Leffler distribution with parameter
{\delta/\alpha}
, or a
{\delta/\alpha}
-stable
distribution, see
[
28
, Theorem 1.8]

and
[
17
, Proposition 16]
.

The continuous-time processes
W(t)
and
B(t)

are studied by
[
13
]
,
which includes our Theorem
4.1
for this balanced urn.

\triangle

Example 14.4
.

Generalizing Example
14.3
,
consider a general triangular
two-colour urn
{\mathcal{U}}
with random replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{11}&\xi_{12}\\
0&\xi_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

(14.31)

Such urns have been studied by
Aguech 2009
,
who proved (among other results)
the existence of a.s. limits under some assumptions
(including our
(A5)
,
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{22}\geqslant\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}
, and an unnecessary
independence assumption).
We extend this result as follows.

Assume
\xi_{11},\xi_{12},\xi_{22}\in L^{2}
(Condition
(A4)
),
and let
\delta:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}
,
\gamma:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{12}
,
\alpha:=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{22}
,
so that
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\delta&\gamma\\
0&\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is the mean replacement matrix.
Suppose that
(A5′)
holds, that
\xi_{11}\geqslant 0
a.s.,
and that, as in Example
14.3
,

\delta>0
,
\gamma>0
,
w_{0}=x_{1}>0
, and
b_{0}=x_{2}\geqslant 0
.
Then the urn satisfies

(A1)
–
(A5)
if
\xi_{22}\geqslant 0
a.s.,
and
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A7)
–
(A8)
in any case.
Hence, Theorems
8.4
and
8.6
apply, and thus the conclusions of Theorems
1.8

and
4.1
hold for all
\alpha
.

As in Section
9
, we
let
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
denote
the mean urn with replacement matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\delta&\gamma\\
0&\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right)
;
this urn is of the type in Example
14.3
.
As discussed in Section
9
, all parameters
\lambda_{i},\lambda^{*}_{i},\widehat{\lambda},\dots

are the same for
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
, and thus all results are qualitatively
the same for the two urns.
Hence, all results in Example
14.3
hold for the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
with random
replacements too, except any assertions on the precise distributions
of the limits (including the moment formula (
14.30
), which as shown in
Example
14.5
below is
not
valid in general for random replacements).
Note, however, that by Theorem
9.1
,
in all cases in Example
14.3
with a deterministic limit
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
for the mean
urn, we have the same limit for the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
.

\triangle

Example 14.5
.

Consider a two-colour urn
{\mathcal{U}}
with the random replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\xi&1-\xi\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

(14.32)

where
\xi\in\operatorname{Be}(p)
for some
p\in(0,1)
.
In other words, if we draw a white ball, we add another ball that is white
with probability
p
, and otherwise black; if we draw a black ball we always
add another black ball. (As usual, we also always return the drawn ball.)
Note that this urn is balanced, in spite of replacements being random.

This urn appears in several applications, see Examples
14.6
and
14.10

for two of them.

This urn is a special case of Example
14.4
, and
a.s. convergence for the urn follows from
Theorem
1.8
.
Moment convergence follows from Theorem
12.5
.

The mean replacement matrix is

\displaystyle(r_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}p&1-p\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(14.33)

By Section
9
, the asymptotic behaviour of the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
is qualitatively the
same as for the mean urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
with the replacement matrix (
14.33
),
which is an instance of Example
14.3
, more precisely the balanced
Case
14.3
.
In particular, since (
14.29
) shows that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\alpha=1
is constant for
the mean urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
,
the same holds for the original urn
{\mathcal{U}}
by Theorem
9.1
.

We may also relate the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
to the mean urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
in another, more
direct, way.
Conditioned on the contents
(X_{n1},X_{n2})
of the urn at time
n
,
the probability that the next added ball is white is, letting
\zeta
be the
colour of the drawn ball,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\bigl(\zeta=1\mid X_{n1},X_{n2}\bigr)\cdot p+\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}\bigl(\zeta=2\mid X_{n1},X_{n2}\bigr)\cdot 0=\frac{pX_{n1}}{X_{n1}+X_{n2}}.

(14.34)

Hence, if we define

\displaystyle Y_{n1}

\displaystyle:=pX_{n1},

(14.35)

\displaystyle Y_{n2}

\displaystyle:=(1-p)X_{n1}+X_{n2},

(14.36)

and note that
Y_{n1}+Y_{n2}=X_{n1}+X_{n2}
, we see from (
14.34
) that
we may
regard the
added
ball in
{\mathcal{U}}
as the
drawn
ball in an urn
with composition
\mathbf{Y}_{n}=(Y_{n1},Y_{n2})
. Adding a white ball to
(X_{n1},X_{n2})

(i.e., increasing
X_{n1}
by 1) means by (
14.35
)–(
14.36
) adding

(p,1-p)
to
(Y_{n1},Y_{n2})
, while adding a black ball to
(X_{n1},X_{n2})

means adding
(0,1)
to
(Y_{n1},Y_{n2})
.
Consequently, the stochastic process
(\mathbf{Y}_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
describes a Pólya
urn with the
replacement matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}p&1-p\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
which is the same as (
14.33
) for the mean urn
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
above.
Note, however, that the
initial conditions now are, by (
14.35
)–(
14.36
),

\displaystyle y_{1}=px_{1},\qquad y_{2}=(1-p)x_{1}+x_{2}.

(14.37)

By Example
14.3
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{n1}/n^{p}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{1}

(14.38)

where the limit by (
14.30
) has moments, recalling (
14.37
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{1}^{r}=p^{r}\frac{\Gamma(y_{1}+y_{2})\,\Gamma(y_{1}/p+r)}{\Gamma(y_{1}/p)\,\Gamma(y_{1}+y_{2}+rp)}=p^{r}\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2})\,\Gamma(x_{1}+r)}{\Gamma(x_{1})\,\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2}+rp)}.

(14.39)

Hence, by (
14.35
), we have in the urn
{\mathcal{U}}

with replacements (
14.32
)

\displaystyle X_{n1}/n^{p}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}

(14.40)

with

\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}=p^{-1}\widehat{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{1}
, and thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{r}=p^{-r}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{1}^{r}=\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2})\,\Gamma(x_{1}+r)}{\Gamma(x_{1})\,\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2}+rp)},\qquad r\geqslant 0.

(14.41)

By comparing (
14.40
) with (
14.30
) for the mean urn, we see that
the means

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
are the same for the two urns, while for the second moment,
(
14.41
) and (
14.30
) yield, for the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
and its mean urn

{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
, respectively,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{2}=x_{1}(x_{1}+1)\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2})}{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2}+2p)},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{2}=x_{1}(x_{1}+p)\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2})}{\Gamma(x_{1}+x_{2}+2p)}.

(14.42)

The variance is thus larger for the urn
{\mathcal{U}}
with random replacement.
(Perhaps not surprisingly.)
This shows that an urn and its mean urn in general have different asymptotic
distributions,
although the qualitative behaviour is the same as shown in Section
9
.

\triangle

Example 14.6
.

One example where the urn in Example
14.5
with replacement matrix
(
14.32
) appears is that
it describes the size of the root cluster
for bond percolation (with parameter
p
)
on the random recursive tree
(with vertices in the root cluster coloured
white and all other vertices black, and
the initial vector
(1,0)
).
(See the argument in the generalization Example
14.7
below.
The root cluster is studied by other methods in

[
6
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
5
]
,
[
14
]
.)
In this case we obtain
(
14.40
) where
(
14.41
) yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{r}=\frac{\Gamma(r+1)}{\Gamma(1+rp)},\qquad r\geqslant 0,

(14.43)

which means that
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
has a
Mittag-Leffler distribution;
this was proved by
Baur and Bertoin 2015
(using other methods).

\triangle

Example 14.7
.

Baur 2020
and

Desmarais, Holmgren, and Wagner 2023
considered (among other things)
the root cluster in bond percolation on a preferential attachment tree,
generalizing Example
14.6
.
The tree is defined as follows, for a real parameter
\alpha
.
Construct the rooted tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
with
n
vertices
recursively, starting with
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
being just the root and adding vertices
one by one;
each new vertex is attached
to a parent
v
chosen among existing vertices with probability proportional
to
\alpha d(v)+1
, where
d(v)
is the current outdegree of
v
.
We also perform bond percolation, and let each edge by
active

with probability
p\in(0,1)
, independently of all other edges.
(Note that both active and passive edges are counted in the outdegree.)
A vertex is active if it is connected to the root by a path of active edges.
Let
Z_{n}
be the number of active vertices in
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
.

The case
\alpha=0
gives the random recursive tree in Example
14.6
.
We consider here the case
\alpha\geqslant 0
(as assumed in
[
5
]
),
and study the modifications for
\alpha<0

in the following example.

We model this process by an urn
with two colours, where each vertex
v
contributes
\alpha d(v)+1
balls,
which are white if the urn is active and black otherwise.
To find the parent of the next vertex corresponds to drawing a ball from the
urn; if the ball is white then the parent is active and the new vertex
becomes active with probability
p
(and otherwise passive); if the ball is
black then the parent is passive and the new vertex always becomes passive.
Since the outdegree of the parent increases by 1, we add
\alpha
balls of the
same colour as the drawn ball, plus one ball for the new vertex which is
white if the new vertex is active, i.e., with probability
p
if the drawn
ball is white, and otherwise black.
Hence, the urn is a Pólya urn with random replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha+\xi&1-\xi\\
0&\alpha+1\end{pmatrix},

(14.44)

where
\xi\in\operatorname{Be}(p)
. We start with 1 active vertex, and thus the urn starts
with a single white ball, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}=(1,0)
.

If
\alpha=0
(the random recursive tree), we get again (
14.32
), as
discussed in Example
14.6
.
In general, unlike Example
14.6
, the number of active vertices
Z_{n}
is not
directly reflected by the contents of the urn.
However, if
N_{n1}
is the number of drawn white balls, then this is the
number of vertices that get an active parent;
hence
N_{n1}
is the total outdegree of the active vertices, and therefore
the number of white balls in the urn is

\displaystyle W_{n}=X_{n1}=\alpha N_{n1}+Z_{n}.

(14.45)

The replacement matrix (
14.44
) shows that the
urn is of the type in Example
14.4
; furthermore, it is balanced with balance

\alpha+1
. We have
\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda_{1}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}=\alpha+p
and
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda_{2}=\alpha+1
;
further
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=0
.
Theorem
1.8
yields

\displaystyle\frac{X_{n1}}{n^{(\alpha+p)/(\alpha+1)}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}.

(14.46)

Moreover, Theorem
11.4
yields

\displaystyle\frac{N_{n1}}{X_{n1}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{\lambda^{*}_{1}}=\frac{1}{\alpha+p},

(14.47)

and thus (
14.45
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{n}}{X_{n1}}=\frac{X_{n1}-\alpha N_{n1}}{X_{n1}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}1-\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+p}=\frac{p}{\alpha+p}.

(14.48)

Hence, (
14.46
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{n}}{n^{(\alpha+p)/(\alpha+1)}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}:=\frac{p}{\alpha+p}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},

(14.49)

where the limit is in
(0,\infty)
a.s.
The limits (
14.46
) and (
14.49
) hold also in
L^{r}
for any
r<\infty

by Theorems
12.5
and
12.8
; hence all moments converge.

This complements

Baur 2020
, who shows
L^{2}
-convergence in
(
14.49
)
and gives the first two moments of the
limit
(by different but related methods),
and

Desmarais, Holmgren, and Wagner 2023
who
prove convergence of all moments in (
14.49
)
and give a recursion for the moments of the limit.
(The distribution of the limit is not known explicitly.)

\triangle

Example 14.8
.

In Example
14.7
, we assumed
\alpha\geqslant 0
. However, the results extend easily
to the case
\alpha<0
.
(This case was included in
[
14
]
.)
In this case, as is well known, we must have
\alpha=-1/d

with

d\geqslant 1
an integer; the random tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
then is a
random
d
-ary recursive tree
[
15
, Section 1.3.3]
.
(The case
d=1
is trivial, and we assume
d\geqslant 2
.)
In this case the replacements (
14.44
) do not satisfy
(A5′)
, since

\alpha+\xi
may take the non-integer negative value
-1/d
.
This is can be remedied as in Remark
8.1

by multiplying the number of white balls by
d
and adjusting the activity

a_{1}
, but in the present case we find it simpler to multiply the number of
balls of both colours by
d
and keep the activities
a_{i}=1
.
This gives the replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}d\xi-1&d-d\xi\\
0&d-1\end{pmatrix},

(14.50)

with the initial state
\mathbf{x}=(d,0)
.
In (
14.45
), we have to replace
X_{n1}
by
X_{n1}/d
, which yields

\displaystyle Z_{n}=\frac{X_{n1}+N_{n1}}{d}.

(14.51)

We have
\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda_{1}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}=dp-1
, so
(A7)
requires
dp>1
.
(In fact, it is easily seen that if
dp\leqslant 1
, then
Z_{n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}Z_{\infty}<\infty

[
14
]
.)
Assuming
dp>1
,
this urn satisfies
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
.
It does not satisfy
(A8)
, but Lemma
10.9
shows that
{\mathcal{X}}_{0}>0
a.s.,
and thus we obtain by Theorem
8.5

the a.s. limit (
14.49
) again
(with
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
replaced by
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}/d
);
this can be written

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{n}}{n^{(dp-1)/(d-1)}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}:=\frac{p}{dp-1}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}.

(14.52)

Moment convergence, earlier shown by

[
14
]
,
follows from Theorems
12.5
and
12.8
.

\triangle

Example 14.9
.

In Example
14.7
, we studied the number
Z_{n}
of vertices in the preferential
attachment tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}
such that the path to the root contains only active
vertices. More generally, let
Z^{(k)}_{n}
be the number of vertices such that
this path contains exactly
k\geqslant 0
passive edges.
For fixed
k
, this can be treated similarly, with an urn
with
q\geqslant k+2
colours
1,\dots,q
,
where vertices
with
j
passive edges on the path to the root are represented by colour

\max(j+1,q)
.
For example, for
q=3
, this leads to an urn with replacement
matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha+\xi&1-\xi&0\\
0&\alpha+\xi&1-\xi\\
0&0&\alpha+1\end{pmatrix},

(14.53)

where
\xi\in\operatorname{Be}(p)
as above. (It does not matter whether we write this
with the same
\xi
on both rows or not; recall Remark
1.4
.)
For a general
q\geqslant 2
we have

\xi_{ii}=\alpha+\xi
and
\xi_{i,i+1}=1-\xi
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant q-1
,

\xi_{qq}=\alpha+1
,
and all other
\xi_{ij}=0
.
The urn is balanced with balance
b=\alpha+1
.
We assume for simplicity
\alpha\geqslant 0
; the case
\alpha<0
can be treated as in
Example
14.8
.

We find
\lambda_{1}=\dots=\lambda_{q-1}=\alpha+p
and
\lambda_{q}=\alpha+1
, and thus

\lambda^{*}_{1}=\dots=\lambda^{*}_{q-1}=\alpha+p
,
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda^{*}_{q}=\alpha+1
,

\kappa_{i}=i-1
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant q-1
,
and
\kappa_{q}=0
.
We have in analogy with (
14.45
),
Z^{(k)}_{n}=X_{n,k+1}-\alpha N_{n,k+1}

for
k\leqslant q-2
.
Theorem
1.8
applies and shows together with Theorem
11.4

as in Example
14.7
,
an a.s. limit. Since
\kappa_{k+1}=k
, we now get

\displaystyle\frac{Z^{(k)}_{n}}{n^{(\alpha+p)/(\alpha+1)}\log^{k}n}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}^{(k)}:=\frac{p}{\alpha+p}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{k+1}.

(14.54)

Furthermore,
we obtain from (
7.3
) and induction,
since
r_{i,i+1}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}(1-\xi)=1-p

for every
i\leqslant q-1
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{1-p}{\alpha+1}\right)^{k}{\mathcal{Z}}^{(0)},

(14.55)

where
{\mathcal{Z}}^{(0)}
equals
{\mathcal{Z}}
in (
14.49
).
Consequently, the numbers
Z^{(k)}_{n}
for different
k
are asymptotically
proportional.

\triangle

Example 14.10
.

Another example where the urn in Example
14.5
with replacement matrix
(
14.32
) appears is for elephant random walks with delays.
In a standard elephant random walk (ERW), the elephant takes steps

Y_{n}\in\{\pm 1\}
;
after an initial step
Y_{1}
, the elephant (which remembers the entire walk)
chooses one of the preceding steps, uniformly at random, and then randomly
either (with probability
p
) repeats it, or (with probability
q
)
takes a step in the opposite direction. (Here
q=1-p
.)
As noted by
Baur and Bertoin 2016

(to which we refer for details and further references),
this may be modelled by a (non-triangular)
Pólya urn, with one white ball for each step
+1
and one black ball for
each step
-1
taken so far; the replacement matrix is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\xi&1-\xi\\
1-\xi&\xi\end{pmatrix}

(14.56)

with
\xi\in\operatorname{Be}(p)
.
Hence results for the ERW follows from known results for irreducible
Pólya urns
[
7
]
. (We have nothing to add here.)

In the
elephant random walk with delays

[
33
;
23
;
22
]
, the elephant has a third possibility:
with probability
r
it makes a step 0 (i.e., stays put), regardless of the
remembered step. (Now
p+q+r=1
; we assume
p,q,r>0
.)
This can be modelled by a 3-colour urn, with colours representing steps
+1
,

-1
, and
0
, and replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\zeta_{1}&\zeta_{2}&\zeta_{3}\\
\zeta_{2}&\zeta_{1}&\zeta_{3}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

(14.57)

where
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})
is a random vector with exactly one
component 1 and the others 0, and
(P[\zeta_{i}=1])_{i=1}^{3}=(p,q,r)
.
This Pólya urn is neither triangular nor irreducible, but it may be
regarded as a combination of two such urns (cf. Remark
2.3
)
as follows. Let as before
Y_{n}\in\{\pm 1,0\}
be the
n
th step, and let

Z_{n}:=|Y_{n}|\in\{0,1\}
;
Z_{n}
thus just records whether the elephant moves
or stays put.
(The process
Z_{n}
is called
Bernoulli elephant random walk
in

[
25
]
; it has also been studied in
[
8
]
and, for
somewhat different reasons, in
[
26
]
.)
As noted by
[
7
, V.C]
,
the process
(Z_{n})
can be modelled by a Pólya urn with

q=2
and one white ball for each step
\pm 1
and one black ball for each
step
0
so far; the replacement matrix is

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1-\zeta_{3}&\zeta_{3}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(14.58)

This is the urn in Example
14.5
with
\xi=1-\zeta_{3}\in\operatorname{Be}(1-r)
.
The number of non-zero steps up to time
n
is
W_{n}
, the number of white
balls in the urn.
By conditioning on
Z_{1}:=|Y_{1}|
, we
may assume that
Z_{1}
is deterministic. Moreover, the case
Z_{1}=0
is trivial,
with
Y_{n}=Z_{n}=0
for all
n\geqslant 1
; hence we may assume
Z_{1}=1
,
and thus the urn starts with 1 white ball.
(
[
22
;
24
]
use a different initial condition with

Z_{1}\sim\operatorname{Be}(1-r)
.)
Then the urn is the same as in Example
14.6
, and Theorem
1.8
yields

\displaystyle W_{n}/n^{1-r}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},

(14.59)

as shown by other methods in
[
22
, Theorem 3.1]
;
furthermore,

[
8
, Lemma 2.1]
and

[
24
, Theorem 5.1]

show that
(
14.43
) holds (with
p
replaced by
1-r
and
r
by
s
, say),
and thus
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
has a Mittag-Leffler distribution
(as we saw in Example
14.6
).
Moreover,
[
8
]
and
[
25
]

show that moment convergence holds in (
14.59
);
this also follows from Theorem
12.5
.

Conditioned on the number
W_{n}
, the position of the
elephant is the same as for a standard ERW with
W_{n}
steps,
and thus the limit results in
[
8
]
and
[
24
]
for the ERW
with delays
may easily be obtained by combining (
14.59
) and
the results for the standard ERW obtained by
[
7
]

from the urn (
14.56
); we leave the details to the reader.

\triangle

Example 14.11
.

The triangular urn with
q=3
and balanced deterministic replacements
(with all entries integers
\geqslant 0
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta&\sigma-\alpha-\beta\\
0&\delta&\sigma-\delta\\
0&0&\sigma\end{pmatrix}

(14.60)

was studied by
Puyhaubert 2005
and

Flajolet, Dumas and Puyhaubert 2006
; the results include convergence in distribution
(after normalization),
and, in some cases,
convergence of all moments with explicit
formulas for moments of the limits.

In particular, they show
(in our notation and correcting several typos)
that
if
\alpha>\delta>0
and
\beta>0
, then

\displaystyle X_{n2}/n^{\alpha/\sigma}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

(14.61)

where the limit has moments

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}^{r}=\left(\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha-\delta}\right)^{r}\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha+r)\Gamma(|\mathbf{x}|/\sigma)}{\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha)\Gamma((|\mathbf{x}|+r\alpha)/\sigma)}.

(14.62)

In this case, we have
\sigma\geqslant\alpha+\beta>\alpha>\delta
and thus

\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\lambda_{1}=\alpha
,
\lambda_{2}=\delta
,
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda^{*}_{3}=\lambda_{3}=\sigma
,

\widehat{\kappa}=\kappa_{i}=0
,
\gamma_{i}=0
(
i=1,2,3
).
Hence, Theorem
1.8
yields (
14.61
) with the stronger convergence a.s.
Moreover,
the leaders are 1 and 3,
and Lemma
7.3
yields
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\widehat{c}_{21}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}
, where Lemma
7.3
and (
6.18
)
show that
(1,\widehat{c}_{21})
is a left eigenvector
of
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
0&\delta\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
with eigenvalue
\alpha
.
Consequently,
\widehat{c}_{21}=\beta/(\alpha-\delta)
and

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\frac{\beta}{\alpha-\delta}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}.

(14.63)

Furthermore, as noted in
[
17
]
, we may in this urn
be partially colour-blind and merge colours 2 and 3; then

(X_{n1},X_{n2}+X_{n3})
is a 2-colour urn with replacement matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&\sigma-\alpha\\
0&\sigma\end{smallmatrix}\right)
; hence the moments
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{r}
are given
by (
14.30
), where now
\alpha
and
\delta
are replaced by
\sigma
and
\alpha
,
and
x_{1}+x_{2}
is replaced by
|\mathbf{x}|=x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}^{r}=\alpha^{r}\frac{\Gamma\bigl(|\mathbf{x}|/\sigma\bigr)\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha+r)}{\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha)\Gamma\bigl((|\mathbf{x}|+r\alpha)/\sigma\bigr)},\qquad r\geqslant 0.

(14.64)

Thus, (
14.62
) follows by (
14.63
).

Similarly, in the case
\alpha=\delta>0
and
\beta>0
,

[
43
]
and
[
17
]
show
(again correcting several typos)

\displaystyle X_{n2}/(n^{\alpha/\sigma}\log n)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}

(14.65)

where the limit has moments

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}^{r}=\left(\frac{\alpha\beta}{\sigma}\right)^{r}\frac{\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha+r)\Gamma(|\mathbf{x}|/\sigma)}{\Gamma(x_{1}/\alpha)\Gamma((|\mathbf{x}|+r\alpha)/\sigma)}.

(14.66)

In this case

\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\alpha
,
\kappa_{1}=0
and

\kappa_{2}=1
, and as above
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda^{*}_{3}=\lambda_{3}=\sigma

and
\widehat{\kappa}=\kappa_{3}=0
.
Hence,
Theorem
1.8
yields (
14.65
) with convergence a.s.
Moreover, Lemma
7.3
and (
7.3
) yield

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=\frac{\beta}{\sigma}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1},

(14.67)

which yields (
14.66
) by (
14.64
).

[
17
]
and
[
43
]
further prove moment convergence
in (
14.61
) and (
14.65
), which also follows from Theorem
12.5
.

Note that
[
17
]
and
[
43
]

assume the replacements to
be integers, while we obtain the results above also for non-integer
replacements.

A.s. convergence in (
14.61
) and (
14.65
)
follows also from

[
12
]
, see Example
14.12
.

\triangle

Example 14.12
.

Bose, Dasgupta, and Maulik 2009
study a rather general class of balanced
triangular urns
with deterministic
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{i}
; thus
\xi_{ij}=r_{ij}
.
They show a.s. convergence of
X_{ni}
suitably normalized, as in our
Theorem
1.8
.

We introduce some of the notation from
[
12
]
.
The colours are
\{1,\dots,q\}
(where they write
q=K+1
),
and the replacement matrix is triangular,
so (
2.4
)–(
2.6
) hold with the natural order
<
.
The diagonal entries
r_{ii}=\lambda_{ii}
are denoted
r_{i}
, and the positions
of the
weak maxima in the sequence
r_{1},\dots,r_{q}
are denoted
i_{1},\dots,i_{J+1}
.
Thus
r_{i_{1}}\leqslant r_{i_{2}}\leqslant\dots\leqslant r_{i_{J+1}}
, and
r_{k}<r_{i_{j}}
when

i_{j}<k<i_{j+1}
.
Since the urn is balanced,
Lemma
10.7
and Remark
10.8
show that

r_{q}=\lambda_{q}=\widehat{\lambda}
is
a maximum, and thus
i_{J+1}=q
. Clearly,
i_{1}=1
.
The
j
th block
of colours is
\{i_{j},\dots,i_{j+1}-1\}
.
(In
[
12
]
, the replacements are normalized by
\lambda_{q}=\widehat{\lambda}=1
, which can
be assumed without loss of generality. It is also assumed that initially there is 1 ball in the urn, i.e.,

\sum_{i}x_{i}=1
; this seems to be a mistake since one cannot in general
normalize both to 1 simultaneously.)

[
12
]
says that the colours are arranged in
increasing order
if
for every
k\in(i_{j},i_{j+1})
(with
1\leqslant j\leqslant J
), there exists
m\in[i_{j},k)

such that
r_{mk}>0
. Using our terminology, this is easily seen to be
equivalent to: If
k\in(i_{j},i_{j+1})
, then
k
is a descendant of
i_{j}
.

[
12
, Proposition 2.1]
shows that in every balanced triangular urn,
the colours can be rearranged in increasing order.
[Sketch of proof:
Construct the blocks in backwards order. In each step find a colour
i
(to be
labelled
i_{j}
) with
\lambda_{i}
maximal among the remaining colours; let the
next block consist of
i
and all its remaining descendants.
Order this block in a suitable way, with
i
first, and place it before the
previously constructed blocks. Repeat with the remaining colours.]

The main result of
[
12
]

further assumes
[
12
, (2.2)]
, which says
that for every
j=1,\dots,J
, there
exists
m\in[i_{j},i_{j+1})
such that
r_{m,i_{j+1}}>0
. In our terminology,
and assuming (as in
[
12
]
) that the colours are in natural order,
this is equivalent to
i_{j+1}\succ i_{j}
.
It follows that the assumptions in
[
12
]

(i.e., increasing order and their (2.2)) imply that

i_{1},\dots,i_{J+1}
are precisely
the colours
i
with
\lambda^{*}_{i}=\lambda_{i}
, i.e.
our leaders and subleaders
(see (
6.3
) and (
6.19
)),
and that the leaders
\nu
have distinct
\lambda^{*}_{\nu}
.
Moreover,
for each leader
\nu=i_{j}
, the subleaders in
\mathsf{D}_{\nu}

(see (
6.13
))
are
i_{j+1},\dots,i_{j+\ell}
where

\ell=\ell_{j}\geqslant 0
is the largest integer with
\lambda_{i_{j+\ell}}=\lambda_{i_{j}}
;
these subleaders form a chain in the partial order
\prec
, and thus

\kappa_{i_{j+k}}=k
for
k=0,\dots,\ell
.
The set
D_{\nu}^{\kappa}
is an interval
[i_{j},i_{j+1})
from a (sub)leader
to the next.

The a.s. convergence in the main result
[
12
, Theorem 3.1]
now
follows from Theorem
1.8
, Lemma
6.7
, and Theorem
7.1
.
Moreover,
[
12
, Remark 3.3]
says that it is clear from their proof
that
a.s. convergence holds
also without their assumptions of increasing order and their (2.2);

[
12
]

thus essentially states our Theorem
1.8
for the case of a balanced urn with
deterministic replacements.

Furthermore,

[
12
]
also shows convergence in
L^{2}
, which is extended to
L^{p}

for any
p
by our Theorem
12.5
.

\triangle

Example 14.13
.

Consider an urn with
q=4
and replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\alpha\eta_{1}-1&0&\eta_{2}&0\\
0&\beta\eta_{1}-1&\eta_{2}&0\\
0&0&\gamma&0\\
0&0&0&\delta\end{pmatrix},

(14.68)

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
are positive integers and
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\operatorname{Be}(1/2)

are independent.
We have
\lambda_{1}=\alpha/2-1
,
\lambda_{2}=\beta/2-1
,
\lambda_{3}=\gamma
, and
\lambda_{4}=\delta
.
Suppose that
\lambda_{4}=\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}>0
, and start with
\mathbf{X}_{0}=(1,1,0,1)
.

This urn satisfies
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and

(A6)
–
(A7)
, but not
(A8)
.
We have
\widehat{\lambda}=\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{3}=\lambda^{*}_{4}=\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda^{*}_{2}=\lambda_{2}
, and
\kappa_{i}=0

\forall i
.
Theorem
8.6
shows that (
4.1
) holds for all colours
i
,
with

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda^{*}_{i}t}X_{i}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{i}.

(14.69)

Furthermore,

{\mathcal{X}}_{4}>0
a.s., as is seen
by considering only balls of colour 4;
hence, by (
5.17
),

{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}({\mathcal{X}}_{1}+{\mathcal{X}}_{4})\geqslant\widehat{\lambda}^{-1}{\mathcal{X}}_{4}>0
a.s.

Furthermore, 3 is a follower of the leader 1, and
Lemma
6.1
with Remark
8.8
yields

{\mathcal{X}}_{3}=c{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
for some
c>0
. (In fact,
c=1/2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{3})
, by the same
argument as for (
14.13
).)
Note also that
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
are independent.

It is obvious that colours 1 and 2 both may die out in a few draws, and that
if they do, they may or may not first generate a ball of colour 3.
If they do not die out, then a.s.
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}>0
and
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}>0
respectively,
see Remark
8.10
.

Consequently, the following cases can appear, all with positive
probabilities:

(i)

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}>0
and then
{\mathcal{X}}_{3}>0
and thus

X_{3}(t)
grows at rate
e^{\lambda^{*}_{3}t}=e^{\lambda_{1}t}
.
Similarly,
by Theorem
8.5
and (
5.12
),
X_{n3}/n\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{3}>0
.

(ii)

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=0
but
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}>0
; then
{\mathcal{X}}_{3}=0
, but by considering the urn with
colour 2, 3, and 4 only (after 1 has died out), it follows that

e^{-\lambda_{2}}X_{3}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{3}^{\prime}:=c^{\prime}{\mathcal{X}}_{2}>0
for some
c^{\prime}>0
, and thus

X_{3}(t)
grows at rate
e^{\lambda_{2}t}
;
similarly

X_{n3}/n^{\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{3}^{\prime}>0
.

(iii)

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{2}=0
and both
X_{1}(t)
and
X_{2}(t)
die out,
but at least one of them first gets a ball of colour 3 as offspring.
Then, by considering only balls of colour 3 and 4 (when the others have died
out),

e^{-\lambda_{3}}X_{3}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{X}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}>0
, and thus

X_{3}(t)
grows at rate
e^{\lambda_{3}t}
;
similarly

X_{n3}/n^{\lambda_{3}/\lambda_{1}}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}>0
.

(iv)

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}={\mathcal{X}}_{2}=0
, and both
X_{1}(t)
and
X_{2}(t)
die out
without producing an offspring of colour 3.
Then,
X_{3}(t)=0
for all
t
and thus
X_{3n}=0
for all
n
.

This example shows that in a case when
{\mathcal{X}}_{i}=0
with positive probability,
it may still be possible to find precise limit results, but different
limit results may hold in different subcases.
It seems that this can be done very generally on a case by case basis,
but as said in Remark
8.7
we do not attempt any general statement.

\triangle

Example 14.14
.

An interesting counterexample is given by
the replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\phantom{+}1\\
0&\pm 1\end{pmatrix},

(14.70)

where
\xi_{22}=\pm 1
denotes a random variable with

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{22}=1)=\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\xi_{22}=-1)=\frac{1}{2}
.
In words: when we draw a white ball, it is replaced together with a black ball;
when we draw a black ball, we toss a coin and then (with probability

\frac{1}{2}
each) either remove the ball or replace it together with another
black ball.

We have
r_{22}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{22}=0
, and thus
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0=\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=\widehat{\lambda}
,

\kappa_{1}=0
,
\kappa_{2}=1
,
\widehat{\kappa}=1
,
\widehat{\kappa}_{0}=2
.
Note that this example is excluded from Theorems
8.4
–
8.6
since

(A7)
does not hold.
(However,

(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and
(A6)
hold, provided
x_{1}>0
and

x_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.)
We will see that, in fact, we do
not
have a.s. convergence
of
B_{n}/n^{1/2}=X_{n2}/n^{1/2}
and
B(t)/t=X_{2}(t)/t
as (
1.4
) and
(
4.1
) would give; however, these converge in distribution.

Consider first a continuous time urn with only black balls.
This is a branching process where balls live an
\operatorname{Exp}(1)
time and then
randomly either die or are split into two.
Let
Y(t)
denote such an urn with black balls, starting with
Y(0)=1
,
and denote its probability generating function by (for
|z|\leqslant 1
, say)

\displaystyle g_{t}(z):=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}z^{Y(t)}.

(14.71)

Then the Kolmogorov backward equation

[
32
, Theorem 12.22]

yields, for
t>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}g_{t}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(1+g_{t}(z)^{2}\bigr)-g_{t}(z)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(1-g_{t}(z)\bigr)^{2}

(14.72)

with
g_{0}(z)=z
, which has the solution

\displaystyle g_{t}(z)=1-\Bigl(\frac{1}{1-z}+\frac{t}{2}\Bigr)^{-1}=\frac{t(1-z)+2z}{t(1-z)+2}=\frac{t}{t+2}+\frac{2}{t+2}\cdot\frac{\frac{2}{t+2}z}{1-\frac{t}{t+2}z}.

(14.73)

Consequently,
Y(t)
has a modified geometric distribution:

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(Y(t)=0)=g_{t}(0)=\frac{t}{t+2}
and the conditional distribution

\bigl(Y(t)\mid Y(t)>0\bigr)
is
\operatorname{Ge}\bigl(\frac{2}{t+2}\bigr)
.
In particular,
\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(Y(t)=0)\to 1
as
{t\to\infty}
; since 0 is an absorbing state, it
follows that a.s.
Y(t)=0
for sufficiently large
t
; in other words,

Y(t)
dies out. (This follows
also since
Y(t)
is a time-changed simple random walk, absorbed at 0.)
A simple calculation yields
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}Y(t)=1
and
\operatorname{Var}Y(t)=t
, in accordance
with (
8.4
)–(
8.5
) and (
8.11
).
Note that
\bigl(t^{-1}Y(t)\mid Y(t)>0\bigr)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Exp}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigr)
as
{t\to\infty}
.
Roughly speaking, for large
t
,
Y(t)
is non-zero with probability

\approx 2/t
, and if it is, it is of order
t
.

Now consider the two-colour urn above, and assume that we start with

\mathbf{X}_{0}=(1,0)
, i.e., 1 white ball.
The number of white balls is constant for this urn, and thus
W(t)=1
for
all
t
in the continuous-time urn.
This means that white balls are drawn according to a Poisson process
\Xi

with
constant rate 1. If the times they are drawn are
(T_{k})_{1}^{\infty}
, then we have

\displaystyle B(t)=\sum_{T_{k}\leqslant t}Y_{k}(t-T_{k}),

(14.74)

where
Y_{k}
are independent copies of the one-colour process
Y(t)
,
independent also of
(T_{k})_{1}^{\infty}
.
Consequently, for
z\in[0,1]
say,
we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\bigl(z^{B(t)}\mid\Xi\bigr)

\displaystyle=\prod_{T_{k}\leqslant t}g_{t-T_{k}}(z)=\exp\Bigl(\sum_{T_{k}\leqslant t}\log g_{t-T_{k}}(z)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(\int_{0}^{t}\log g_{t-u}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\Xi(u)\Bigr).

(14.75)

Hence, by a standard formula for Poisson processes

[
32
, Lemma 12.2]
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}{z^{B(t)}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\exp\Bigl(\int_{0}^{t}\log g_{t-u}(z)\,\mathrm{d}\Xi(u)\Bigr)=\exp\Bigl(\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(g_{s}(z)-1\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\frac{1}{1-z}+\frac{s}{2}\Bigr)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\Bigr)=\exp\Bigl(-2\Bigl(\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{1-z}+\frac{t}{2}\Bigr)-\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{1-z}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(1+\frac{t}{2}(1-z)\Bigr)^{-2}=\Bigl(\frac{\frac{2}{t+2}}{1-\frac{t}{t+2}z}\Bigr)^{2}.

(14.76)

Consequently,
B(t)
has a negative binomial distribution

\operatorname{NegBin}\bigl(2,\frac{2}{t+2}\bigr)
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}B(t)=t,

(14.77)

which also follows directly from (
14.74
).

It follows from (
14.14
) that
as
{t\to\infty}
,
for any
s>0
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}e^{-sB(t)/t}=\Bigl(1+\frac{t}{2}(1-e^{-s/t})\Bigr)^{-2}\to\Bigl(1+\frac{s}{2}\Bigr)^{-2}

(14.78)

and thus

B(t)/t
converges in distribution to a Gamma distribution:

\displaystyle t^{-1}B(t)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\Gamma(2,\tfrac{1}{2}),\qquad\text{as }{t\to\infty}.

(14.79)

However, we will see that
B(t)/t
does
not
converge a.s., which shows
that Theorem
8.6
does not extend to this example.

To see this, we extend (
14.79
) to
process convergence.
We claim that as
t\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle t^{-1}B(tx)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{B}(x):=\tfrac{1}{4}\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)\qquad\text{in $D[0,\infty)$},

(14.80)

where
\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)
denotes a squared 4-dimensional Bessel process

[
44
, Chapter XI]
. Recall that

\displaystyle\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)=|\mathcal{W}(x)|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{W}_{i}(x)^{2},

(14.81)

where
\mathcal{W}_{1}(x),\dots,\mathcal{W}_{4}(x)
are independent standard Brownian motions
(Wiener processes), and
\mathcal{W}(x):=\bigl(\mathcal{W}_{1}(x),\dots,\mathcal{W}_{4}(x)\bigr)
thus is a
4-dimensional Brownian motion. Hence,

\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)\sim\Gamma(2,x/2)
, in accordance with (
14.79
).
Furthermore, a.s.
\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)>0
for every
x>0
.

The proof of (
14.80
) is somewhat technical and is given in Appendix
C
,
where we also extend the result to other initial values
(w_{0},b_{0})
,
see Theorem
C.1
.

Suppose now that
B(t)/t\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{Z}}
for some random variable
{\mathcal{Z}}
; then

{\mathcal{Z}}\sim\Gamma(2,\frac{1}{2})
by (
14.79
).
Moreover, for every
r>0
, we would have

\displaystyle t^{-1}\bigl(B(rt)-rB(t)\bigr)=r\Bigl(\frac{B(rt)}{rt}-\frac{B(t)}{t}\Bigr)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}r({\mathcal{Z}}-{\mathcal{Z}})=0.

(14.82)

However, the process convergence in (
14.80
) implies finite dimension
convergence, and in particular

\displaystyle t^{-1}\bigl(B(rt)-rB(t)\bigr)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{1}{4}\bigl(\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(r)-r\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(1)\bigr).

(14.83)

Hence, (
14.82
) would imply

\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(r)=r\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(1)
a.s., for every
r>0
, which obviously is false
(even for a single
r\neq 1
).
This contradiction proves the claim that
B(t)/t
does not converge a.s.
Hence, the convergence in (
14.79
) holds
in distribution but not a.s.

We use (
14.80
) to derive corresponding results for the discrete-time urn.
Let, as above,

\widehat{T}_{n}
be the
n
th time that a ball is drawn, and
let
N(t)
be the total number of draws up to time
t
;
thus
N(\widehat{T}_{n})=n
.
Since all balls are drawn with intensity 1,

\displaystyle\widetilde{N}(t):=N(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(W(s)+B(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s=N(t)-t-\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s

(14.84)

is a local martingale with
\widetilde{N}(0)=0
, and
it follows as in the proof of Lemma
3.1

(now using (
14.77
)) that
\widetilde{N}(t)
is a martingale.
In particular
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{N}(t)=0
, and thus by (
14.84
) and (
14.77
)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t)=t+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\int_{0}^{t}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=t+\int_{0}^{t}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=t+t^{2}/2.

(14.85)

Furthermore, all jumps are
+1
and thus the quadratic variation is by
(
2.16
)

\displaystyle[\widetilde{N},\widetilde{N}]_{t}=\sum_{0<s\leqslant t}\Delta N(s)=N(t).

(14.86)

Consequently,
by Doob’s inequality (
2.18
) and (
14.85
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\widetilde{N}^{*}(t)^{2}\leqslant C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}[\widetilde{N},\widetilde{N}]_{t}=C\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}N(t)=Ct+Ct^{2}.

(14.87)

In particular,
\widetilde{N}^{*}(t)/t^{2}\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\longrightarrow}0
as
{t\to\infty}
,
which together with (
14.84
) implies that

\displaystyle N(tx)/t^{2}-\int_{0}^{x}t^{-1}B(ty)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=t^{-2}N(tx)-t^{-2}\int_{0}^{tx}B(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=t^{-2}\widetilde{N}(tx)+t^{-1}x\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\longrightarrow}0

(14.88)

in
D[0,\infty)
, and consequently (
14.80
) implies

\displaystyle N(tx)/t^{2}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{V}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\mathcal{B}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

(14.89)

in
D[0,\infty)
,
jointly with (
14.80
).
Note that
\mathcal{V}(x)
is a continuous stochastic process which strictly
increases from
\mathcal{V}(0)=0
to
\infty
.
Define
\tau
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\tau)=1;

(14.90)

thus
\tau
is random with
0<\tau<\infty
a.s.
It follows easily from (
14.89
) (we omit the details) that, jointly with
(
14.80
),

\displaystyle\widehat{T}_{n}/\sqrt{n}\overset{\mathrm{p}}{\longrightarrow}\tau

(14.91)

and as a consequence

\displaystyle B_{n}/\sqrt{n}=B(\widehat{T}_{n})/\sqrt{n}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{B}(\tau).

(14.92)

This proves convergence in distribution of
B_{n}/\sqrt{n}
.
The limit
\mathcal{B}(\tau)
is determined by (
14.80
), (
14.89
), and
(
14.90
); unfortunately we do not know any simpler description of the
limit distribution, and we leave
it as an open problem to find one.

For the discrete-time urn,
we thus have convergence in distribution of

B_{n}/n^{1/2}
.
(The exponent
1/2
equals
\kappa_{2}/\widehat{\kappa}_{0}
, just as in
Theorem
1.8
(ii)
although we cannot apply that theorem.)
However, we do not have convergence a.s. in (
14.92
).
In fact, if
B_{n}/n^{1/2}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
for some
\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}
, then
for every
x>0
, as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle\frac{B(xt)^{2}}{N(xt)\phantom{{}^{2}}}=\frac{B_{N(xt)}^{2}}{N(xt)}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{2},

(14.93)

while the joint convergence of (
14.80
) and (
14.89
) implies

\displaystyle\frac{B(xt)^{2}}{N(xt)\phantom{{}^{2}}}=\left(\frac{B(xt)}{t}\right)^{2}\cdot\frac{t^{2}}{N(xt)}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\frac{\mathcal{B}(x)^{2}}{\mathcal{V}(x)}

(14.94)

in
D(0,\infty)
, i.e., in
D[a,b]
for every
0<a<b<\infty
.
Consequently, (
14.93
) would imply that a.s.

\mathcal{B}(x)^{2}/\mathcal{V}(x)=\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{2}
for every
x>0
, and thus a.s.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\mathcal{B}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{V}(x)=\widehat{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{-2}\mathcal{B}(x)^{2},\qquad x>0.

(14.95)

But this impossible, for example because (
14.95
) would imply that

\mathcal{B}(x)
,
and thus
\mathrm{BESQ}^{4}(x)
,
is differentiable (and, moreover, linear). This contradiction shows
that
B_{n}/\sqrt{n}
does not converge a.s.

\triangle

Example 14.15
.

A counterexample somewhat similar to Example
14.14
is given by
the replacement matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\phantom{+}1\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(14.96)

In words: when we draw a white ball, it is replaced together with a black ball;
when we draw a black ball, we discard it.

We have
\lambda_{1}=0
and
\lambda_{2}=-1
, and thus
\lambda^{*}_{1}=\lambda^{*}_{2}=0
.
Note that this example too is excluded from Theorem
8.6
since

(A7)
does not hold.
(However, again
(A1)
–
(A4)
,
(A5′)
, and
(A6)
hold, provided

x_{1}>0
and
x_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
.)
We will see that, as in Example
14.14
,

B(t)
converges in distribution but
not
a.s.

Suppose that the urn starts with a single white ball, i.e.,
\mathbf{X}_{0}=(1,0)
.
Then, for the
continuous-time urn,
W(t)=1
for all
t
, and thus white balls are drawn
according to a Poisson process with constant rate 1.
At each draw in this Poisson process, we add a black ball. Black balls live
an exponential time with mean 1, and then disappear.
Consequently,
B(t)
, the number of black balls, is a birth-death
process where the birth rate is constant 1 and the death rate equals the
number of particles. (Thus
\mu_{k}=1
and
\lambda_{k}=k
in the standard notation.)

Let
T_{k}
be the time of the
k
th white draw, and
L_{k}
the life-length of the
black ball that then is added. Then the pairs
(T_{k},L_{k})
form a Poisson process
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
with rate
e^{-y}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y
. The number of black balls at
time
t
is

\displaystyle B(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{T_{k}\leqslant t\}}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{L_{k}>t-T_{k}\}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{(T_{k},L_{k})\in D_{t}\}}

(14.97)

where
D_{t}:=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0<x\leqslant t,\,y>t-x\}
.
Consequently,
B(t)
has a Poisson distribution
\operatorname{Po}(\mu(t))
,
where

\displaystyle\mu(t)=\int_{D_{t}}e^{-y}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{t-x}^{\infty}e^{-y}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{t}e^{x-t}\,\mathrm{d}x=1-e^{-t}.

(14.98)

As
{t\to\infty}
, we thus have convergence in distribution
B(t)\overset{\mathrm{d}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Po}(1)
.
Obviously, we cannot have convergence a.s., since
B(t)
jumps
+1
at every

T_{k}
, and
T_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
.

B_{n}
does not converge in distribution as
{n\to\infty}

for a simple parity reason: since
B_{n}

changes by
\pm 1
at each draw, we have
B_{n}\equiv n\pmod{2}
.
However,
B_{n}
is a
Markov chain (since
W_{n}
is constant),
it is irreducible,
and it is easily
seen that the expected time to return to 0 is finite;
hence the Markov chain
B_{n}
is positive recurrent.
The period is 2, and
and it follows that the two subsequences
B_{2n}
and
B_{2n+1}

converge in distribution to some limits, say

\widehat{B}_{\textrm{even}}
and
\widehat{B}_{\textrm{odd}}
.
The mixture (with equal weights) of
\widehat{B}_{\textrm{even}}
and
\widehat{B}_{\textrm{odd}}
has a stationary
distribution for the Markov chain, and it is easily found that the limit
distributions are given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\widehat{B}_{\textrm{even}}=k)

\displaystyle=\frac{k+1}{k!}e^{-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{k\text{ is even}\}},

(14.99)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{P}{}}(\widehat{B}_{\textrm{odd}}=k)

\displaystyle=\frac{k+1}{k!}e^{-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{\{k\text{ is odd}\}}.

(14.100)

These can be described as

\operatorname{Po}(1)
rounded up to nearest even or odd integer, respectively.

Obviously, we do not have convergence a.s., even for these subsequences.

\triangle

Example 14.16
.

Consider a diagonal urn with
q=2
and replacement matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\xi_{11}&0\\
0&\xi_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
as in
[
2
]
; cf. the
deterministic case in Example
14.2
.
Assume that
\xi_{11},\xi_{22}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
a.s.,
that
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{22}=1
, and that

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{22}^{2}<\infty

but
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{11}\log\xi_{11}=\infty
, so that
(A4)
does
not
hold.
(We may simply take
\xi_{22}=1
a.s.)
Then the stochastic processes

X_{1}(t)
and
X_{2}(t)
are independent Markov branching processes, and

[
4
, Theorem III.7.2]
shows that, as
{t\to\infty}
,

\displaystyle e^{-t}X_{1}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}0.

(14.101)

Hence, (
4.1
) holds with
{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=0
a.s., while Theorem
4.1
(applied to
colour 2 only), or
[
4
, Theorem III.7.2]
again, shows that
(
4.1
) holds also for
i=2
with
{\mathcal{X}}_{2}>0
a.s.
It follows that
X_{1}(t)/X_{2}(t)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}0
as
{t\to\infty}
, and thus

X_{n1}/X_{n2}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}0
as
{n\to\infty}
.
It follows easily that (
1.3
) holds, which in this case is

X_{ni}/n\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{\longrightarrow}\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{i}
, with
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}=0
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{2}=1
;
we thus have

{\mathcal{X}}_{1}=\widehat{{\mathcal{X}}}_{1}=0
, in contrast to Theorems
1.8
and
4.1
.

This shows that the main results in the present paper do not hold without
assuming at least
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}{}\xi_{ij}\log\xi_{ij}<\infty
.
We have for convenience assumed the stronger second moment condition
(A4)
,
but as said in Remark
2.1
, we conjecture that it can be weakened.

\triangle
